- **1.11** — ἔνθα "Now": the point of time at which the Odyssey begins, the end of the seventh year on Calypso's island (Merry on 1.11), though the opening ἔνθʼ ἄλλοι μὲν πάντες is "Then all the other Achaeans" at *Iliad* 1.22. Merry (on 1.16) reckons the ten years from the fall of Troy.
- **1.18–19** — οὐδʼ ἔνθα … καὶ μετὰ οἷσι φίλοισι: for Merry (on 1.19) ἔνθα answers ὅτε δὴ ἔτος ἦλθε (16), and καί after the negative is "or": "Not even then was he safe out of danger or among his friends", as the English takes it. Nitzsch made the words a parenthesis, with the main clause at θεοὶ δʼ ἐλέαιρον; Köchly, after Bekker, rejected 18–19, and critics of the same school struck out 23–26 (Merry on 1.19).
- **1.29** — ἀμύμονος Αἰγίσθοιο "noble Aegisthus": the epithet marks birth or beauty, with no necessary moral sense, "almost titular, like our epithet ‘gallant’" (Merry on 1.29).
- **1.34** — ὑπὲρ μόρον "against fate" (as *Iliad* 21.517): Merry (on 1.34) calls it a popular solution of the problem of divine power and human freedom, a fated share that misconduct may aggravate.
- **1.40** — τίσις Ἀτρεΐδαο, vengeance for the son of Atreus, Agamemnon; Merry (on 1.40) rejects taking Ὀρέσταο Ἀτρεΐδαο together, and notes the change from reported to direct speech, as at 1.276 and 1.374.
- **1.47** — Scipio Africanus quoted the line on the death of Tiberius Gracchus (Merry on 1.47).
- **1.48** — δαΐφρων "wise-hearted", from δαῆναι "learn", not "warlike" from δαΐς (Merry on 1.48, against Buttmann).
- **1.52–54** — Atlas: ὀλοόφρων "baleful", the word of those whose knowledge is feared, as Aeetes and Minos (Merry on 1.52); ἀμφὶς ἔχουσι, either "support at either side" or "keep asunder", earth below and heaven above, which Merry (on 1.53) finds simpler and the English follows. Merry quotes Hesiod (*Theogony* 517, 746) for Atlas holding up the wide sky.
- **1.62** — ὠδύσαο "Why are you so angry with him": a play on the name of Odysseus, "a man of wrath" (Merry on 1.62, comparing 19.275 and 19.407 ff.).
- **1.64** — ἕρκος ὀδόντων "the barrier of your teeth": the teeth themselves, which look like a fence when the lips part, rather than the lips (Merry on 1.64).
- **1.68** — γαιήοχος "the earth-holder": "earth-surrounding" (the older commentators), "earth-supporting", or "lord of earth" (Autenrieth), as Merry (on 1.68) reports.
- **1.70** — ὅου, which Curtius would write ὅο; Bekker's ἔσκε for ἐστί rests on slight authority (Merry on 1.70).
- **1.91** — ἀπειπέμεν "speak out", not "renounce" (Merry on 1.91, against Nitzsch), as μῦθον ἀπηλεγέως ἀποείπω "speak out my word … without regard for anyone" (1.373), and ἀποειπεῖν at *Iliad* 9.309.
- **1.92** — μῆλʼ ἁδινά "thronging sheep" (Merry's "thick-thronging"), where the Iliad's ἁδινός is "swarming" of bees and flies (2.87, 2.469); εἰλίποδας "shambling", of the rolling gait of oxen, against Buttmann's "heavy-treading"; ἕλικας "with curving horns", one of the two senses the ancient lexicon gives, which the Hymn to Hermes (192) supports (Merry on 1.92). The phrase keeps the Iliad's "shambling cattle with curving horns" (*Iliad* 9.466).
- **1.105** — Mentes, leader of the Taphians, island pirates off the Leucadian coast (Merry on 1.105). The phrase Ταφίων ἡγήτορι Μέντῃ answers Κικόνων ἡγήτορι Μέντῃ, the leader of the Cicones whose likeness Apollo takes (*Iliad* 17.73).
- **1.107** — πεσσοί "game pieces": Merry (on 1.107) quotes Athenaeus on the game as the suitors played it, each casting a piece at one set in the middle and called Penelope.
- **1.130** — ὑπὸ λῖτα πετάσσας "spreading a cloth beneath her" is best taken as a parenthesis, so that καλὸν δαιδάλεον goes with θρόνον; λῖτα is an accusative singular, from λίς, not Wolf's and Nitzsch's neuter plural (Merry on 1.130). The next line keeps the Iliad's "fine and intricately worked; and there was a footstool under her feet" (*Iliad* 18.390).
- **1.132** — κλισμός and θρόνος, both "chair": the θρόνος stood higher, a chair of state, and the κλισμός had a sloping back (Merry on 1.132).
- **1.140** — The line serves elsewhere in the Odyssey for what can be made ready quickly for an unexpected guest, and it was objected to here, where the banquet is about to be served; Merry (on 1.140) answers that Telemachus' separate provision for his guest marks his distance from the suitors.
- **1.152** — μολπή "song": Aristarchus held that in Homer the word means play, above all dancing, not song; Merry (on 1.152), after Lehrs, finds that this passage, with Phemius' lyre, leans to the wider sense. ἀναθήματα "accompaniments", not the later sense of ornaments or offerings to the gods (Merry on 1.152).
- **1.170** — τίς πόθεν: two questions blended into one (Merry on 1.170).
- **1.176** — ἴσαν "used to come", from εἶμι, against Nitzsch's "knew" (Merry on 1.176).
- **1.182** — ὧδε "as you see": Aristarchus held that ὧδε in Homer always means "so", never "here"; Buttmann disputed it (Merry on 1.182).
- **1.184** — Temese: Strabo places it in Bruttium (later Tempsa); Merry (on 1.184) prefers Tamassus in Cyprus, famous for its copper. χαλκός is "bronze", as everywhere.
- **1.185** — ἐπʼ ἀγροῦ "by the fields": whether the ship is drawn up on land or afloat (Merry on 1.185).
- **1.193** — γουνὸν ἀλωῆς οἰνοπέδοιο "the slope of his vineyard": γουνός from γόνυ, a knoll or rising ground (Merry on 1.193).
- **1.216** — The scholia set beside the line sayings of Euripides and Menander on a man's not knowing his father (Merry on 1.216).
- **1.226** — εἰλαπίνη ἠὲ γάμος: by Herodian's rule the second member of a double question takes the circumflex, ἦε (Merry on 1.175, and on 1.226); the text prints ἠὲ, as the digitized Murray does. εἰλαπίνη "banquet", given by one host; ἔρανος "shared feast", held at each house in turn, as the suitors feast at 1.375 (Merry on 1.226).
- **1.227** — ὥς τε: comparative, "like men committing outrage", as Merry (on 1.227) prefers; others take it as final or exclamatory.
- **1.241** — ἀκλειῶς "without report": Merry (on 1.241) holds that κλέος in Homer means "report", "tidings", so that no news of Odysseus has come (ἄιστος ἄπυστος, 1.242); the Iliad's ἀκλειῶς is "without glory" (22.304). ἅρπυιαι, "the snatchers", the storm-winds personified (Merry on 1.241); compare the Harpy Podarge (*Iliad* 16.150).
- **1.259** — Ephyra, the Thesprotian or the Elean (Merry on 1.259); Ilus son of Mermerus is not the Ilus of Troy.
- **1.263** — νεμεσίζετο takes an accusative only here, "feared the indignation of"; poisoned arrows appear nowhere in the Iliad, and the Odyssey alludes to them as impious (Merry on 1.263).
- **1.267** — θεῶν ἐν γούνασι κεῖται: Merry (on 1.267) rejects the suppliant's and the votive explanations and suggests the gods as umpires, with the prizes on their knees.
- **1.275–278** — The anacoluthon of μητέρα δʼ … ἂψ ἴτω (Merry on 1.275). Whether οἱ δέ (277) are the bride's family (Nitzsch, after Eustathius) or, as Merry finds simpler, the suitors; ἔεδνα "bride-price", gifts to the bride's family, which are not a dowry before Pindar (Merry on 1.277).
- **1.276** — Icarius: Aristotle (*Poetics* 25, 1461b4–9) reports the Cephallenians' story that Odysseus married among them and that the man was Icadius, not Icarius (Merry on 1.276).
- **1.293–302** — Nauck marks the lines spurious, and so Hentze–Ameis, as inconsistent with the advice of 1.274–278 (Merry on 1.293).
- **1.318** — ἑλών "taking out", from the store-room, not "choosing" (Merry on 1.318).
- **1.320** — ἀνόπαια: a bird's name, "unseen", "through the smoke-vent", or "upward", which Merry (on 1.320) prefers, after Eustathius and Empedocles.
- **1.327** — The grim homecoming: the anger of Pallas for the outrage of Ajax, son of Oileus, to Cassandra in her temple; at 4.499 his death is laid to his insolence against Poseidon (Merry on 1.326).
- **1.344** — καθʼ Ἑλλάδα καὶ μέσον Ἄργος "through Hellas and the heart of Argos": a phrase for the whole of Greece, north and south (Merry on 1.344).
- **1.349** — ἀλφησταί "toiling": "enterprising, gain-getting" men, or "bread-eating" ones, from ἄλφιτον (Merry on 1.347).
- **1.351–352** — Plato (*Republic* 424b) quotes the lines with ἐπιφρονέουσιν and ἀειδόντεσσι (Merry on 1.351).
- **1.356–359** — Aristarchus rejected the lines; Merry (on 1.360) doubts the rejection, since something in Telemachus' words must account for Penelope's amazement. They are Hector's words to Andromache (*Iliad* 6.490–493), with μῦθος "speech" for πόλεμος "war" and a new close to 359.
- **1.374–375** — Kirchhoff took the lines for an awkward imitation of 2.139 (Merry on 1.374).
- **1.377, 1.380** — νήποινον "without payment"; νήποινοι "unavenged": once warned, the suitors would die in his house with no compensation owed for them (Merry on 1.377).
- **1.391** — ἦ φῂς: Nitzsch preferred ἢ "or" (Merry on 1.391).
- **1.402** — δώμασιν οἷσιν: whether the possessive ὅς may refer to the second person, or σοῖσιν should be read (Merry on 1.402).
- **1.431** — ἐεικοσάβοια "the worth of twenty oxen", a high price for a slave, where a skilled woman is valued at four oxen at *Iliad* 23.705 (Merry on 1.431).
- **1.440** — τρητοῖσι λέχεσσι "the corded bed", as *Iliad* 3.448; Merry (on 1.440) takes τρητός as "morticed", of the frame, citing Plato (*Statesman* 279e), against the holes for cords.
- **1.441–442** — How the door is fastened: a bolt drawn home by a strap through a slit in the door, and a handle (κορώνη) on the outside (Merry on 1.441).
- **2.1** — ῥοδοδάκτυλος "rosy-fingered": Merry (on 2.1) sees perhaps the spreading rays of rosy light before sunrise, like the fingers of an open hand, and quotes Eustathius' allegory of the sun's rays as Dawn's fingers.
- **2.3** — εἵματα ἑσσάμενος: it was the custom to sit up and put on at least part of one's clothing before leaving the bed (Merry on 2.3, comparing *Iliad* 2.42, 10.21).
- **2.5** — ἄντην "to look upon": "in presence", literally "if looked at in the face" (Merry on 2.5, comparing 4.310).
- **2.7, 2.26** — ἀγορή and θῶκος: the council of kings and chiefs was the βουλή or θῶκος, and the ἀγορή the gathering of the people, who assented or dissented (Merry on 2.7, comparing *Iliad* 2.53 and 2.93, and reporting Aristotle's account of the Cretan constitution in the *Politics*). θόωκος is "session", the council of elders (Merry on 2.26, comparing 3.127), kept apart in the English from βουλή "council".
- **2.9** — The line is no tautology: the first clause is the people gathering, the second the assembly fully formed (Merry on 2.9).
- **2.11** — δύω κύνες ἀργοί "two swift dogs": Merry (on 2.11) defends the reading, notes the variant κύνες πόδας ἀργοί, and takes ἀργός of swiftness, not of color, against the older "white" (Eustathius' λευκόποδες).
- **2.13** — θηεῦντο, from a present θηέομαι beside the commoner θεάομαι (Merry on 2.13).
- **2.14** — The elders sit on seats of stone in the place of assembly, and Telemachus, as the king's son, takes his father's seat while they make way (Merry on 2.14, comparing 17.61–63, 20.144–146).
- **2.17** — καὶ γάρ more probably gives the reason why Aegyptius speaks first, his longing for his lost son, than an explanation of his age (Merry on 2.17).
- **2.20** — πύματον: Antiphus was the last of the six companions of Odysseus whom the Cyclops ate (Merry on 2.20, comparing 9.369, and rejecting Eustathius' "last of all men"); the stress may fall on ὡπλίσσατο, since once blinded the Cyclops could no longer wait on himself. ὡπλίσσατο δόρπον is the Cyclops' own formula (9.291, 9.344).
- **2.22** — ἔργα "fields", the farms (Merry on 2.22: *opera rustica*).
- **2.30, 2.42** — στρατοῦ "of the army": the scholia give two senses, an enemy's army or the army that went to Ilios; Ameis takes the second, since Aegyptius longs for his son, but Merry (on 2.30) thinks an invasion or a raid of pirates the likelier cause for calling an assembly in haste. The English keeps the ambiguity. The optative εἴποι (2.31) becomes the subjunctive εἴπω when Telemachus repeats the words (2.43), marking that he would tell such news if he had it (Merry on 2.31).
- **2.33** — ὀνήμενος "may he prosper!": the older commentators supplied εἴη ("may he be blessed"), an ellipse Merry (on 2.33) calls unparalleled in Homer; he prefers Classen's explanation, which takes the participle from a formula of blessing, as οὐλόμενος comes from the curse ὄλοιο.
- **2.34** — The digitized text prints ὅτι; the sense is the relative ὅ τι "whatever", as this text prints it at 5.89 and 8.577 and the Iliad at 14.221, and the English takes it so.
- **2.35** — φήμη "word of omen": a chance utterance whose bearing the speaker does not know; Aegyptius blesses Telemachus unawares, before he has said that he called the assembly (Merry on 2.35, comparing κλεηδών, 18.117, 20.120).
- **2.37** — The scepter was the badge of public office, carried by kings, priests, prophets, heralds and judges; the herald who puts it in a speaker's hand invests him with that office for the time (Merry on 2.37).
- **2.39** — καθαπτόμενος "approaching … with words": earnest or animated address, not necessarily rough, as of an urgent but submissive appeal (10.70) or of an angry retort (18.415; Merry on 2.39, comparing 2.240).
- **2.40** — οὗτος ἀνήρ "that man": not a periphrasis for "I", but "this man (about whom you ask) is not far off", until he adds that it is he (Merry on 2.40).
- **2.45** — With Aristophanes' κακά, printed here, ὅ is "in that": "two evils have fallen upon my house"; with Aristarchus' κακόν, δοιά is "in two ways" or answers the "first" and "far greater" evils of 2.46–48 (Merry on 2.45). The English follows the text printed.
- **2.46, 2.48** — τοίσδεσσιν "here", with a gesture of the hand (Merry on 2.46); νῦν δʼ αὖ καὶ πολὺ μεῖζον understands κακὸν ἔμπεσε, where the balance wants τὸ δέ after τὸ μέν (Merry on 2.48).
- **2.51–52** — The scholia ask why only the suitors of Ithaca, "the men who are the best here", are blamed; and they take εἰς οἶκον to mean that Icarius lived in Ithaca (Merry on 2.51, 2.52, who also cites the legend that made Icarius king of Cephallenia, *Poetics* 25: see the note on 1.276).
- **2.53** — ἐεδνώσαιτο "settle the bride-price": not "to dower" his daughter, a later usage, but to accept gifts of wooing for her, or "to make terms about the marriage" (Merry on 2.53, after Butcher and Lang and Cobet; see the note on 1.275–278).
- **2.60** — ἦ καὶ ἔπειτα "truly, even then": ἔπειτα, as distinguished from ὀπίσσω "hereafter", points to the immediate future: if we do (καί) try, we shall prove weaklings (Merry on 2.60, comparing 1.65 and 2.273, 2.280, where the English has "then" as well).
- **2.67** — μεταστρέψωσιν "turn upon you": "lest they make some change, in wrath at evil deeds", the gods turning from indifference to interference, as μεταστρέψεις at *Iliad* 15.203 (Merry on 2.67); Fäsi joins μεταστρέψωσιν κακὰ ἔργα, "bring the evil deeds down on the guilty", comparing μετάτροπα ἔργα in Hesiod (*Theogony* 89, as Merry reports him).
- **2.70** — σχέσθε, φίλοι "hold back, my friends", "let be": with φίλοι Telemachus addresses not the suitors but the Ithacans (Merry on 2.70).
- **2.86** — ἐθέλοις δέ κε: many manuscripts have ἐθέλεις, and Bekker reads ἐθέλεις δʼ ἐκ μῶμον ἀνάψαι (Merry's critical note and on 2.86).
- **2.89** — τάχα δʼ εἶσι τέταρτον "and soon the fourth will pass": 2.106–107 and 13.377 decide the sense (Merry on 2.89). ἐστίν is "is completed", as at *Iliad* 2.295, so that three years are past and the fourth is "fast passing", as the scholium explains εἶσι (διελεύσεται); Nitzsch took it as "is coming".
- **2.92** — ἀγγελίας "messages", as ἀγγελιάων (2.255); Ameis takes it as "messengers", from a supposed ἀγγελίης (Merry on 2.92).
- **2.98** — μεταμώνια "wasted": a variant μεταμώλια, which Eustathius calls Attic; Apion derived the word from going "with the winds", which Merry calls very doubtful (Merry's critical note and on 2.98).
- **2.100** — τανηλεγέος θανάτοιο "of death that lays men low": commonly "the outstretcher", of the body laid out for burial, though from the root of λέγω, "lay", the form would be τανηλεχής (Düntzer, in Merry on 2.100).
- **2.102** — κεῖται, the manuscripts' reading, which Buttmann defends on the ground that the verb has no distinct subjunctive form; Merry's text prints Wolf's conjecture κῆται (Merry's critical note).
- **2.105** — ἐπεὶ … παραθεῖτο, as Bekker reads with Eustathius; nearly all manuscripts have ἐπήν, which Merry keeps, an optative of recurring action, comparing *Iliad* 24.227 (Merry's critical note and on 2.105).
- **2.107** — The same verse at 19.152 and 24.142 is followed by μηνῶν φθινόντων, περὶ δʼ ἤματα πόλλʼ ἐτελέσθη, which this passage lacks.
- **2.116** — ὅ οἱ is the Harleian manuscript's reading, ὅ with the force of "that", as at *Iliad* 9.493; with the plural ἅ she ponders the gifts that Athena has given her (Merry on 2.116).
- **2.126** — ποθήν: two manuscripts have ποθή, with ἐστί understood; ποιεῖτʼ is ποιεῖται, "she is making fame for herself; but for thee the loss of much substance" (Merry on 2.126).
- **2.131–132** — ζώει ὅ γʼ ἦ τέθνηκε "alive or dead": "as to my father, he is away elsewhere in the world, whether he be alive or dead", a doubt put as an indirect question, with the circumflex on the second member (Merry on 2.131).
- **2.133** — ἑκών "of my own will": the manuscripts are divided between ἑκών and ἐγών, with the greater number for ἑκών; Bekker adopts ἐγών (Merry's critical note).
- **2.141** — ὑμῖν here, ὕμιν in the identical verse at 1.376, as the digitized text prints them.
- **2.151** — πυκνά "thick" is La Roche's reading, with eleven manuscripts, for the common πολλά (Merry's critical note).
- **2.154** — αὐτῶν "of the people": Aristophanes read αὖτις or αὔτως (Merry's critical note).
- **2.167** — ἐυδείελος "clear-seen": the word recurs of Ithaca and islands (9.21, 13.212, 13.325, 19.132). The scholia give "lying well toward the evening", from δείελος "evening" (*Iliad* 21.232), the view of Buttmann and of Düntzer, "well-defined" and "conspicuous"; Merry (on 2.167) takes the last as right, if with Curtius εὐδείελος is another form of εὐδέελος, that is εὔδηλος, "clear to see".
- **2.172** — εἰσανέβαινον "were embarking", as ἀναβήμεναι "embarked" (1.210); the Iliad's Ἴλιον εἰσανέβησαν is "gone up into Ilios" (6.74).
- **2.190** — ἀνιηρέστερον: Ameis, with Bekker, reads ἀνιηρώτερον from a Vienna manuscript, needlessly; the Alexandrines called the form Attic (Merry's critical note).
- **2.191** — The verse is wanting in two Venetian manuscripts, among others, and Wolf rejected it as made up from *Iliad* 1.562 with a varying second half, some reading εἵνεκα τῶνδε and others οἷος ἀπʼ ἄλλων; neither Eustathius nor the scholia notice it (Merry's critical note). Merry (on 2.191) calls it spurious: εἵνεκα τῶνδε may mean "because of [the resistance of] these suitors", but τῶνδε is an odd way to say "us", and the verse spoils the contrast of αὐτῷ μέν οἱ (2.190) and σοὶ δέ (2.192). The English renders it "because of these men".
- **2.198** — παύσεσθαι is Wolf's conjecture for παύσασθαι or παύεσθαι (Merry's critical note).
- **2.209** — ἀγαυοί "noble": the scholia record a variant ἀγανοί (Merry's critical note).
- **2.222** — χεύω "heap up": Ptolemy son of Oroandes wrote χείω, and so did Aristarchus; Herodian read χεύω, a present for the future (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **2.227** — γέροντι: Eustathius and Merry take the old man as Laertes, "Odysseus entrusted all his house to Mentor, to obey his aged father, and keep all secure"; Nitzsch and others take him as Mentor, with the house the subject of πείθεσθαι and Mentor of φυλάσσειν (Merry on 2.227, citing Monro), which the English follows: the house is to obey the old man, and Mentor to keep all things safe.
- **2.236** — κακορραφίῃσι "in the mischief": the scholia record κακοφραδίῃσι (Merry's critical note).
- **2.245** — ἀνδράσι καὶ πλεόνεσσι goes with μαχήσασθαι, of the suitors who outnumber the people (as 2.251), or with ἀργαλέον, "hard even for men who are more numerous". Some wrote ἀνδράσι καὶ παύροισι, "even for a few" (the scholia); Merry (on 2.245) takes πλεόνεσσι with μαχήσασθαι, comparing 2.251 and 16.88, as the English does.
- **2.262** — ὃ χθιζὸς θεὸς ἤλυθες "you god who came yesterday": θεός, drawn from the vocative into the nominative of the relative clause, "thou that camest yesterday in thy godhead" (Merry on 2.262, who notes the vulgate κλῦθί μοι); Athena came in the likeness of Mentes, and Telemachus knew her for a god (1.323, 1.420).
- **2.270, 2.278** — οὐδʼ ὄπιθεν … οὐδʼ ἀνοήμων: "no coward and no fool" hereafter, or "not hereafter either", as he has not been until now: "as thou hast not been spiritless nor witless to-day, in the assembly, so neither hereafter wilt thou be" (Merry on 2.270).
- **2.311** — ἀκέοντα "in silence" is Rhianus' reading; the scholia record ἀέκοντα (Merry's critical note).
- **2.321** — σπάσατʼ is Aristarchus' reading; others read σπάσεν (Merry's critical note).
- **2.338** — ὅθι νητός "where … lay heaped": Aristophanes wrote ὁθιννητός, with two ν (Merry's critical note).
- **2.344** — ἔπεσαν is from ἔπειμι, "were on it", not from πίπτω: Merry renders "were fixed thereto", of folding doors, the frame perhaps made large to admit the chests (Merry on 2.344, comparing 23.194).
- **2.377** — γρῆυς, as the digitized text prints it here and at 7.8; γρηῢς in the same opening at 18.185, 19.361, 19.386, 19.503, 22.433. ἀπώμνυ is "swore not to".
- **2.383** — Τηλεμάχῳ ἐικυῖα, Bekker's reading with good manuscripts, for Τηλεμάχῳ δʼ ἐικυῖα (Merry's critical note).
- **2.385** — ἀγέρεσθαι is accented as a present infinitive, though Homer's middle forms of the verb are aorists (ἀγέροντο, *Iliad* 2.94, 18.245): the irregular accent of the aorist has the authority of the grammarians, who took the form as a present (Merry's critical note).
- **2.412** — δμωαί, without the iota subscript, as the digitized text prints it here and at 6.307, 7.103 (δμῳαί elsewhere).
- **2.421** — ἀκραῆ "fresh": a few manuscripts read εὐκραῆ (Merry's critical note).
- **2.434** — ἠῶ "through the dawn": time over which, or "and at dawn".
- **3.1** — λίμνην "water": the sea, here the farther margin of the Ocean stream, "water which washes a shore" (Merry on 3.1, who reports that Nitzsch refuted Voss's view that the Caspian is meant).
- **3.4** — Pylos: Strabo argued for the Triphylian Pylos; the scholia, Eustathius and Pindar (*Pythian* 6.35, "the Messenian old man") for the Messenian, which Merry prefers (on 3.4).
- **3.7** — The nine companies of five hundred answer to the nine cities of Nestor, and 4,500 men to his ninety ships at fifty a ship (Merry on 3.7, after the scholia and *Iliad* 2.719).
- **3.8** — προύχοντο, as the digitized text prints it, for προὔχοντο (Merry): "held before them", the bulls set out ready for the sacrifice (Merry on 3.8, comparing *Iliad* 17.355).
- **3.11** — The sail is furled by brailing it up to the yard, not lowered and stowed (Merry on 3.11, who reports the reading σεῖσαν ascribed to Zenodotus).
- **3.95** — πλαζομένου goes with μῦθον and refers to Odysseus, as the following γάρ shows: "word of him … as he wandered" (Merry on 3.95).
- **3.124–125** — ἐοικότες … ἐοικότα "like his", "so like him", not "fitting" (Nitzsch): Merry (on 3.124), from ὧδε and from 4.141–143 and 19.380–381.
- **3.146** — The verse is *Iliad* 20.466, where the one who would not be persuaded is Achilles; here it is Athena ("she").
- **3.171** — ἐπὶ Ψυρίης "by the island of Psyria", keeping Psyria itself on the left, the straight course from Lesbos to Geraestus (Merry on 3.169); the scholia, he reports, were unsure whether αὐτήν is Psyria or Chios.
- **3.199** — καὶ σὺ φίλος, without the comma after σύ that the same verse has at 1.301 (and the same opening at 3.313), as the digitized text prints it.
- **3.204** — καὶ ἐσσομένοισι πυθέσθαι is Wolf's reading from Eustathius; the manuscripts have καὶ ἐσσομένοισιν ἀοιδήν, "a song even for men to come" (Merry on 3.204).
- **3.244–245** — περὶ … οἶδε … ἄλλων "knows better than others", with δίκας "rules of right" (Merry on 3.244). ἀνάξασθαι is the only middle of ἀνάσσω in Homer: Merry (on 3.245) suspects the line, reports Nauck's conjecture ἀνασσέμεναι, and, if it is genuine, takes γένεʼ as duration, "has been king for three generations of men", the tradition of *Iliad* 1.250–252.
- **3.246** — ὥς τε "like", comparative (Merry on 3.246, referring to his note on 1.227), not the later consecutive ὥστε.
- **3.269** — μιν: Clytemnestra, "fettered" to yield, as the English takes it with Eustathius' preferred view; Merry (on 3.269) thinks Aegisthus, "fate-bound to his ruin", the simplest, and Nitzsch took it of the singer.
- **3.290** — τροφέοντο "swelled", as the digitized text prints it; Merry's text reads τροφόεντα "big", which would make the waves a second object of "poured" (Merry on 3.290).
- **3.303–305** — Murray prints 305 (ἑπτάετες δʼ ἤνασσε …) before 304 (κτείνας Ἀτρεΐδην …), and so does Merry's text, after Bergk and La Roche and the scholiast on Sophocles' *Electra* 267, who quotes "Aegisthus ruled over Mycenae rich in gold, having killed the son of Atreus" (Merry on 3.304). The rows of this text must run in the order of their numbers, so they keep the manuscripts' order, in which ταῦτα "throws its meaning forward" and κτείνας explains it (Merry): the full stop after λυγρά (303) becomes a comma, the one change made to the digitized text, and the English reads "devised these grim things at home, / killing the son of Atreus, …".
- **3.307** — ἀπʼ Ἀθηνάων "back from Athens": the tragic legend brings Orestes from Phocis, and Aeschylus sends him to Athens only after the deed (Merry on 3.307).
- **3.309–310** — The scholia say these lines were missing in some editions, and report Aristarchus' view that they hint that Clytemnestra died with Aegisthus, though not plainly by Orestes' hand (Merry on 3.309).
- **3.335** — The raised dot after ἔοικεν, as the digitized text prints it; the infinitives of 3.336 depend on ἔοικεν.
- **3.348** — ἦ, as the digitized text prints it; Merry (on 3.348) construes the line with ἢ, as 19.109 prints ὥς τέ τευ ἢ βασιλῆος.
- **3.372** — φήνη "sea eagle": named with vultures at 16.217, "commonly taken to be the sea-eagle", or the lammergeier (Merry on 3.372); after Seiler, he takes εἰδομένη of bodily likeness, so that the goddess is seen to change.
- **3.384, 3.437** — περιχεύας, περίχευεν "poured around": gold leaf spread round the horns, not molten metal, since the smith brings anvil, hammer and tongs (Merry on 3.384, 3.437).
- **3.400** — πὰρʼ δʼ, as the digitized text prints it, for πὰρ δʼ (3.267, 3.416, 3.482).
- **3.442** — No stop after κανέῳ in the digitized text, though a new clause begins with πέλεκυν δέ.
- **3.453** — ἀνελόντες has no object: they lift the victim's head to bare the throat (Merry on 3.453, comparing *Iliad* 1.459).
- **4.1** — κητώεσσαν "with its many ravines" (*Iliad* 2.581): Zenodotus read καιετάεσσαν (Merry's critical note, from the scholia and Eustathius). Merry (on 4.1) derives κητώεις from κῆτος, "gulf", hence "cavernous", and with Tozer takes it of the many rifts and fissures of the valley, citing Strabo on the καιετοί, the rifts left by earthquakes in Laconia.
- **4.3** — ἔται "kinsmen", as in the Iliad (6.239, 9.464, 16.456). The scholia explain the word as πολῖται "citizens", Apollonius as ἑταῖροι, and Nitzsch as near relations or dependents; Merry (on 4.3) argues from the Iliad's places that it names neither kinsmen nor ἑταῖροι but a wider bond that women shared, short of the whole people: "perhaps *aequales*, persons of the same generation".
- **4.3–19** — Athenaeus (5.180, as Merry reports him on 4.3–19 and 4.15–19) says that Diodorus the Aristophanean struck out the wedding, and that the singer and the tumblers (4.15–19) were brought in from the Shield (*Iliad* 18.604–606) by Aristarchus' school; a scholium calls the five lines Aristarchus', not Homer's (Merry's critical note). Merry answers that without 4.3–19 the passage from 4.2 to 4.20 is very awkward.
- **4.10** — Σπάρτηθεν "of Sparta" goes with Ἀλέκτορος, not with ἤγετο, for the wedding was at Sparta (Merry on 4.10).
- **4.12** — δούλης "a slave woman": some read Δούλης as a name (scholia, in Merry's critical note); she was not a slave born, Merry says, but a captive (on 4.12).
- **4.19** — μολπῆς ἐξάρχοντος, singular: the singer leads the song and dance (Merry on 4.19), where the Shield has the plural, of the tumblers (ἐξάρχοντες, *Iliad* 18.606).
- **4.57–58, 4.62–64** — Most editors since Wolf reject 4.57–58, which the Harleian and several other manuscripts lack; 4.62–64 were athetized, as already by Zenodotus and Aristophanes, for the monosyllable σφῷν, a contracted dual found only here (Merry's critical note, and on 4.62).
- **4.73** — ἤλεκτρον "amber": Pliny took it for electrum, gold alloyed with silver, and cited this verse; Merry (on 4.73) finds that tempting between gold and silver, but the necklaces strung with ἤλεκτρα (15.460, 18.296) require amber.
- **4.74** — αὐλή "court": Athenaeus (188F) quotes Seleucus' reading τοιαῦτα δόμοις …, without the court (Merry's critical note); if the word keeps its proper sense, Telemachus turns back from the splendours of the hall to the court (Merry on 4.74).
- **4.84** — Ἐρεμβούς is Aristarchus' reading; Crates read Ἐρεμνούς (Merry's critical note).
- **4.85** — ἄφαρ κεραοὶ τελέθουσι "are horned at once": Herodotus (4.29) quotes the verse, with ὅθι for ἵνα, as rightly said, since horns grow quickly in hot lands; Merry (on 4.85) takes τελέθω here as "be", πέλω.
- **4.89** — ἐπηετανόν "unfailing": the old explanation "lasting out the year" (ἐπί, ἔτος); Curtius, after Döderlein, derives it from ἐπί and αἰεί, "perpetual" (Merry on 4.89).
- **4.113** — ὑφʼ ἵμερον ὦρσε γόοιο "roused the longing for lament": Merry (on 4.113) says that Aristotle quotes "this line" (*Rhetoric* 1.11.12) for the pleasure there is in mourning, but the verse the *Rhetoric* quotes, τοῖσι δὲ πᾶσιν ὑφʼ ἵμερον ὦρσε γόοιο (1370b29), is 4.183, the same as *Iliad* 23.108.
- **4.119** — ἕκαστά τε πειρήσαιτο "put every point to the test": ἕκαστα is the object, "should test all he said" (Merry on 4.119), not "in every particular" with αὐτοῦ understood (Ameis).
- **4.122** — χρυσηλάκατος "of the golden distaff", as in the Iliad (16.183, 20.70), and here beside Helen's own golden distaff (4.131); Merry (on 4.122) thinks the Iliad's company of epithets requires "with golden arrows".
- **4.140** — ψεύσομαι, ἦ ἔτυμον ἐρέω; "Shall I be wrong, or speak the truth?", as *Iliad* 10.534: Merry (on 4.140) argues from the Iliad's verse that the choice is between speaking the truth and saying nothing, "Shall I withhold the truth?", against the common "Shall I be mistaken?" (Ameis).
- **4.151–152** — ἀμφʼ Ὀδυσῆι goes with μυθεόμην, "I was speaking about Odysseus", while μεμνημένος "remembering" stands by itself (Merry on 4.151).
- **4.158–167** — Rhianus' text lacked 4.158–160, and the scholia report the athetesis of five lines as superfluous and unfit for a young man to speak; others rejected 4.163 (Merry's critical note, who reports Ameis' view that the athetesis concerns 4.163–167).
- **4.192** — Aristarchus rejected the verse (scholia, in Merry's critical note), and Wolf and the modern editors except Fäsi follow him; Merry (on 4.192) keeps it, taking οἷσιν ἐνὶ μεγάροισι with Nestor's saying, "in his own halls".
- **4.194** — μεταδόρπιος "after supper": Telemachus came at sunset (3.497), so the meal is the evening δόρπον; Peisistratus does not like to end the day in weeping, and Menelaus takes up the meal again (4.213) (Merry on 4.194, with Lehrs, for whom the word can mean nothing else). Nitzsch and most commentators take it as "during supper".
- **4.220–232** — The drug: Merry (on 4.220) reports Dioscorides (4.28) on bugloss cast into wine to cheer, the view of Sprengel and of Mure that it was opium, of Sir H. Halford that it was henbane, and the allegory of Plutarch (*Table Talk* 1.1.4) and Macrobius (*Saturnalia* 7.1), who took it for Helen's eloquence.
- **4.248** — δέκτῃ "a beggar", as Aristarchus took it (ἐπαίτῃ); the cyclic poet read Δέκτης as a name (scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **4.285–289** — Hardly any edition had the five lines, and Aristarchus rejected them, since the Iliad does not mention Anticlus (Merry's critical note); Merry (on 4.285) adds that they agree ill with 4.282–284 and 4.275, and reports a scholium that Anticlus came from the Cycle.
- **4.325** — περὶ, as the digitized text prints it, where the same verse at 3.95 has πέρι; Merry's text also has περὶ here.
- **4.339** — ἀμφοτέροισι "on both of them": Aristophanes took it of the hind and her young; Merry (on 4.339) prefers Eustathius' remark that the hind has twins, so that the many suitors are not likened to one fawn.
- **4.353** — Zenodotus rejected the line, asking what commands there had been (scholia, in Merry's critical note); for Merry it is a saying added by a later rhapsode.
- **4.366** — Εἰδοθέη: Zenodotus read Εὐρυνόμη, and the scholia add that Aeschylus in his *Proteus* named her Εἰδοθέα (Merry's critical note).
- **4.400** — ἀμφιβεβήκῃ, the subjunctive, is Bekker's reading from a Vienna manuscript, for ἀμφιβεβήκει (Merry's critical note).
- **4.404** — νέποδες "brood": "web-footed" (Apollonius, the *Etymologicum Magnum*) or "footless" (other grammarians); Merry (on 4.404) refers the word to the root of Latin nepos.
- **4.412** — πεμπάσσεται "will count them off by fives", properly on the fingers (Merry on 4.412).
- **4.441** — ἔνθα κεν "There … would have been": the scholia's lemma is κεῖθι δὴ αἰνότατος, adding that most copies have ἔνθα κεν, which Bekker restored (Merry's critical note).
- **4.456** — ἠυγένειος "bearded" (*Iliad* 15.275); Merry (on 4.456) suspects a lengthened form of εὐγενής, "noble".
- **4.465** — παρατροπέων "trying to mislead me", transitive, as παρατρωπάω at *Iliad* 9.500 (Merry on 4.465); Nitzsch took it intransitively, "shirking the truth". Aristarchus read ἐρεείνεις, the text's word, not ἀγορεύεις (Merry's critical note).
- **4.477, 4.581** — διιπετέος ποταμοῖο "the river fallen from Zeus" (*Iliad* 16.174): Eustathius explains it as "rain-fed", since the Nile is filled by the heavy summer rains in Ethiopia, "as Aristotle and Eudoxus say" (Merry on 4.477).
- **4.489** — ἀδευκέι "bitter": the older rendering, from δεῦκος, "sweet" in Aetolian (scholia); Apollonius explains it as "unseemly", Hesychius as "unexpected" (Merry on 4.489).
- **4.492–555** — The quotation marks of the speeches Menelaus reports inside his tale (4.333–592) are single throughout: the digitized text gave nine of the thirteen single marks, and four double or mixed (no opening mark at 492, ” at 537, “ ” at 543–547 and 551–553, ‘“ at 555), which the rows correct.
- **4.500** — Γυρῇσιν "the Gyrae", rocks named for their rounded shape (Γυραίῃ, 4.507): Eustathius and Hesychius place them near Myconos, Quintus of Smyrna (14.560) off Caphereus in Euboea, which Merry thinks more correct (on 4.499).
- **4.511** — The scholia say that the line was in no edition and call it very cheap; Nitzsch rejects it on much the same ground (Merry on 4.510–511 and his critical note).
- **4.514–520** — What brought Agamemnon near Malea? Merry (on 4.514) weighs the course by the islands, reports that Nitzsch held 4.514–516 an interpolation and that Bothe would move 4.519–520, and notes that the difficulty disappears if, with the geographer Andron, Thyestes and Aegisthus after him had their home on Cythera.
- **4.536** — None of Agamemnon's companions survived, nor one of Aegisthus' men; the story here differs from the later form at 11.405 and following and at 24.97, where Clytemnestra plays so great a part (Merry on 4.536).
- **4.553** — Generally rejected (Merry's critical note); Eustathius defends it as the doubt of a man confused by grief (Merry on 4.553).
- **4.563** — Ἠλύσιον: Apion derived the name from the mud (ἰλύς) of the Nile, so that he may have read Ἰλύσιον (Merry's critical note, from Eustathius); Merry (on 4.563) suggests ἠλυθ-, "where men go".
- **4.567** — The *Problems* ascribed to Aristotle quote the verse as ἀλλʼ αἰεὶ ζεφύροιο διαπνείουσιν ἀῆται, "but always the breezes of the West Wind blow through", and ask why the west wind is the fairest and sweetest of the winds (26.31). The breeze shows that the Elysian plain belongs to the land of the living (Merry on 4.567).
- **4.569** — σφιν "in their eyes": the gods count Menelaus the son-in-law of Zeus, an ethical dative (Merry on 4.569).
- **4.572, 4.574** — ἤια, and νύξ, with commas, as the digitized text prints them, where the same verses at 4.427 and 4.429 end in raised dots.
- **4.590** — τρεῖς ἵππους "three horses": a pair and a trace-horse (scholia, in Merry on 4.590; compare *Iliad* 16.149–154).
- **4.594** — Telemachus asks to leave at once, yet the reckoning of the later books keeps him at Sparta for a month (Merry on 4.594).
- **4.603** — λωτός "lotus": a kind of clover, not the lotus of Book 9 (Merry on 4.603).
- **4.617** — Φαίδιμος, printed as a name: the scholia say it is uncertain whether φαίδιμος "glorious" is a proper name (Merry's critical note).
- **4.620–624** — Most editors since Wolf reject the lines as a patch, since a shared feast (ἔρανος) is another thing from the wedding feast of 4.3; Merry (on 4.621–624) rejects Eustathius' view that the lines describe what was going on in Ithaca.
- **4.641** — Ἐυπείθεος, as the digitized text prints it here only (Εὐπείθεος at 4.660 and 1.383), and without the comma after προσέφη that the same verse has at 1.383.
- **4.655–656** — Noemon saw Mentor "yesterday at daybreak" (χθιζὸν ὑπηοῖον), the morning after the ship sailed: the scenes in Ithaca (4.625–847) fall on the fourth day of the poem's action, while Telemachus drives from Pylos to Pherae, though the poem tells them after the day in Sparta (see structure/04.json).
- **4.668** — πρὶν ἥβης μέτρον ἱκέσθαι is Aristarchus' reading; the common text had πρὶν ἡμῖν πῆμα γενέσθαι, or φυτεῦσαι (Merry's critical note and on 4.668).
- **4.678** — αὐλῆς ἐκτὸς ἐών "being outside the court": Merry (on 4.678) takes αὐλῆς as a genitive of place, "in the yard, outside" the hall.
- **4.726** — The scholia call the verse superfluous (cp. 1.344; Merry's critical note). The digitized text ends it with a full stop, though the sentence runs on from μέν (4.724) to αὖ (4.727), and the same verse at 4.816 ends in a raised dot.
- **4.774–775** — πάντας ὁμῶς goes with μύθους, "all of it alike"; Nitzsch needlessly reads πάντες (Merry on 4.774).
- **4.784** — τεύχεα "arms": the weapons the suitors take against Telemachus, not the ship's tackle (Merry on 4.784).
- **4.785** — ἐν νοτίῳ "out in the water": Aristophanes read εἰνοδίῳ, "ready for the voyage" (Merry's critical note); the ship lies afloat, moored to the shore at the stern and to the anchor-stones at the bow, ready to sail (Merry on 4.785).
- **4.797** — Ἰφθίμη: Aristarchus doubted whether ἰφθίμη is an epithet or a name (Merry's critical note).
- **4.846** — Asteris: Merry rejects the modern identification with the rock of Daskalio (on 4.846, referring to his Appendix on Ithaca).
- **5.1** — Ἠὼς δʼ ἐκ λεχέων, the dawn verse of *Iliad* 11.1, is "a poetical way of expressing 'the next morning'" (Merry on 5.1). The poem does not say which morning: the structure file counts the council as the day after the last day at Sparta (4.306–624), and notes that it may follow the night that ends Book 4 in Ithaca instead.
- **5.3** — θῶκόνδε καθίζανον "sat down to their session": the gods' ordinary daily gathering in the hall of Zeus, not an assembly he calls (Merry on 5.3, comparing *Iliad* 4.1 and, for a summoned assembly, 8.2, 20.4); θόωκος is the session rather than the seat, as at 2.26.
- **5.7–20** — Athena's speech is a cento of earlier verses: 5.8–12 are Mentor's words in the assembly (2.230–234), 5.14–17 Proteus' words of Odysseus (4.557–560), and 5.19–20 Medon's to Penelope (4.701–702). Merry (on 5.7) allows that this may be an intentional recapitulation, against Hermann's canon that Homer repeats only formulas and messages.
- **5.20** — ἠγαθέην "sacred": Rhianus read ἠμαθίην, "sandy" (Merry's critical note).
- **5.23–24** — Athena had not spoken of vengeance on the suitors at the first council, where she spoke only of Odysseus' return (1.81–95); the thought is first Nestor's (3.216), and Teiresias' answer will repeat it (11.118; Merry on 5.23). The two verses return at 24.479–480.
- **5.27** — παλιμπετές "back again": a neuter adverb from παλιμπετής, not a shortened plural (Merry on 5.27, with Buttmann), joined with ἄψ in *Iliad* 16.395; the reversal of a course gives the sense "in vain". The raised dot the digitized text prints after νηί divides the subject from its verb; Merry's text has no stop there: a slip, left in the text, which the English reads as one clause.
- **5.28** — ἀντίον ηὔδα "said … face to face": here not an answer, but an address in another's presence (Merry on 5.28).
- **5.33** — σχεδίη "raft": properly the feminine of σχέδιος, perhaps "made of joined planks" (from σχεδόν, "close"), to which πολυδέσμου and 5.248 point, or "hastily made", as the scholia explain it and Plato uses σχεδία of a makeshift craft (*Phaedo* 85d, as Merry quotes it; Merry on 5.33). In his Appendix on the Homeric ship Merry treats it as a boat built like an ordinary ship rather than a raft or a flat-bottomed box (Brieger's view; Appendix I §1, §6). The English keeps "raft".
- **5.34** — ἤματί κʼ εἰκοστῷ: the commoner texts omitted κε (the scholia, in Merry's critical note); Aristarchus seems to have kept it.
- **5.35** — ἀγχίθεοι "near kin to the gods": the Phaeacians are of divine lineage (Merry on 5.34–35, where he reports the stories of their name and of Scheria, the "Coast", and its identification with Corcyra by Callimachus, as Strabo reports him, and by Thucydides).
- **5.36** — περὶ κῆρι "with all their hearts": περί of a completed circle, hence thoroughness and excess; the grammarians disagreed on its accent (Merry on 5.36).
- **5.39** — Τροίης "from Troy": a variant Τρωίης, which the scholia take as an adjective with ληίδος (5.40), "from the Trojan spoil" (Merry's critical note and on 5.39).
- **5.43–49** — = *Iliad* 24.339–345 (and 5.45–46 = 1.97–98). One scholium says the verses were carried from here, without need, to Athena's departure in Book 1 (1.96) and to Hermes' descent to Troy (*Iliad* 24.339); another reports the objection that the wand is of no use here, as it is in the Iliad, where it puts the guards to sleep, and answers that such things are the gods' own attributes, as Poseidon carries his trident to the Ethiopians (Merry's critical note). Merry (on 5.43–49) finds the wand more fitting in the Iliad.
- **5.54** — τῷ ἴκελος "like it": the scholia and Eustathius say the verse was added without need (Merry's critical note). Merry (on 5.54) defends the repetition after ἐοικώς (5.51), comparing *Iliad* 5.87, 5.93: Hermes is like the bird in his swoop, not in his form.
- **5.59** — ἐσχαρόφιν "on the hearth": a form of the o-declension for the metre; the ἐσχάρα here is perhaps a portable brazier (Merry on 5.59).
- **5.60** — κέδρου τʼ εὐκεάτοιο θύου τε "of cedar that splits easily and of thyine wood": Homer's κέδρος is not the cedar of Lebanon but a juniper, probably *Juniperus oxycedrus*; εὐκέατος is "easily split" or "ready split" into billets; θύον is a tree with resinous wood, perhaps the arbor vitae, not incense (Merry on 5.60, citing Athenaeus and Pliny). The English keeps "cedar" and "thyine wood".
- **5.62** — κερκίς "shuttle", rather than the rod that beats the threads close (Merry on 5.62).
- **5.66** — σκῶπες "owls": some wrote κῶπες (Eustathius, in Merry's critical note); Merry (on 5.66) reports Pliny's doubt what bird the scopes were.
- **5.69** — ἡμερίς "garden vine": a vine brought into cultivation from the wild (Merry on 5.69).
- **5.72** — ἴου "of violets": a variant σίου, "of marsh parsley", is said to be the correction of King Ptolemy Euergetes, who held the violet out of place there (Athenaeus, as Merry reports; Eustathius says many of the ancients liked it: Merry's critical note).
- **5.77–78** — ἄντην goes with ἰδοῦσα, "when she looked him in the face" (Merry on 5.77).
- **5.80** — οὐδʼ εἴ τις: Aristarchus read οὐδʼ ἥ τις, and ναίει, not ναίοι (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **5.83–84** — 5.83–84 return at 5.157–158; the scholia call 5.84 superfluous here and say that 5.158 was carried up from its place (Merry's critical note), and Merry's text brackets 5.84.
- **5.90** — εἰ τετελεσμένον ἐστίν "if it is a thing that can be accomplished": Merry (on 5.90) renders "if it is a thing that ever hath been done", which would imply that it can be done again, and reports Nitzsch's comparison with Aristotle's "what has happened is evidently possible" (*Poetics* 1451b17–18).
- **5.91** — "But come farther in …" = *Iliad* 18.387 is wanting in the best manuscripts, and does not fit Hermes already seated (5.86); Merry thinks it interpolated from the Iliad, unless the guest is bidden from one room to another (Merry's critical note).
- **5.92** — ὥς ἄρα: the digitized text prints the acute, where every other instance in Homer has ὣς ἄρα (5.148, 5.351 and often): a slip, left in the text.
- **5.100** — τίς δʼ ἄν: Bekker and Nauck would read τίς κε, since ἑκών once began with a digamma (Merry's critical note).
- **5.104** — ἁλιῶσαι "made vain": Merry (on 5.104) renders it "to thwart by open resistance", and παρεξελθεῖν "to overreach".
- **5.105–111** — Hermes sums up the returns: the Achaeans sinned against Athena, who roused an evil wind against them (as 3.132–135). Merry (on 5.108) holds that, whether or not the whole passage 5.105–111 is rejected, 5.110–111 cannot stand here (his text brackets them), since they would make Athena's storm the one that wrecked Odysseus, while the wreck was the work of Zeus, as Calypso says in the same verses (5.131–134) and Odysseus later (12.403–425).
- **5.112** — ἠνώγειν "bade" stands for ἠνώγεεν; Aristarchus seems to have written the pluperfect with the movable ν (Merry's critical note). The tense looks back to the moment when Zeus sent Hermes (Merry on 5.112).
- **5.118** — ζηλήμονες "jealous": Eustathius and the scholia record δηλήμονες, the word of *Iliad* 24.33; Merry (on 5.118) keeps ζηλήμονες, explained by the next verse.
- **5.120** — Merry puts the comma after ἀμφαδίην, so that "if one makes a man her dear husband" explains lying with men "openly"; ἤν τις has a variant ἥτις (Merry's critical note and on 5.120).
- **5.121–124** — Orion's death by Artemis in Ortygia: Merry (on 5.121, 5.123) reports Apollodorus' account of Artemis killing him in Delos and later forms of the story, and takes Ortygia as Delos, as against the Syracusan Ortygia; the Ortygia of 15.404 lies near Syrie. Some athetized 5.123–124, since in Homer Artemis never kills men (the scholia, in Merry's critical note); Merry's text reads ἕως μιν for ἧος.
- **5.124** — ἀγανοῖς βελέεσσιν "gentle arrows": of a painless death, here an arrow shot in anger (Merry on 5.124).
- **5.125–128** — Iasion: Hesiod has Demeter bear Plutus to the hero Iasion, "in a thrice-plowed fallow, in the rich land of Crete" (νειῷ ἔνι τριπόλῳ, *Theogony* 969–971; Merry on 5.125 cites the verse with the form Ἰάσιος); τρίπολος is a field plowed three times (Merry on 5.127); the scholia quote, with disapproval, a reading τριπύλῳ (Merry's critical note).
- **5.132** — ἔλσας "struck", from εἴλω, of a crushing blow, or "driving down" (Merry on 5.132); Zenodotus read ἐλάσας (Merry's critical note).
- **5.136** — ἀγήραον: Aristarchus read ἀγήρων (Merry's critical note). Merry (on 5.136) reports Hayman's remark that Calypso now feeds Odysseus with mortal food (5.196–199).
- **5.146** — ἐποπίζεο "have regard for": from ὄπις, the regard due to the gods, or their watch over men (Merry on 5.146).
- **5.156** — ἂμ πέτρῃσι is Aristarchus' reading for ἐν πέτρῃσι (Merry's critical note).
- **5.164** — ὑψοῦ "high up": the ἴκρια are the highest part of the hull; Brieger puts a comma after αὐτῆς and takes ὑψοῦ with φέρῃσιν (Merry on 5.164).
- **5.168** — ἵκηαι: Aristophanes read ἵκοιο, and two manuscripts ἣν πατρίδα (Merry's critical note).
- **5.179** — ἄλλο: Aristophanes wrote ἄλλοις, which Nauck thinks a slip for ἄλλως (Merry's critical note).
- **5.185** — ὕδωρ: a variant ὕδατος (Merry's critical note); Merry (on 5.185) reports Hesiod's Styx, made the great oath of the gods (*Theogony* 389, 775 and following).
- **5.202** — τοῖς ἄρα μύθων ἦρχε: the plural of the formula stands though Odysseus alone is there, Hermes having gone (Merry on 5.202); Aristonicus marked the line, since one speaks to one (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **5.208** — σὺν ἐμοί: most manuscripts; Eustathius and most editors παρʼ ἐμοί (Merry's critical note).
- **5.216–217** — Calypso spoke of Penelope's looks; Odysseus calls her περίφρων, "prudent", and speaks of his home (Merry on 5.216). ἀκιδνοτέρη is uncertain, "weaker" or "meaner" in the scholia (Merry on 5.217); εἰσάντα: Aristarchus εἰς ἄντα, the commoner texts εἰς σῶμα (Merry's critical note).
- **5.229** — "a cloak and a tunic": the cloak is named first, as the more conspicuous garment (Merry on 5.229).
- **5.232** — ἐφύπερθε is the reading of Aristarchus' copies, where the commoner ones had ἐπέθηκε, the text of 10.545 (Merry's critical note); κεφαλῇ goes with περιβάλετο (Merry on 5.232).
- **5.234** — δῶκέν οἱ is Bekker's reading and most modern editors', for the manuscripts' δῶκε μέν οἱ, because οἱ once began with a digamma; La Roche would have taken it if a single manuscript had it (Merry's critical note).
- **5.235** — ἀμφοτέρωθεν ἀκαχμένον "sharpened on both sides": a double blade (Merry on 5.235), as Göbel takes the axes of the contest of the bow (19.574).
- **5.240** — περίκηλα "well seasoned" is Aristarchus' reading; Chrysippus divided περὶ κῆλα (Merry's critical note): trees long dead but standing (Merry on 5.240).
- **5.243** — ἤνυτο: Cobet would read ἤνετο, which Nauck adopts (Merry on 5.243).
- **5.247–248** — Aristophanes held that the two verses say the same thing; ἄρασσεν has a variant ἄρηρεν (Merry's critical note). Merry (on 5.247) takes them as boring the pieces through to match and driving home the pegs.
- **5.249–251** — "As wide as a man … marks out the hull of a broad freight ship, … so wide did Odysseus make his broad raft" (Merry on 5.249); some read τορνώσατʼ for ποιήσατʼ in 5.251 (Merry's critical note). τορνώσεται points to curved sides, against Brieger's flat box (Appendix I §6).
- **5.252–253** — ἴκρια … ἐπηγκενίδεσσι: Merry translates "And he worked away, setting up the decks by fitting them to the close-set ribs, and he finished off with the long side-planking" (on 5.252): σταμῖνες the ribs, ἴκρια the decks fore and aft, ἐπηγκενίδες the long planks of the sides (Appendix I §§4–6). Brieger and Hentze make the ἴκρια the ribs and the σταμῖνες their stays. Rhianus read ἐπητανίδεσσι (Merry's critical note). The English follows Merry, with "deck" for the ἴκρια, as *Iliad* 15.729.
- **5.256–257** — φράξε … ῥίπεσσι … οἰσυΐνῃσι "fenced it … with osier withes": a wattled bulwark round the edge, "a safeguard against the waves" (Appendix I §7, after Eustathius); Autenrieth took it of calking the seams with withes. ὕλην: the scholia take it as ballast, which Merry finds open to objection (ἐπιχέω is "pour over", not "pour in"); the English keeps "wood" (Appendix I §7).
- **5.260** — ὑπέρας τε κάλους τε πόδας τε "the braces and halyards and sheets": the κάλοι raise the yard, the ὑπέραι at the yard-arms trim it, the πόδες at the lower corners set the sail (Appendix I §16).
- **5.261** — μοχλοῖσιν "with levers": a lever lifts rather than drags, and Merry thinks the word may mean "rollers" (Merry on 5.261).
- **5.262** — τέτρατον ἦμαρ ἔην, καὶ τῷ τετέλεστο ἅπαντα: "It was the end of the fourth day when all was now accomplished", καί joining the clauses (Merry on 5.262).
- **5.272** — ἐσορῶντι: Aristarchus' copies had both ἐσορῶντι and ἐσορῶντα (Merry's critical note).
- **5.273–275** — = *Iliad* 18.487–489, but the text prints Ἄρκτον θʼ … ἄμαξαν, where the Iliad has Ἄρκτόν θʼ … Ἄμαξαν, the Wagon as a name; Merry's text too has ἄμαξαν. Aristotle cites οἴη δʼ ἄμμορος as a metaphor, "the best known" put for the only one (*Poetics* 1461a20–21), since the Bear is not the only constellation that never sets; Merry (on 5.275) reports the attempts of Strabo and others to save the statement.
- **5.277** — ἐπʼ ἀριστερὰ χειρός "on his left hand": a variant νηός, which Nauck adopts (Merry's critical note); the phrase is found only here in Homer with χειρός, perhaps of the steersman's hand (Merry on 5.277).
- **5.281** — εἴσατο δʼ ὡς ὅτε ῥινόν "it looked like a shield": ῥινός is the oxhide, the shield, the low coast its rim and the mountains its boss; Aristarchus read ἐρινόν, "a fig", and some scholia explain ῥινόν as "mist" (Merry's critical note and on 5.281). Merry would read ὥς τε ῥινόν; Fäsi conjectured ὡς ὅτε τε ῥίον (Merry's critical note).
- **5.283** — the mountains of the Solymi: Strabo puts the Solymi on the heights of Taurus in Lycia and Pisidia (Merry on 5.283).
- **5.289** — μέγα πεῖραρ ὀιζύος "the great bounds of the misery": ἥ agrees with ὀιζύος, the misery that has come upon him. Merry (on 5.289) refers to his note on 12.51, where he rejects "snares of misery" (Autenrieth's *laquei miseriae*) and takes πεῖραρ ὀιζύος as the end, the consummation of misery, as τέλος θανάτοιο.
- **5.290** — ἅδην ἐλάαν κακότητος "give him his fill of misery": "drive him to his heart's content along the road of misery" (Merry on 5.290), as *Iliad* 19.423; others take ἅδην as a noun, "to satiety"; Cobet proposed a form of ἄω, "satiate" (Merry on 5.290).
- **5.296** — αἰθρηγενέτης "born of the clear sky", passive, of the North Wind coming down from the heights; the scholia explain it as bringing fair weather, or cold (Merry on 5.296); Rhianus and Aristophanes read αἰθρηγενέης (Merry's critical note).
- **5.302** — ἀναπλήσειν: also written ἀναπλῆσαι (Merry's critical note).
- **5.304** — Odysseus, not knowing that the storm is Poseidon's, blames Zeus, as men commonly do (the scholia, in Merry on 5.304).
- **5.309–311** — the fight over the body of Achilles, where Ajax carried off the body and Odysseus held off the Trojans, is told at 24.36–42 (Merry on 5.309).
- **5.311** — κλέος ἦγον "would have spread my fame", or "brought back the report of me" (Merry on 5.311).
- **5.312** — ἁλῶναι: Demetrius Ixion read ἀλέσθαι, for which Buttmann reads ὀλέσθαι (Merry's critical note).
- **5.314** — ἐπεσσύμενον: Aristarchus wrote ἐπισσύμενον, taking ἐπεσσύμενον for a perfect passive (Merry's critical note).
- **5.315** — πέσε: Rhianus read αὐτὸν βάλε, "it threw him", which the scholia call better (Merry's critical note).
- **5.318** — σπεῖρον "sail", the word of the winding-sheet at 2.102 (again 6.269).
- **5.328–329** — ὀπωρινὸς Βορέης "the North Wind of late summer": ὀπώρα runs from the rising of the Dog Star to the setting of the Pleiads (Merry on 5.328); the thistles are the dry heads blown over the plain (Merry on 5.329).
- **5.334** — βροτὸς αὐδήεσσα "a mortal with human voice": the ancients doubted the word; Merry (on 5.334) prefers Aristophanes' "speaking with human voice" to "famous" or the variant οὐδήεσσα, "earthly", which the scholia give to Aristotle, who is said to have written οὐδήεσσα of Ino and αὐλήεσσα of Circe and Calypso, living alone in their halls (Merry's critical note).
- **5.337** — ἀνεδύσετο: the verse was missing in most copies, and Aristarchus doubted its athetesis and wrote ὑπεδύσατο (the scholia, in Merry's critical note); Merry's text brackets it, as made up from 5.353.
- **5.344–345** — νόστου γαίης Φαιήκων "strive to come / to the land of the Phaeacians": νόστος here of coming to a place, not of returning (Merry on 5.345).
- **5.346–349** — στέρνοιο: Aristarchus' two editions had στέρνοισι; the scholia accent τανύσσαι as an infinitive, to go with βαλέειν at 5.349, where Merry prints the imperative τάνυσσαι; 5.347 δέος has a variant κακόν, and at 5.349 Aristophanes wrote αἶψʼ for ἄψ (Merry's critical notes).
- **5.350** — ἀπονόσφι τραπέσθαι "turn yourself away": those who may not watch a power above men at work turn away, as Circe bids at 10.528 (Merry on 5.350).
- **5.357** — ὅ τε "since": the text prints ὅ τε, "in that"; the common reading, which Merry keeps, is ὅτε, "when", and Bekker writes ὅ τε in several such places (Merry's critical note and on 5.357).
- **5.368–369** — ἠΐων θημῶνα … καρφαλέων "a heap of husks, dry ones": the refuse heaps of the winnowing floor (Merry on 5.368, comparing *Iliad* 5.502).
- **5.371** — κέληθʼ ὡς ἵππον ἐλαύνων "as though riding a horse": the art of riding is alluded to elsewhere in Homer only in the simile of *Iliad* 15.679–684, Diomedes' ride on Rhesus' horses being a case of necessity (*Iliad* 10.513; Merry on 5.371).
- **5.378** — διοτρεφέεσσι "whom Zeus has nurtured", here of a whole people; a variant gives Φαιήκεσσι (Merry's critical note and on 5.378).
- **5.379** — ὀνόσσεσθαι κακότητος "make light of your misery": a sarcastic threat, as if nothing short of the extremity of suffering would satisfy him (Merry on 5.379).
- **5.385–386** — πρὸ δὲ κύματʼ ἔαξεν "broke the waves before him" (Merry on 5.385); for ἧος some wrote ὅππως (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **5.389** — πλάζετο "he wandered": Aristarchus read it with a long α, perhaps taking it from πλήσσω, "was beaten", not from πλάζομαι (Merry's critical note).
- **5.391** — ἠδὲ γαλήνη: Aristarchus read ἡ δὲ γαλήνη, the article (Merry's critical note); Merry (on 5.391) prefers it, "and there came a breathless calm".
- **5.393** — ὑπό: Aristophanes and Rhianus read ἐπὶ κύματος (Merry's critical note).
- **5.395** — κεῖται: Merry's text prints κῆται, after Hermann; the manuscripts give κεῖται (Merry's critical note).
- **5.400** — ὅσσον τε γέγωνε βοήσας "as far away as a man can make himself heard with a shout" (Merry on 5.400).
- **5.402** — ῥόχθει: Demosthenes is said to have cured his lisp by repeating this verse (Merry on 5.402, reporting Zosimus' life of Demosthenes).
- **5.404–405** — ἐπιωγαί are roadsteads under the lee of the land, not harbors; ἀκταί headlands, σπιλάδες jagged rocks (Merry on 5.404–405).
- **5.409** — ἐπέρησα: Merry's text reads ἐτέλεσσα, with a variant ἐπέρασσα, which La Roche adopts (Merry's critical note).
- **5.422** — ἐξ ἁλός: Aristarchus wrote εἰν ἁλί (the scholia, in Merry's critical note); with ἐξ ἁλός, ἅλς is the water by the shore (Merry on 5.422).
- **5.431** — ἐπεσσύμενον is the wave rushing on him (Merry on 5.431); Demetrius Ixion read ἀπεσσύμενον (Merry's critical note).
- **5.432–435** — the octopus: as pebbles stick to the suckers of an octopus dragged from its hole, so the skin of his hands stuck to the rock; the likeness is the clinging grip, and the simile is not to be pressed (Merry on 5.432–433).
- **5.437** — ἐπιφροσύνην δῶκε "given him presence of mind": a variant εἰ μὴ ἐπὶ φρεσὶ θῆκε, as 5.427 (Merry's critical note).
- **5.445** — πολύλλιστον "longed for with many prayers", "greatly longed for", as τρίλλιστος (*Iliad* 8.488); the scholia also take it as an adverb, or as owning the river's divinity (Merry on 5.445 and his critical note).
- **5.453–454** — γούνατʼ ἔκαμψε "let both his knees sink", with "his strong hands": the picture is of a man exhausted, arms dropping and knees giving way, not of the rest that γόνυ κάμψειν means at *Iliad* 7.118 (Merry on 5.453).
- **5.458** — ἄμπνυτο "got his breath back" is the common reading (printed again at 24.349); Aristarchus read ἔμπνυτο, as at *Iliad* 22.475, whose second half the verse shares (Merry's critical note).
- **5.459** — ἀπὸ ἕο: Aristarchus' reading, as La Roche shows; the scholia give it to Zenodotus, with ἀπὸ ἕθεν as the common text (Merry's critical note).
- **5.466–469** — φυλάσσω is Aristarchus' reading, with φυλάξω (Merry's critical note); "I fear lest the cruel frost and fresh dew quench my life" (Merry's translation of 5.467–468); with κεκαφηότα Merry follows Eustathius, "having gasped out the spirit" (on 5.468). αὔρη δʼ has a variant αὔρη γάρ (Merry's critical note).
- **5.471** — μεθείη: the majority of manuscripts; others μεθείῃ, and La Roche gives Aristarchus μεθήῃ (Merry's critical note). The optative marks a further hope, beyond falling asleep (Merry on 5.471).
- **5.476–477** — ἐν περιφαινομένῳ "in a place open to view", a clearing; ἐξ ὁμόθεν "from the same spot", or "from the same root" (Merry on 5.476–477); the scholia report γεγαῶτας in the commentaries for πεφυῶτας, which Merry takes for a correction by Aristarchus (his critical note).
- **5.488–491** — the brand in the ashes: a man so lonely that he has no neighbor to give him a light keeps a brand buried in the ashes, as Odysseus keeps his spark of life under the leaves (Merry on 5.488).
- **5.492–493** — ὕπνος is the subject of παύσειε, as the masculine ἀμφικαλύψας shows (Merry on 5.492).
- **6.2** — ἀρημένος: Eustathius knew ἁρημένος with the rough breathing, and a few manuscripts give βεβαρημένος (Merry's critical note). The derivation is uncertain; unless ὕπνῳ can mean "sleepiness", Merry takes ὕπνῳ καὶ καμάτῳ as a syllepsis, or as one idea in two words, "oppressed with the sleep that weariness brings" (Merry on 6.2).
- **6.4** — εὐρυχόρῳ "with its wide dancing floors": Eustathius explains the word in Homer as a place "where there is room to dance", and in later writers as merely "spacious" (Merry on 6.4).
- **6.8** — δὲ Σχερίῃ is Aristarchus' reading; others read δʼ ἐν Σχερίῃ, and Apollonius ἐς Σχερίην (Merry's critical note).
- **6.27–28** — τὰ δέ answers καλὰ μέν, as if καλὰ δέ had been written: "thou thyself must don fine clothes, and give other garments to those who are going to take thee to their home" (Merry on 6.27).
- **6.29** — φάτις "talk", as φῆμις (6.273); "report" is κλέος. Callistratus read χάρις, "pleasure" at the sight, and said that Aristophanes altered it to φάτις (the scholia, in Merry's critical note). ἐκ τούτων is "from such sumptuous style" (Merry on 6.29).
- **6.35** — ὅθι τοι γένος ἐστὶ καὶ αὐτῇ: with ἐστί, "where you have your own family ties"; ὅθι takes up δῆμον rather than ἀριστῆες, so that Athena reminds Nausicaa that her suitors are the noblest men of her own people (Merry on 6.35).
- **6.42–46** — ὅθι φασί "where, they say", in what seems the statement of a belief all share: the scholia hold the words fitting if Olympus is the mountain, not if it is the sky. Köchly took the phrase as proof that the whole passage was inserted later; Merry thinks that needlessly strong: the verses may be suspected, since the context needs no description of Olympus, but the picture agrees with Homer's Olympus elsewhere (Merry on 6.42). Rhianus read αἰθήρ for αἴθρη (6.44) and τῇ ἔνι for τῷ ἔνι (6.46), referring it to the gleam (Merry's critical notes).
- **6.50** — διά: some read κατά (Merry's critical note).
- **6.57** — οὐκ ἂν δή μοι ἐφοπλίσσειας: "a tentative, beseeching form of question", "Could you not get me ready?" (Merry on 6.57). Rhianus read ἐφοπλίσσειαν, of the slaves (Merry's critical note).
- **6.60–61** — The manuscripts divide between ἐόντα, ἔχοντα and ἐόντι, ἔχοντι; Nauck preferred the datives; the text prints the accusatives (Merry's note on 6.60).
- **6.61** — χροΐ: the digitized text prints the acute before a word, where the same phrase has καθαρὰ χροῒ εἵμαθʼ (4.750, 4.759) and this book περὶ χροῒ (6.129): a slip, left in the text.
- **6.70** — ὑπερτερίῃ ἀραρυῖαν "fitted with a frame above": the scholia take ὑπερτερίη as a box for baggage, like the πεῖρινς, the wicker body fixed on Priam's wagon (*Iliad* 24.190, 24.267; 15.131); others as a movable hood or awning against sun and rain, which the participle ἀραρυῖαν makes the likelier to Merry (on 6.70). The English keeps the general sense, "upper works".
- **6.74–75** — φέρεν, κατέθηκεν: Aristophanes read φέρον and κατέθηκαν, making the slaves the subject (Merry's critical note).
- **6.80** — χυτλώσαιτο: χύτλον is anything poured, and technically a mixture of oil and water used by bathers, so the verb covers both the bathing and the anointing of 6.96 (Merry on 6.80).
- **6.83** — ἄμοτον "without end", as everywhere in the Iliad (19.300, 23.567), and τανύοντο "strained", of draft animals (*Iliad* 16.375). Aristarchus derived the word from ἀ- and μοτός "lint", "with unstaunched flow"; Merry glosses "without flagging", and the pace was steady rather than fast, since the handmaids followed on foot (Merry on 6.83).
- **6.87** — ὑπεκπρόρεεν: the manuscripts read the present ὑπεκπρορέει, which seems contrary to Homeric usage; Friedlander and Nauck would read the imperfect, which the text prints (Merry's critical note).
- **6.95** — ἀποπλύνεσκε: a variant ἀποπτύεσκε, with the scholium's gloss "cast up", would make the sea throw up the line of shingle; Nitzsch joins ποτὶ χέρσον with θάλασσα, "where the sea, beating on the shore, scoured the pebbles clean" (Merry on 6.95), and the English follows him.
- **6.100** — σφαίρῃ ταὶ δʼ ἄρʼ ἔπαιζον, with δέ in the third place of the verse, is the reading of all the copies of Aristarchus' recension (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **6.102** — κατʼ οὔρεα "over the mountains": Merry's text prints κατʼ οὔρεος, and a scholium records οὔρεα as the better reading, which the text here prints (Merry's critical note).
- **6.102–109** — Virgil turned the simile to Dido (*Aeneid* 1.498–504), and Valerius Probus, as Gellius reports, judged no passage of Homer less happily rendered: Nausicaa at play among her friends in a lonely place is rightly likened to Diana hunting on the mountains among the nymphs, but Dido walking through her city among the Tyrian lords is not (Merry on 6.102, quoting Gellius, *Attic Nights* 9.9).
- **6.106** — ἀγρονόμοι "who haunt the fields": some accented the word on the antepenult, and Megaclides read ἀγρόμεναι παίζουσιν ἀνὰ δρία παιπαλόεντα (the scholia, in Merry's critical note and on 6.106).
- **6.107** — ὑπὲρ … κάρη ἔχει: "lifts her head above all", or "overtops them by head and shoulders" (Merry on 6.107).
- **6.110–111** — ζεύξασʼ … πτύξασα describe the going home she now has in mind, "she now thought of packing up and going home"; the preparations themselves are not begun until 6.252–253 (Merry on 6.110).
- **6.115** — On the game, Eustathius guessed at a kind of ball play in which the thrower feints toward one player and throws to another, and recalled that Sophocles wrote a satyr play, the *Washerwomen* (Πλύντριαι), in which he himself played Nausicaa (Eustathius, as Merry reports him on 6.115).
- **6.116** — ἔμβαλε: restored by Wolf from Eustathius and the Harleian scholia; others read ἔμπεσε, which Nitzsch thinks the original, altered lest anyone imagine that Nausicaa fell into the water (Merry's critical note). Nausicaa is the subject of both ἅμαρτε and ἔμβαλε (Merry on 6.116).
- **6.122–125** — ὥς τε goes with κουράων, "as it were the voice of girls", and κουράων is defined by νυμφάων (Merry on 6.122). The verses 6.123–124, accepted by the scholia, are suspected or rejected by many modern editors: Nitzsch notes that 6.124 is *Iliad* 20.9 (and the *Homeric Hymn to Aphrodite* 99), and that a cry of nymphs would hardly suggest that mortals lived near (Merry on 6.122). ἦ νύ που (6.125) is a question, "Am I perhaps"; the scholiast, who thought the loneliness of the place suggested nymphs, wrote the disjunctive ἤ, "or", for the other possibility (Merry on 6.122). Editors end 6.125 with a full stop or with a question mark; the text prints the question (Merry's critical note).
- **6.144** — Athenocles, a pupil of Aristarchus, suspected the verse, for the repeated λίσσοιτο and because the only question is whether to clasp her knees or speak from a distance (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **6.149** — θεός νύ τις, ἦ βροτός ἐσσι: Ptolemy of Ascalon wrote ἦ with the circumflex, as a question, which the scholium thinks the more graceful; others took it as "whether a god or a mortal" (Merry's critical note). The text and the English give the question.
- **6.156** — ἐυφροσύνῃσιν: a reading ἐν εὐφροσύνῃσιν is rejected by the scholia, since Homer never divides the word (Merry's critical note).
- **6.160** — τοιοῦτον ἴδον: Bekker printed τοῖον Ϝεῖδον, La Roche, after Grashof, τοιόνδε ἴδον, and Nauck τοιοῦτον ἐγὼ ἴδον (Merry's critical note).
- **6.162–163** — The visit to Delos would belong to the voyage from Aulis to Troy through the Cyclades (Merry on 6.162). The scholia refer the palm to the first palm, by which Leto gave birth (*Hecuba* 458, as Merry cites it). Cicero's Quintus, in *On the Laws*, sets beside the oak of Marius' poem the olive on the Acropolis and "the same tall and slender palm that Homer's Ulysses said he saw at Delos", things that last longer in remembrance than in nature (*On the Laws* 1.2); Merry reads him as saying that the palm was still shown.
- **6.165** — μέλλεν is the reading of most manuscripts; a scholium preserves Aristonicus' remark that the poet does not know the form, which is of later Attic writers, a remark that makes sense only if it was made of ἤμελλεν (Merry's critical note).
- **6.166–168** — ὣς δʼ αὔτως (6.166) is answered by ὡς σέ (6.168): the comparison begins "at the wrong end", "tantum, illud conspicatus, obstupui quantum te iam nunc admiror" (Merry on 6.166).
- **6.167** — ἀνήλυθεν ἐκ δόρυ γαίης: the preposition stands apart from its noun, a licence unusual in Homer (Merry on 6.167, comparing 9.535, 11.115).
- **6.172** — κάββαλε: the Harleian and other manuscripts read κάμβαλε, which Bekker, La Roche and Ameis adopt (Merry's critical note). Merry renders "cast me ashore" (on 6.172).
- **6.174** — πάροιθεν "before that", before the respite comes; the scholium's "hereafter" cannot be right, since Homer says ὄπιθεν or ὀπίσσω for that (Merry on 6.174).
- **6.179** — εἴλυμα σπείρων "some wrapping for the clothes": a wrapper of coarse cloth in which the washing was packed, so that his request is a modest one (Merry on 6.179, after the scholia).
- **6.185** — μάλιστα δέ τʼ ἔκλυον αὐτοί "and they themselves know it best": Merry follows the scholium, "they themselves feel the good of it", comparing μάλιστα δὲ καὐτὸς ἀνέγνω (*Iliad* 13.734), and rejects Lobeck's "they praise each other and hear themselves praised"; but he calls the expression very strange, and records Nauck's judgment, *verba vitiosa* (Merry on 6.185). The English follows *Iliad* 13.734.
- **6.187–193** — The sentence begun by ἐπεί (6.187) is resumed at 6.191 after the parenthesis 6.188–190 on Zeus' arbitrary gifts (Merry on 6.187, 6.188).
- **6.193** — ταλαπείριος "much-tried": Aristarchus took it as ταλαίπωρος, "wretched", others as a stranger "come from afar" (the scholia on 7.24, as Merry quotes them); Merry refers both ταλαίπωρος and ταλαπείριος to a root περ- (on 6.193). One manuscript reads ἀντιάσασθαι (Merry's critical note); with ὧν ἐπέοιχʼ the sense "not to lack" is carried over from 6.192, "which a much-tried suppliant should not lack when he has come our way" (Merry on 6.193).
- **6.201** — διερὸς βροτός: Aristarchus explained διερός as "living"; Callistratus wrote δυερός, "wretched" (the scholia, in Merry's critical note). Merry renders "That man exists not as a living mortal, nor ever will be born, who shall come as a foeman to the Phaeacians' land", with οὗτος not pointing at Odysseus (on 6.201, 6.206); the English follows him.
- **6.204** — ἐνὶ πόντῳ does not show that Scheria is an island, only that the Phaeacians lived far across the sea (Merry on 6.204).
- **6.208** — δόσις δʼ ὀλίγη τε φίλη τε "a gift is small but dear": small to the giver, dear to the receiver, as the scholium explains (Merry on 6.208).
- **6.212** — Ὀδυσσῆʼ: others read Ὀδυσσέα (Merry's critical note).
- **6.222** — μετελθών: Eustathius reads ἐπελθών (Merry's critical note).
- **6.231** — The hyacinth is compared for the clustering curls, not the color: the Greeks thought the hyacinth dark, and Odysseus' hair is ξανθαί, "fair" (Merry: "auburn"), at 13.399 (Merry on 6.231–232).
- **6.239** — κλῦτέ μευ is a scholium's reading; the manuscripts have κλῦτέ μοι (Merry's critical note).
- **6.244–245** — Aristarchus athetized both verses, hesitating over the first, since Alcman imitated it, bringing in maidens who say "Father Zeus, would that he were my husband" (the scholia, in Merry's critical note). Plutarch blamed the girl's boldness; Ephorus, as a scholium reports, praised the words as from a soul well made for virtue, while the scholiast saw in them the luxury of the Phaeacians; Merry sides with Ephorus (on 6.244).
- **6.262** — αὐτὰρ ἐπήν …: the clause has no main clause unless it is sought at 6.295, where Nausicaa's directions resume; the form of the sentence is forgotten as the description of the city lengthens (Merry on 6.262), and the English leaves it unfinished. πόλιος ἐπιβήομεν "set foot in the city", as *Iliad* 16.396. ἐπιβήομεν is the scholia's reading; most manuscripts have ἐπιβείομεν, which La Roche supports and Merry prints, though he thinks ἐπιβήομεν the right form (Merry's critical note and note on 6.262).
- **6.262–265** — A harbor on either side and a narrow way in: the town stands on a peninsula joined to the land by a narrow neck, as Tyre was joined to the mainland by its causeway (Merry on 6.263); Aristophanes read εἰσίσθμη, after ἰσθμός (the scholia, in Merry's critical note on 6.264). νῆες ὁδὸν … εἰρύαται is "are drawn up along the road", not "guard the road", for guard ships seem un-Homeric (Merry on 6.264).
- **6.267** — ῥυτοῖσιν λάεσσι: "hauled" stones, too big to carry, as in the wall of Eumaeus' yard (14.10); they formed a low wall round the place of assembly, and perhaps its seats (8.5–6), not a pavement (Merry on 6.267).
- **6.269** — For σπεῖρα Eustathius gives a variant σπείρας, warping cables; for ἀποξύνουσιν he mentions another reading, which Buttmann, Bekker and Dindorf adopt, though, as La Roche says, it has no manuscript authority (Merry's critical note; the variant's spelling is not legible in the scan).
- **6.275–288** — The scholia report that the fourteen verses from 6.275 to "mingled with men" (6.288) were athetized as unfitting to the speaker (Merry's critical note). Merry reads 6.282–284 as bitter sarcasm: it is better so, since nothing in Phaeacia was good enough for her (on 6.282).
- **6.280** — ἤ τίς οἱ: the manuscripts give no variant, but it is doubtful whether τις can stay short before οἱ; Bekker reads ἠέ τις, others ἤ τί οἱ or ἠέ οἱ (Merry's critical note).
- **6.281** — ἕξει δέ μιν: the subject is still the god, "he will have her to wife for evermore", as ἔχεις Ἑλένην (4.569); in ἤματα πάντα Merry suspects an ironical glance at the gods' proverbial inconstancy toward their mortal loves (Merry on 6.281).
- **6.282** — καὐτή: Wolf changed the common reading εἴ κʼ to εἰ κʼ, but καὐτή is an admissible crasis (compare 3.258) (Merry's critical note).
- **6.287** — ἀέκητι φίλων πατρὸς καὶ μητρὸς ἐόντων: on the hint of a scholium that marks a short pause after φίλων, the parents are the friends, "in despite of her friends, father and mother, still alive" (Merry on 6.287). For ἥ τʼ a scholium records Aristarchus' ἠδʼ (Merry's critical note).
- **6.289** — ὦκʼ: Aristarchus' reading, probably on the analogy of νῦν δʼ ἐμέθεν ξύνες ὦκα (*Iliad* 2.26), which the text prints; Merry prints ὧδʼ (Merry's critical note).
- **6.293** — τέμενος "domain", "the royal park": the memory of it was kept in Corcyra in the Peloponnesian War, when stakes were cut from the precincts of Zeus and of Alcinous (Thucydides 3.70, as Merry cites it, on 6.293).
- **6.301–303** — οἷος δόμος Ἀλκινόοιο / ἥρωος is an awkward explanation of ἐοικότα τοῖσι (Merry on 6.301), and ἥρωος is scanned as a dactyl (Merry on 6.303).
- **6.307–309** — κίονι κεκλιμένη: Arete is seated, with her chair against the pillar, and the king's seat leans close to hers (αὐτῇ, "against her seat"), nearer the door (Merry on 6.307–308); a scholium reads αὐγῇ (Merry's critical note).
- **6.313–315** — The verses are wanting in several manuscripts and are found only in the margin of the Harleian; modern editors generally reject them as adding nothing new, and their place is 7.75–77 (Merry's critical note).
- **6.318** — τρώχων "ran", as τρωχῶσι of horses (*Iliad* 22.163): Callistratus read τρεχέτην (the scholia, in Merry's critical note). Eustathius contrasted the two verbs, a brisk pace and a walk, "And well they trotted, and well they walked"; Merry finds no different pace in them, taking πλίσσοντο as an explanation of τρώχων, "they sped on with prancing feet" (Merry on 6.318).
- **6.319–320** — "she drove just so that they might keep up on foot, … and she laid on the lash with judgment" (Merry on 6.319); νόῳ alone, without a preposition, is unusual (Merry on 6.320).
- **6.327** — The verse is quoted in the *Rhetoric* for the two things a speaker must aim at, to be liked and to be pitied (*Rhetoric* 1415b26–27).
- **6.329–331** — Athena does not yet show herself, and throws off this reserve only when Odysseus has landed in Ithaca, beyond Poseidon's power: it was the custom of the gods not to oppose one another openly (Merry on 6.330), as Artemis tells Theseus, "this is the custom among the gods: none of us will set himself against the eager will of another, but we always stand aside" (Euripides, *Hippolytus* 1328–1330).
- **7.1** — ὧς: the digitized text prints the circumflex, where the verse-opening ὣς ὁ μὲν ἔνθα has the grave everywhere else in the Odyssey (3.284, 3.301, 4.511, 6.1, 7.344): a slip, left in the text.
- **7.7** — δαῖε δέ οἱ πῦρ: a scholium takes the fire as a sign of winter; Merry thinks the evening reason enough, for light or warmth (Merry on 7.7).
- **7.13** — Zenodotus athetized the verse, since 7.7 has already said that the fire was lit, and on the difference between εἴσω and ἔνδον; Merry finds the second objection unintelligible (Merry's critical note).
- **7.14** — ἀμφὶ δʼ Ἀθήνη is in two manuscripts and in Macrobius, and Ameis adopted it; Merry prints αὐτὰρ Ἀθήνη (Merry's critical note). The text and the English follow ἀμφί.
- **7.22** — Aristophanes read ἦ ῥά μοι; Merry thinks ἦ ῥά νύ μοι more likely (Merry's critical note).
- **7.26** — a variant ending, καὶ ἔργα νέμονται, is recorded by a scholium and by Eustathius (Merry's critical note).
- **7.33** — ἔλθῃ with Bekker and La Roche; most manuscripts have ἔλθοι (Merry's critical note).
- **7.34** — θοός is the conventional epithet, ὠκύς the special one, "trusting in the speed of their swift ships" (Merry on 7.34), which the English follows.
- **7.41** — Zenodotus read ἥ σφισιν; the scholia reject it, since only Odysseus is covered (7.143) (Merry's critical note).
- **7.52** — Bekker and others reject the verse; a scholium records εἰ καὶ μάλα τηλόθεν (Merry's critical note).
- **7.54–55** — ὄνομα is the subject, "Arete is her name", and ἐπώνυμον a name given with a meaning; the scholia derive it from ἀρητός, "prayed for" (Merry on 7.54). τοκῆες are her ancestors: Alcinous is her uncle as well as her husband (7.63–66).
- **7.64–65** — ἄκουρον "without male issue"; Nauck's ἄωρον is needless (Merry on 7.64). νυμφίον "newly married", as *Iliad* 23.223 (see the Iliad's note there).
- **7.69** — τετίμηταί τε καὶ ἔστιν: Nauck calls the words corrupt (*verba vitiosa*), though the manuscripts give no variant (Merry's critical note).
