- **7.74** — ᾗσί τʼ, the feminine, "for the women she favors", is the reading Eustathius calls the more graceful; Merry prints οἷσίν τʼ, with the scholia he judges more correct, "for those to whom she shows favour, be they even men", and rejects "gives wise counsel" (Merry's critical note and on 7.74). The English follows the text.
- **7.79–80** — the passage is suspected, as Chaeris says (the scholia, in Merry's critical note), probably as an addition by a later rhapsode to please Athenian feeling (Merry).
- **7.86** — ἐληλέδατο, the reading of most manuscripts, "were drawn out": ἐλαύνειν of the continuous line of a wall, as ἐλαύνειν ὄγμον (*Iliad* 11.68); the vulgate has ἐληλάδατο, and some read ἐρηρέδατο, as at 7.95 (Merry's critical note and on 7.86). The walls are those of the court (Merry on 7.86).
- **7.87** — κύανος: Merry rejects "blue steel", which would rust, for a blue glass or enamel (Merry on 7.87); the Iliad's note on 11.19–28 names this coping.
- **7.100** — βωμῶν "pedestals": the scholia reject a reading βουνῶν; one Vienna manuscript has πύργων (Merry's critical note and on 7.100).
- **7.104** — μύλῃς: Merry prints μύλης and records μύλῃς and μύλοις (Merry's critical note).
- **7.106** — the point of the simile is the constant movement of the women at their work, not their sitting close as leaves grow (Merry on 7.106, against Nitzsch).
- **7.107** — καιρουσσέων, contracted from καιροεσσέων, "close-woven" (καῖρος, the loop that holds each thread of the warp); Buttmann thought Aristarchus read καιροσέων (Merry's critical note and on 7.107). Merry reports several explanations of the oil that drips from the linen.
- **7.110** — ἱστῶν τεχνῆσσαι; the vulgate has ἱστὸν … τεχνῆσαι (Merry's critical note).
- **7.114** — πεφύκασι is Herodian's reading; the manuscripts have πεφύκει (Merry's critical note).
- **7.123–126** — the vintage in three scenes: grapes drying on the warm level ground and others gathered, the treading, and the grapes that are only now forming or turning dark (Merry on 7.123–126). θειλόπεδον has a variant εἱλόπεδον, which Bekker and Nauck print (Merry's critical note).
- **7.144–145** — Aristarchus took ἄνεῳ as an adverb; λιτάνευεν is his reading, where the vulgate has ἐλλιτάνευεν (Merry's critical note).
- **7.155** — a scholium records Ἁλιθέρσης for Ἐχένηος (Merry's critical note).
- **7.156** — προγενέστερος "older", with Φαιήκων a partitive genitive, "like our use of elderly" (Merry on 7.156); Bekker reads προγενέστατος (Merry's critical note).
- **7.159** — a scholium records οὐ μὲν καὶ τό γε (Merry's critical note). κάλλιον "more honorable" than their custom of welcoming strangers (Merry on 7.159).
- **7.162** — ἄλλʼ ἄγε: the digitized text prints the elided ἄλλα for the conjunction of the formula ἀλλʼ ἄγε δή, "But come", found in some eighty other lines of the Odyssey: a slip, left in the text.
- **7.174** — a scholium marking an athetesis at 7.174 belongs to 7.232 (Merry's critical note on 7.232).
- **7.189** — ἐπί goes with καλέσαντες, "bidding them gather to the meeting" (Merry on 7.189); the English "call more of the elders" leaves it unexpressed.
- **7.195** — μεσσηγύς of space, "in between", as usual, not of time (Merry on 7.195, after a scholium).
- **7.197** — κατὰ κλῶθες βαρεῖαι: the Spinners, "the half-personified agency of αἶσα", the three Moirai being post-Homeric; κατά adverbial with νήσαντο; Eustathius knew a reading Κατακλῶθες (Merry on 7.197).
- **7.198** — γιγνομένῳ, as the model verse *Iliad* 20.128; Merry prints γεινομένῳ (on 7.198).
- **7.199** — Aristarchus' copies read κατʼ οὐρανόν (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **7.205** — κατακρύπτουσιν "they do not disguise themselves", as κατακρύπτων (4.247); ἐγγύθεν "near" in kin or in place, both views in Merry (on 7.205). The English "near to them" keeps both.
- **7.221** — ἐνιπλησθῆναι, the passive, given by the scholia as Aristarchus' reading; Merry prints ἐνιπλήσασθαι, which the scholia call the more poetic (Merry's critical note).
- **7.222** — ὀτρύνεσθαι, the infinitive for the imperative; Zenodotus, not knowing the use, wrote ὀτρύνεσθε (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **7.250** — ἔλσας: a variant ἐλάσας, perhaps Zenodotus' reading; and two readings, ἐκέασσε and ἐκέδασσε (Merry's critical note).
- **7.251–258** — the scholia say that eight verses were athetized, since the story is told later (12.447–453); Buttmann refers the remark to these verses, and Merry finds 7.254–255, a repetition of what Odysseus has just said, the most suspicious (Merry's critical note).
- **7.261** — ὀγδόατον is Dindorf's conjecture, which Bekker adopts; Merry prints ὄγδοον, and so this text prints the same verse at 14.287 (Merry's critical note).
- **7.272** — κέλευθον with Ameis and La Roche; Nauck and Merry read κέλευθα, after Ellendt (Merry's critical note and on 7.272).
- **7.273** — Nitzsch read οὐδʼ ἔτι (Merry's critical note).
- **7.289** — δείλετο is Aristarchus' reading, as Eustathius reports; others read δύσετο, which Merry prints; La Roche thought δείλετο a conjecture of Aristarchus, Buttmann defends it, and Merry takes both of sunset (Merry's critical note and on 7.289).
- **7.294** — the verse "seems to come in awkwardly"; ἐρξέμεν is probably an aorist (Merry on 7.294).
- **7.301** — σὺ δʼ ἄρα … ἱκέτευσας gives the reason why she was wrong, "for it was to her first thou didst make thy supplication" (Merry on 7.301); ἐς ἡμέτερον, "to our house", an Attic turn, as the scholia say (Merry's critical note).
- **7.303** — μή τοι: Merry's text has μή μοι, without a note; the English is the same either way.
- **7.311–316** — Aristarchus doubted whether the six verses were Homer's (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **7.314** — δέ κʼ is Hermann's correction of δέ τʼ, which makes the construction clearer (Merry on 7.314 and his critical note).
- **7.317–318** — αὔριον ἔς· τῆμος with most modern editors since Nitzsch; the majority of manuscripts divide the verse at αὔριον, reading ἐς τῆμος δέ, "until then" (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **7.323–324** — Rhadamanthys lives in Elysium (4.564) and Tityus suffers in Hades (11.576): Merry finds no clue to the legend of their voyage (Merry on 7.323).
- **7.326** — ἀπήνυσαν: a gloss gives ἀπήγαγον (Merry's critical note).
- **7.330** — a scholium preserves two other endings, εἶπε πρὸς ὃν μεγαλήτορα θυμόν and ἰδὼν εἰς οὐρανὸν εὐρύν (Merry's critical note).
- **7.347** — πόρσυνε; Aristarchus' copies had πόρσαινε (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.15** — Bekker, after Bentley, reads θυμόν τε ἑκάστου, for the initial digamma of ἕκαστος (Merry's critical note). Ameis notes that the formula, ten times in the Iliad, is found only here in the Odyssey (Merry on 8.15).
- **8.16** — βροτῶν: the translation takes it with ἔμπληντο, "were filled with mortals"; Merry would rather join it with ἀγοραί τε καὶ ἕδραι, "the places of assembly and the seats of men" (Merry on 8.16).
- **8.22–23** — ἐκτελέσειεν ἀέθλους / πολλούς: Odysseus in fact takes part only in the discus. Zenodotus athetized 8.23, "for he did not accomplish many contests in Phaeacia, but only threw the discus" (the scholia, in Merry's critical note), and Merry reports Crates' attempt, from Nitzsch, to take the "contests" as the full tale of his past sufferings, which he rejects (Merry on 8.22).
- **8.29** — "whether from the men of the east or from the men of the west": the two clauses expand ὅς τις, "whoever he may be" (Merry on 8.29).
- **8.30** — ἔμπεδον εἶναι, "that it be made sure": of a boon, "certain" before it is given and after (Merry on 8.30).
- **8.31, 8.36** — πάρος in its idiomatic use for custom, "as has been our custom", "as always" (Merry on 8.31).
- **8.35–36** — κούρω δὲ δύω καὶ πεντήκοντα / κρινάσθων: the translation takes the imperative as passive in sense, "let two and fifty young men be chosen", as the scholia do; Merry takes it as a true middle with an indefinite subject, "let people choose", and κούρω as its object (Merry on 8.36). The dual with fifty-two is an "irrational" use, as βήτην at 8.49 (Merry on 8.35, 8.49); a few manuscripts, mistaking the voice of κρινάσθων, wrote κοῦροι (Merry's critical note).
- **8.38** — ἔκβητε, "step out again": the young men leave the ship at its moorings (Merry on 8.38).
- **8.58** — the verse is missing in the Harleian and some other manuscripts, perhaps as superfluous after 8.17 (Bothe, quoted in Merry's critical note); neither the scholia nor Eustathius notice it.
- **8.67, 8.105** — Aristophanes read δῆσε for κρέμασεν (Merry's critical notes).
- **8.68** — ἐπέφραδε χερσὶν ἑλέσθαι: the herald shows the blind singer where the lyre hangs, guiding his hand to it (Merry on 8.68).
- **8.73** — a variant ἐνῆκεν for ἀνῆκεν (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.74–82** — οἴμης, the "strain", literally the "way" of song (Merry on 8.74). The quarrel of Odysseus and Achilles is told nowhere else in Homer. The scholia say that Apollo at Delphi told Agamemnon that Ilium would fall when the best of the Greeks quarreled, and set the quarrel after Hector's death, Achilles praising courage and Odysseus wit; Nitzsch placed it at Tenedos before the Iliad (Merry on 8.75), where the *Rhetoric* puts Achilles' anger at not being invited to dinner (1401b16–19). Merry takes the oracle as given before the war (Merry on 8.81). Some editions lacked 8.81–82, and they were athetized (the scholia); Merry finds the notice incomplete (Merry's critical note).
- **8.78** — ὅ τʼ "that", which this text prints with La Roche, or ὅτʼ "when", which Merry prints (Merry's critical note).
- **8.80** — λάινον οὐδόν, the stone threshold of the temple at Pytho, as *Iliad* 9.404 (Merry on 8.80).
- **8.85** — κὰκ κεφαλῆς εἴρυσσε: the φᾶρος, a square cloth worn over the left shoulder, has a long corner that Odysseus throws over his head from behind (Merry on 8.85).
- **8.87–92** — the iterative optatives: at every pause Odysseus wipes his tears and pours a libation, and at every new beginning he covers his head again (Merry on 8.87).
- **8.99** — some wrote ἑταίρῃ for θαλείῃ, probably a gloss on συνήορος from 17.271 (Merry's critical note).
- **8.103, 8.126** — παλαιμοσύνῃ, παλαιμοσύνης: Aristarchus' spelling, without the σ that the Iliad's text prints (*Iliad* 23.701) (Eustathius, in Merry's critical note on 8.103).
- **8.108** — ἀέθλια, "the games", the tests of prowess (Merry on 8.108).
- **8.111–119** — the Phaeacian names are taken from the sea and seafaring, except Alcinous, Arete, Laodamas, Polybus and Rhexenor; Ναυβολίδης may come from βολίς, a sounding-lead (Merry on 8.111).
- **8.113** — Πρωρεύς: the text lacks the iota subscript that it writes elsewhere in πρῳρ- (κυανοπρῴροιο 9.482 and often), and Merry prints Πρῳρεύς: a slip, left in the text.
- **8.116** — the common reading Ναυβολίδης θʼ would make "the son of Naubolus" another competitor; La Roche, after Bekker, and Nauck omit θʼ, as this text does (Merry's critical note).
- **8.121–125** — ἀπὸ νύσσης, "from the starting line"; τέτατο δρόμος, "the running was strained to the full" (Merry on 8.121); the mules' furrow-length (8.124), as *Iliad* 10.351–352 (Merry on 8.124); ὑπεκπροθέων, "slipping forward in advance" (Merry on 8.125).
- **8.139** — συγχεῦαι, "to unstring", "weaken", a word generally used of the mind, here of the body (Merry on 8.139).
- **8.142** — neither Aristarchus nor Zenodotus nor Aristophanes knew the verse (the scholia, in Merry's critical notes on 8.68 and 8.142, with Lehrs' comment that it was absent from the better manuscripts).
- **8.144** — βῆ for στῆ (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.148** — ἤ ὅ τι: the text keeps the acute before a word that is not an enclitic, where it prints the grave elsewhere (ἢ ὅ γε, 2.327 and often): a slip, left in the text.
- **8.158** — φώνησέν τε for νείκεσέ τʼ ἄντην (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.161–164** — Merry's version: "But to such an one as plies with a many-benched ship, captain of a crew that are traders, and is either in charge of a freight or vigilant over a home-cargo of greedily gotten gains" (Merry on 8.161), the τε … καί being disjunctive (Merry on 8.162–164). Variants: θαμά for θʼ ἅμα (8.161); ἐπίστροφος (Aristophanes) for ἐπίσκοπος, εἶσι for ᾖσιν, ἑταίρων for ὁδαίων (8.163) (Merry's critical notes).
- **8.169** — Bekker and Merry read γάρ for γάρ τʼ, the τε being added only to lengthen the syllable (Merry's critical note).
- **8.170** — μορφὴν … ἔπεσι στέφει: μορφήν is a quasi-cognate accusative and ἔπεσι the remoter object, "puts a crown of grace upon his words", as the passive χάρις ἀμφιπεριστέφεται ἐπέεσσιν (8.175) shows (Merry on 8.170); the translation follows him, "a god crowns his words with beauty". Lehrs would write ἀμφὶ περιστέφεται at 8.175 (Merry's critical note).
- **8.171–172** — αἰδοῖ μειλιχίῃ: the translation joins it to the speaker; Merry, after Hesiod's like portrait of a king (*Theogony* 81 ff.), joins it to the onlookers, who look on him "with submissive reverence" (Merry on 8.170–172).
- **8.177** — ἀποφώλιος: the derivation is most uncertain (ἀπό and ὄφελος; a root "to blow"; ἀπάφεσθαι "to cheat"; Autenrieth's "grown out of shape") (Merry on 8.177).
- **8.180–181** — ἔμμεναι is a past tense, as πεποίθεα: "I was among the first, when I trusted in my youth" (Merry on 8.181).
- **8.186** — αὐτῷ φάρει, "with his mantle still on", the sociative dative (Merry on 8.186, after Monro).
- **8.187** — πάχετον: a positive, a collateral form of παχύς (Merry), or a shortened comparative, παχύτερον (the scholia and the Etymologicum Magnum) (Merry on 8.187).
- **8.192–193** — σήματα are the pegs that marked where each discus fell, the discus itself being taken up again; τέρματα, the place where Odysseus' fell (Merry on 8.192, 8.193). Variants βήματα for σήματα (the scholia) and πάντα for πάντων (Merry's critical note).
- **8.198** — τόδε γʼ is Aristarchus' reading; others had τόν γʼ or τόνδʼ (Merry's critical note and on 8.198).
- **8.214** — πάντα, "thoroughly": "thoroughly skilled am I" (Merry on 8.214); the translation takes it with the contests, "at all the contests there are".
- **8.225** — ἐρίζεσκον only means "matched themselves"; of the two, Eurytus alone contended with the gods (Merry on 8.225).
- **8.232–233** — κομιδή: "provision", or more generally "care", "comfort" (Merry on 8.232, who finds a real difficulty in the lines); Bekker omits the words between ἐπεὶ οὐ and ἐπηετανός (Merry's critical note).
- **8.237–239** — ὡς ἂν … ὄνοιτο: the translation takes it of the manner of the rebuke, with Bothe and Eustathius; Merry makes it the consequence of ἐθέλεις, "in such manner that no one would think slightingly of thee" (Merry on 8.237).
- **8.243** — δαινύῃ; Ameis' δαινύεαι, which Monro accepts (Merry on 8.243).
- **8.251** — παίσατε, from παίζω, as 6.106 (Merry on 8.251); variants παίξατε, Zenodotus' παίσατον, and a scholium's πλήξατε (Merry's critical note).
- **8.260** — χορός, the dancing floor; ἀγών, the ring of spectators, "as already in 200, 238" (Merry on 8.260).
- **8.266–366** — the song of Ares and Aphrodite: ancient critics may have rejected it, but Aristarchus, Rhianus and Herodian commented on its readings (8.288, 8.299, 8.307, 8.352, 8.355), and the scholia note that Homer elsewhere marries Hephaestus to Charis (*Iliad* 18.382–383) (Merry on 8.267). 8.333–342 were missing in some copies "because of their impropriety", and athetized in some (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.269** — πολλὰ δʼ ἔδωκε: Ares' gifts to Aphrodite, to win her consent (Merry on 8.269).
- **8.271** — Ἥλιος, contracted, only here in Homer, where the poem always says Ἠέλιος (the scholia); Nauck suggests Ἠέλιος, ὃ νόησε (Merry's critical note).
- **8.284** — Merry prints ἔσκεν for ἐστίν; the manuscripts vary, and the scholia record ἔπλετο πασέων (Merry's critical note).
- **8.285** — ἀλαοσκοπιήν, "a blind watch" (Merry on 8.285); La Roche gives ἀλαὸς σκοπιήν as Aristarchus' reading, and ἀλαὸν σκοπιήν seems to have been Zenodotus' (Merry's critical note).
- **8.288** — a reading ἰχανόων (the Etymologicum Magnum); Ἀφροδίτης for Κυθερείης (Merry's critical notes).
- **8.292** — γύναι for φίλη; Merry and La Roche read the dual εὐνηθέντε (Merry's critical note). τραπείομεν, from τέρπω, "take our pleasure" (Merry on 8.292).
- **8.294** — Σίντιας ἀγριοφώνους: Porson referred the gloss ἀκριτόφωνος of Apollonius and Hesychius to this line (Merry's critical note); the Sintians were a Thracian people (Merry on 8.283, from Strabo).
- **8.299** — ὅ τʼ "that" (this text) or ὅτʼ "when", which the emphasis of τότε δή favors (Merry on 8.299); Rhianus read πέλοιτο (Merry's critical note).
- **8.300** — ἀμφιγυήεις: "lame of both feet", or "of the two strong arms" (Merry on 8.300).
- **8.303** — the verse is omitted in most manuscripts, borrowed from 2.298 (Merry's critical note); the text lacks the comma after δῶμα that 2.298 has.
- **8.307** — γελαστά is the reading of the manuscripts, of Aristarchus and of Herodian; Apollonius' lexicon, the Etymologicum Magnum and Eustathius have ἔργʼ ἀγέλαστα, "no laughing matter", which Nauck follows (Merry's critical note). Merry prefers "deeds of mockery", and takes οὐκ ἐπιεικτά as "unyielding", and so "hard, cruel" (Merry on 8.307); the translation's "laughable, and hard to bear" keeps both senses open.
- **8.309** — ἀίδηλος, "annihilating", "making unseen" (Merry on 8.309): "destructive", as *Iliad* 5.880.
- **8.318** — ἀποδῷσιν, the reading of this text, of La Roche and of Nauck; most manuscripts have ἀποδώσει (Merry's critical note and on 8.318).
- **8.320** — οὐκ ἐχέθυμος: the translation follows the scholium's "restraining desire", "she has no self-control"; Merry, taking θυμός as "heart, affections", would render "heartless", and would prefer the scholium's sense "were it not for" the compound ἐχέφρων (Merry on 8.320).
- **8.325** — ἑάων: the breathing and the gender are uncertain (Merry on 8.325 and his critical note).
- **8.351** — δειλαί τοι δειλῶν γε καὶ ἐγγύαι ἐγγυάασθαι: "Even surety for a sorry fellow is a sorry thing to hold in hand" (Merry on 8.351), the middle being the taker's act (Eustathius); Nitzsch took δειλός as "weak".
- **8.352–353** — δέοιμι, "put thee in chains" (Merry on 8.352); a scholium's gloss points to δήοιμι, and Aristarchus is reported with εὐθύνοιμι, which La Roche takes as his explanation, not his reading; the scholia give χρέως and χρείως, and Aristarchus δασμόν (Merry's critical notes).
- **8.365** — ἐπενήνοθεν: "such as deck the everliving gods" (Merry on 8.365), or, with the oil as subject, "is shed over" them.
- **8.377** — ἀνʼ ἰθύν: the ancients took ἀνιθύν as one word, an adverb (Eustathius, in Merry's critical note); Merry, "straight upwards" (Merry on 8.377).
- **8.380** — ἑστεῶτες is Aristarchus' reading; a variant δοῦπος for κόμπος (Merry's critical note).
- **8.396** — Düntzer and Nauck read αὐτός for αὐτόν (Merry on 8.396).
- **8.405** — "it will be worth much to him": a thing of price, or a gift that makes amends for much (Merry on 8.405).
- **8.425, 8.441** — αὐτή (Bekker) for αὐτῇ: "put in it yourself", besides the gifts of the others (Merry's critical note and on 8.425).
- **8.429** — ὕμνος, only here in Homer (Merry on 8.429).
- **8.444** — αὖτε: "again", or "by and by" (Merry on 8.444); δηλήσεται of the contents, "should spoil them for thee" (Merry).
- **8.450** — the text prints ἄρ before a vowel, where Merry has ἄρʼ.
- **8.461–462** — ἵνα is final: "may it be well with thee, stranger, in order that" (Merry on 8.461). ζῳάγρια: Merry, after Nitzsch, sets the sense he finds in the Iliad, "the price paid to a conqueror for sparing the life of a defeated foeman" (18.407), against this one, the debt of gratitude for a life saved, and finds that "the price of life" expresses both (Merry on 8.462); but at *Iliad* 18.405–407 the word is Hephaestus' debt to Thetis, who saved him when he fell, the same sense as Nausicaa's.
- **8.463** — the text ends the speech formula with a full stop, where it has a raised dot elsewhere (8.412, 5.214, 7.240): a slip, left in the text.
- **8.477–478** — προσπτύξομαι, still under ὄφρα: "that I may do homage to him, though sad at heart" (Merry on 8.477).
- **8.489–490** — Bekker rejected 8.490 without manuscript authority; Düntzer suspected ἔρξαν and the repeated Ἀχαιοί (Merry on 8.490).
- **8.492** — μετάβηθι, "change your theme": from the first lay, the quarrel (8.75), not the lay of Ares (Merry on 8.492).
- **8.494** — δόλον; Aristarchus and Aristophanes read δόλῳ, "by a trick" (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **8.499** — ὁρμηθεὶς θεοῦ: "stirred by the god", or, in a scholium, "starting from the god", with a prelude (Merry on 8.499).
- **8.505** — ἄκριτα: "endless", "undecided" or "indiscriminate", Merry preferring the last (Merry on 8.505).
- **8.507** — διαπλῆξαι is Aristarchus' reading; διατμῆξαι perhaps Zenodotus' (Merry's critical note).
- **8.509** — θελκτήριον, "to appease": "a propitiation of the gods" (Merry on 8.509), where the Iliad's θελκτήρια are "enchantments" (*Iliad* 14.215).
- **8.529** — εἴρερον, "slavery", only here in Homer (Merry on 8.529, who finds the passage not free from suspicion).
- **8.541** — μάλα; the scholia record μέγα (Merry's critical note).
- **8.546** — ἀντί, "as good as", as *Iliad* 21.75 (Merry on 8.546).
- **8.552** — πάμπαν goes with οὔ τις, "no one at all" (Merry on 8.552).
- **8.555** — the text prints a raised dot after γαῖάν τε, which breaks the series τε … τε … τε; Merry prints no stop: a slip, left in the text.
- **8.556–563** — the ships that know men's minds: Merry reports Welcker's view of the Phaeacians as ferrymen of the dead, which he thinks little suited to them (Merry on 8.556).
- **8.569** — ῥαισέμεναι; variants ῥαίσεσθαι and ῥαίσασθαι (Merry's critical note). The mountain is a wall round the city, cutting it off from the sea, not a mountain thrown upon it (Merry on 8.569).
- **8.583** — αὐτῶν; a variant ἀνδρῶν, probably a gloss (Merry's critical note).
- **8.586** — πεπνυμένα; a variant κεχαρισμένα (Merry's critical note).
- **9.1** — Odysseus' tale (Books 9–12) was called Ἀλκίνου ἀπόλογος or ἀπόλογοι, "the story told to Alcinous", as early as Plato (*Republic* 614b), and Aristotle uses the phrase (*Rhetoric* 1417a14, *Poetics* 1455a2), though his reference is rather to 8.83–95 and 8.521–534, as Mayor remarks; the time of the book is the evening of the second day after the arrival in Scheria (Merry on 9.1).
- **9.3–4** — repeated from 1.370–371 (Merry on 9.3–4).
- **9.5** — τέλος "fulfillment": the "realisation" or "consummation", the "highest perfection" of a thing rather than its end (Merry on 9.5). Lines 5–8 bear obeli in Ambros. Q, which Merry takes for an objection to the sentiment, not an athetesis (his critical note).
- **9.6** — ἔχῃ κάτα: the text accents κάτα, in tmesis with ἔχῃ (κατέχῃ), and the English follows it; Merry prints κατὰ δῆμον and takes ἔχῃ as intransitive, "reigns" throughout the people (Merry on 9.6). Eratosthenes read κακότητος ἀπούσης (Merry's critical note).
- **9.11** — τοῦτό τι: "this thing in a sort of way", τι softening the superlative, as in σχεδόν τι, πάνυ τι, not a hyperbaton for κάλλιστόν τι (Merry on 9.11).
- **9.14** — τί πρῶτόν τοι ἔπειτα: the reading of the best manuscripts, against τί πρῶτον, τί δʼ ἔπειτα; ἔπειτα introduces a new stage, "What shall I tell you then first?" (Merry on 9.14 and his critical note).
- **9.19** — πᾶσι goes, in the sense of παντοίοις, with δόλοισιν rather than with ἀνθρώποισι: "settled" by 3.121–122 παντοίοισι δόλοισι (Merry on 9.19).
- **9.21–28** — Ithaca's place among the islands: Merry takes πανυπερτάτη πρὸς ζόφον as "farthest westward", against Strabo's "northward", and χθαμαλή as "low" in its plain sense, against Strabo's "near the mainland"; he concludes that the passage misplaces Ithaca among the islands of the group (Merry, Appendix on Ithaca). The English adds no name to 25: "my island itself".
- **9.22, 9.24** — Νήριτον: Crates and Philoxenus preferred Νήιον (Eustathius and the scholia); Σάμη: Apollodorus wrote Σάμος here and at 1.246, Aristarchus Σάμη (Merry's critical note).
- **9.27** — κουροτρόφος, "nurse of young men", like βωτιάνειρα of Phthia (*Iliad* 1.155) (Merry on 9.27).
- **9.28, 9.34** — ἧς γαίης, ἧς πατρίδος, "one's own land": the possessive of the third person used generally; the scholia: he did not say "mine", so that the saying might be more general (Merry on 9.28).
- **9.29–36** — 30 is wanting in most manuscripts and not quoted by Eustathius, probably interpolated from 1.15; Wolf rejected it and most modern editors follow him. 34–36 bear an obelus in Ambros. Q and one or two other manuscripts, accepted by Nitzsch and Bekker (Merry's critical note). The text prints the lines, and the English keeps them.
- **9.32** — Αἰαίη, the name also of Circe's island (10.135) (Merry on 9.32).
- **9.39–40** — the Cicones, αἰχμηταί at *Iliad* 2.846, on the south coast of Thrace; Ismarus their town, at the foot of the mountain of that name; Ἰσμάρῳ defines Κικόνεσσι, as Κύπρον … ἐς Πάφον (8.362–363) (Merry on 9.39).
- **9.42** — μή τίς μοι … "that no one, as far as I could help it, should go away deprived of a fair share", μοι as the later ἐμοῦ γʼ ἕνεκα (Merry on 9.42); the verse again 9.549, after *Iliad* 11.705.
- **9.43** — διερῷ ποδί "with nimble foot" (Merry on 6.201: see the note on 6.201).
- **9.49–50** — ἤπειρον "inland", against the Cicones of Ismarus by the sea; they know "how to fight from the chariot, and how to fight on foot in that part of the battle where one ought to do so" (Merry on 9.49–50).
- **9.52** — ἠέριοι "in the morning", from ἀήρ, the morning mists, or from ἦρι (Merry on 9.52).
- **9.54** — στησάμενοι … μάχην, "having set the battle in array", as *Iliad* 18.533 (Merry on 9.54).
- **9.58** — μετενίσσετο: Eustathius mentions μετενείσετο and μετενίσετο; the preposition marks the sun's changed course after noon, a descent, and at the end of the day's work the Sun unyokes his steeds, as the plowman his oxen (Merry on 9.58).
- **9.60** — six from each ship: Crates took it as no more than seventy-two in all, an average of six from twelve ships (9.159); Zoilus was offended at the suspicious symmetry (Merry on 9.60).
- **9.63** — ἄσμενοι ἐκ θανάτοιο, "glad to have escaped death", the full expression at *Iliad* 20.350; repeated at 9.566 (Merry on 9.63).
- **9.65** — the threefold call: τινα, distributive, is the subject of ἀῦσαι; the scholia explain it as the ancient custom of calling three times on the souls of men who die in a foreign land, and Eustathius adds that it called any survivor still hiding; Nitzsch distinguishes it from the farewell of *Aeneid* 6.231, but Merry finds it a similar religious act (Merry on 9.65).
- **9.70** — ἐπικάρσιαι: Eustathius "head downwards", the ships plunging their bows as they run before the wind; Nitzsch, after Apollonius' Lexicon, "slantwise", the ships driven with the wind abeam, which Merry adopts and the English follows (Merry on 9.70).
- **9.73–74** — προερέσσαμεν, according to Eustathius the reading of Aristarchus; συνεχές, with the first syllable long, since ἔχω once began with σ (Merry on 9.73–74 and his critical notes).
- **9.75** — θυμὸν ἔδοντες: compare Cicero's "ipse suum cor edens" (Merry on 9.75).
- **9.80** — Malea: the proverb "after rounding Malea, forget your home", quoted by Strabo, marks the dangers of the cape (Merry on 9.80).
- **9.83** — ἀτάρ, with Bekker and most modern editors and several manuscripts, for the common αὐτάρ (Merry's critical note).
- **9.84** — ἄνθινον εἶδαρ, "vegetable" food; Bothe conjectured ἀκάνθινον from Polybius' description of the lotus shrub (Merry on 9.84).
- **9.89–90** — σῖτον ἔδοντες marks human beings, as at *Iliad* 5.341 it marks men against gods (Merry on 9.89); Ameis rejects 90, which in the oldest manuscripts stands before 89 (Merry's critical note).
- **9.94** — the lotus of the Lotus-eaters is not the grass of *Iliad* 2.776; Merry quotes Herodotus' description of the fruit, as big as a mastic berry and as sweet as the date, from which the Lotus-eaters also make wine (Herodotus 4.177.1, read in the local text; Merry on 9.94).
- **9.96–99** — βούλοντο "would rather" (Merry on 9.96); ἐρεπτόμενοι, of beasts feeding (*Iliad* 2.776, 5.196), here of men "browsing" (Merry on 9.97); νηυσὶν ἔνι answers ἐπὶ νῆας: "when I had got them there" (Merry on 9.98–99).
- **9.102** — μή πως, against the older μή πω (Merry on 9.102 and his critical note).
- **9.106** — Κύκλωπες: the Theogony makes the name "round-eyed" (Hesiod, *Theogony* 144–145); Merry rejects Göttling's allusion to "Cyclopean" walls and reports Döderlein's derivation from κλώψ, a race of robbers (Merry on 9.106).
- **9.107** — θεοῖσι πεποιθότες: no devoutness, as 9.275 shows, but a careless confidence in the bounty of nature (Merry on 9.107).
- **9.114–115** — θεμιστεύει "is lord and master" (Merry on 9.114); Aristotle quotes the line with ἀλόχου in the *Nicomachean Ethics* (10) and with ἀλόχων in the *Politics* (1.2), as Merry reports (his critical note on 9.115).
- **9.116** — λάχεια: the scholia refer it to λαχαίνω, "with good soil", εὔγεως; the special mention of Zenodotus, who read ἐλάχεια (βραχεῖα, "small"), suggests that λάχεια was Aristarchus' reading; Nitzsch takes it as "rough, overgrown", against which Merry sets ἄροσις λείη (9.134), and Döderlein as "low-lying" (Merry on 9.116 and his critical note). ἔπειτα "now", a mere transition (Merry on 9.116).
- **9.122** — ἀρότοισιν "with tillage", by zeugma with καταΐσχεται "is occupied" (Merry on 9.122).
- **9.125** — μιλτοπάρῃοι, the epithet of Odysseus' own ships in the Catalogue (*Iliad* 2.637) (Merry on 9.125).
- **9.130** — ἐυκτιμένην predicative, with καί closely with νῆσον: the craftsmen would have worked the island as well as built ships (Merry on 9.130).
- **9.131** — ὥρια "in due season" (Merry on 9.131).
- **9.134–135** — ἄροσις λείη "smooth plowland" (Merry on 9.134); ἐπεὶ μάλα πῖαρ ὑπʼ οὖδας, "since very rich soil spreads below the surface", πῖαρ a noun (Merry on 9.135); γρ. ἀμμοῷεν for ἀμῷεν (Schol. H., in Merry's critical note).
- **9.136–138** — πεῖσμα, mooring tackle in general, the cable to the anchor-stones and the stern-cables (Merry on 9.136); after the negative οὐ χρεώ, the affirmative is understood: "all that is needful is" to run ashore and wait (Merry on 9.138).
- **9.140–141** — ἐπὶ κρατός "at the head of the harbor" (Merry on 9.140, with 13.102); ὑπὸ σπείους "forth from under" the cave (Merry on 9.141).
- **9.143–145** — νύκτα διʼ ὀρφναίην: not a standing epithet of night, but of this misty night (Merry on 9.143); περί, with the scholia and Eustathius, for the common παρά (144; Merry's critical note). The text prints προυφαίνετʼ and προύφαινε without the coronis, as at 3.8: left as printed.
- **9.147** — οὔτʼ οὖν: Merry keeps οὔτε against those who would read οὐδέ, an οὔτε being implied before νῆσον (Merry on 9.147).
- **9.149** — κελσάσῃσι νηυσί, a dativus commodi where a genitive absolute is expected (Merry on 9.149).
- **9.151** — ἀποβρίξαντες "having fallen asleep", properly "heavy with sleep" (Merry on 9.151).
- **9.157** — διὰ … τρίχα κοσμηθέντες, "arranged in three bands" (Merry on 9.157).
- **9.159–160** — Ameis reads ἐν δὲ ἑκάστῃ with some manuscripts, and Cod. Vind. 56 has αἶγας (Merry's critical note); λάγχανον, the one place in Homer where λαγχάνω means "fall by lot" (Merry on 9.160).
- **9.165** — the text prints a full stop after Κικόνων, which leaves ἱερὸν πτολίεθρον ἑλόντες without a verb: Κικόνων goes with πτολίεθρον, as Τροίης ἱερὸν πτολίεθρον (1.2), and Merry's text has no stop. A slip, left as printed; the English reads the verse without it.
- **9.167** — a scholium calls the verse "wholly spondaic", which implies the reading οἰῶν without τε (Merry's critical note); αὐτῶν, the Cyclopes against their flocks: they judged the land near by the smoke and the voices (Merry on 9.167).
- **9.173** — ἐμῇ … ἐμοῖς emphatic, "in my own ship with my own crew": the rest of the fleet stays at the island (Merry on 9.173).
- **9.182** — σπέος εἴδομεν: the digamma of εἴδομεν makes the position difficult; one manuscript (Ven. Marc. 456) has ἴδομεν (Merry's critical note).
- **9.185** — δέδμητο is Aristarchus' reading, Aristophanes' βέβλητο (the scholia, in Merry's critical note).
- **9.189** — ἀθεμίστια ᾔδη, οἶδα with the accusative of knowledge, as μήδεα εἰδώς (Merry on 9.189).
- **9.192** — ὅ τε "and this", which Wolf wrote with several manuscripts and Ameis, La Roche and Nauck follow, as the text prints it and Merry; Bekker, with Fäsi, follows the common reading ὅτε, a conjunction (Merry's critical note).
- **9.197** — Maron is the son of Dionysus in Euripides' *Cyclops* (141), Euanthes in the scholia on Apollonius (Merry on 9.197).
- **9.198** — ἀμφιβεβήκει "used to protect": the pluperfect refers to the time before Ismarus fell, since a god deserts a city whose doom is sealed; Nitzsch's ἀμφιβέβηκε is needless (Merry on 9.198).
- **9.199** — σὺν παιδί, the reading of Aristarchus and Aristophanes (Schol. H.); others σὺν παισί (Merry's critical note).
- **9.202** — τάλαντα: a definite weight of gold, not coined money (Merry on 9.202).
- **9.204** — δυώδεκα πᾶσιν "twelve in all", as 5.244 (Merry on 9.204).
- **9.206** — ἠείδη; La Roche writes ἠείδει with Eustathius, Hesychius and four manuscripts (Merry's critical note).
- **9.209** — the wine was poured into the water, the earliest custom, not the water into the wine (Merry on 9.209; compare 7.164).
- **9.214** — ἄνδρα is the subject of ἐπελεύσεσθαι, not its object, as Nitzsch prefers (Merry on 9.214).
- **9.216** — γρ. οὐδέ τινʼ ἔνδον (Schol. H., in Merry's critical note); οὐδέ with the force of ἀτὰρ οὐ (Merry on 9.216).
- **9.219** — ταρσοί, the racks for drying cheeses, the πλεκτοὶ τάλαροι of 9.247 (the scholia, from τέρσαι; Merry on 9.219).
- **9.222** — ναῖον "ran over" with whey; the variant νᾶον (Aristarchus, in Merry's critical note; Merry on 9.222).
- **9.225** — αἰνυμένους (ἡμᾶς) τυρῶν, "that we should take some of the cheeses and go back"; γρ. ἀχθομένους (Eustathius) (Merry on 9.225 and his critical note).
- **9.231** — ἐθύσαμεν, an offering to the gods of part of the cheeses, θύειν in Homer being the offering of firstfruits, not the slaughter of a victim; κήαντες is apparently Aristarchus' reading, κείαντες that of most manuscripts according to Eustathius (Merry on 9.231 and his critical note).
- **9.234** — ποτιδόρπιον, "that it might serve him for light at supper" (the scholia); the wood is for light, as the cressets of 18.307 (Merry on 9.234).
- **9.235, 9.239, 9.338** — 235 ἔντοσθεν, Eustathius' reading, for the common ἔκτοσθεν; at 239 and 338 most modern editors since Bekker's second edition print Rumpf's conjecture ἔντοθεν, the males left in the court, and so does Merry; the text prints ἔκτοθεν, which Döderlein and others take as "outside", with αὐλῆς a genitive of place; the English follows the text, "outside in the high-walled fold" (Merry on 9.239, 9.338 and his critical notes).
- **9.241–242** — the twenty-two wagons: a hyperbolic parody of the proverbial two men of *Iliad* 12.447–448, comic in its circumstantial number (Merry on 9.241); most modern editions print τεσσαράκυκλοι (Barnes) for τετράκυκλοι, which would need its ε and α lengthened, and Bekker reads ὀχλήσειαν (Merry's critical note); the scan's reference for τετράκυκλον is to the form of *Iliad* 24.324.
- **9.243** — ἠλίβατον: Merry rejects Apollonius' derivation from ἥλιος and βαίνειν, and prefers Buttmann's, after the scholia, "hard to climb" (Merry on 9.243).
- **9.246–247** — θρέψας "having curdled", ἀμησάμενος "having gathered it together" (Merry on 9.246–247); the lemma of the scholia has πονησάμενος μετέθηκεν (Merry's critical note).
- **9.250** — σπεῦσε πονησάμενος τὰ ἃ ἔργα, "having busied himself, he had despatched these works of his" (Merry on 9.250); the verse again 9.310, 9.343.
- **9.252–255** — = 3.71–74 (Merry on 9.252); the text prints ἦ τι where 3.72 has ἤ τι, and a comma after ληιστῆρες that 3.73 lacks: left as printed; γρ. μηχανόωνται for ἀλόωνται (Schol. H. P., in Merry's critical note).
- **9.256** — ἡμῖν … δεισάντων, the change from dative to genitive (Merry on 9.256, with 6.152).
- **9.261** — ἄλλην ὁδὸν ἄλλα κέλευθα, "a different route and a different journey" from the homeward one, with the scholia (Merry on 9.261).
- **9.264** — μέγιστον predicative: "his fame, far as the heaven covers, is the greatest" (Merry on 9.264).
- **9.267–268** — ξεινήιον πορεῖν, the most general expression for hospitality, δωτίνη a more specific gift (Merry on 9.267).
- **9.271** — Bekker relegated the verse to the foot of the page, and Köchly found it feeble after 270 and derived from 7.165 = 7.181 (Merry's critical note); αἰδοίοισιν: all guests are revered as guests (Merry on 9.271).
- **9.275** — the boast is inconsistent with what the Cyclopes acknowledge of Zeus (9.410–411) and with Polyphemus' claim to be Poseidon's son, but true to the braggart's nature (Merry on 9.275).
- **9.279–282** — εἴπʼ … ἔσχες "where you moored your ship" (Merry on 9.279); εἰδότα πολλά "with my knowledge of the world" (Merry on 9.281); ἄψορρον only "I answered him back", as at 9.501 (Merry on 9.282).
- **9.283** — νέα, Aristarchus' reading (Schol. H.), in synizesis; several manuscripts νῆα (Merry's critical note and on 9.283).
- **9.285** — ἐκ πόντου with ἄνεμος, "a wind from the sea", as πλημυρὶς ἐκ πόντοιο (9.486), or with the verb, "brought in from the open sea" on a lee shore (Merry on 9.285); the English takes the first.
- **9.288** — Euripides' Cyclops kills his victims in turn and cooks them (*Cyclops* 397 ff.): the cooking is a later refinement, not in the Homeric story (Merry on 9.288).
- **9.291–292** — διὰ … ταμὼν μελεϊστί, as *Iliad* 24.409 (Merry on 9.291); οὐδʼ ἀπέλειπεν parenthetical, "without leaving anything" (Merry on 9.292).
- **9.297** — ἐπὶ … πίνων "drinking on the top of it", as Euripides, *Cyclops* 327 (Merry on 9.297).
- **9.301–302** — ὅθι φρένες ἧπαρ ἔχουσι, "where the midriff enfolds the liver" (Merry on 9.301); χείρʼ is χειρί, and ἐπιμασσάμενος is better "feeling for" the mortal spot, passing his hand over the monster's body in the dark, than "fingering" his sword (Merry on 9.302).
- **9.311, 9.344** — δὴ αὖτε, with most modern editors since Bekker, for δʼ αὖτε; δόρπον at 344 (Schol. H.), others δεῖπνον (Merry's critical notes).
- **9.315** — πολλῇ ῥοίζῳ, "with frequent whistle" (Merry on 9.315).
- **9.319–322** — γάρ introduces the main sentence that begins with τοῦ (325) (Merry on 9.319); ἔκταμεν, for which "the more exact" wrote ἔκσπασε, according to Eustathius (Merry's critical note on 9.320); ὅσσον θʼ ἱστόν, a brachylogy (Merry on 9.322), the comparison that Milton imitated for Satan's spear (Merry on 9.322).
- **9.325–327** — ὄργυια "fathom", the stretch of the extended arms (Merry on 9.325); ἀποξῦναι, which Merry keeps against Buttmann's ἀποξῦσαι (his critical note and on 9.326); ἐθόωσα "I sharpened it", the men doing the rougher work and Odysseus shaping the point (Merry on 9.327).
- **9.329–330** — Aristophanes read ὑπὸ κόπρου (Merry's critical note); μεγάλʼ, used adverbially in Homer almost always of sound, Merry finds "very suspicious" here (Merry on 9.330).
- **9.331** — πεπαλάσθαι, the reading of Aristarchus and Herodian (Schol. H.; as at *Iliad* 7.171), against the manuscripts' πεπαλάχθαι (Merry's critical note and on 9.331).
- **9.334–335** — τοὺς ἄν κε: ἄν taken up by the pronoun, κε qualifying the verb (Merry on 9.334); ἐλέγμην "I counted myself in", as λέκτο δʼ ἀριθμόν (4.451); the variant ἐλέχθην (Merry on 9.335).
- **9.339** — ὀισάμενος "having some foreboding" (Merry on 9.339).
- **9.346** — κισσύβιον: in Euripides' *Cyclops* a σκύφος κισσοῦ (390; Merry cites it as 383), in Theocritus 1 a drinking cup carved with ivy; probably first a bowl of ivy wood, then any wooden cup or bowl (Merry on 9.346). Merry calls it a milking pail at 14.78, but there, as at 16.52, Eumaeus mixes wine in it.
- **9.349** — σοὶ … λοιβήν: flattery, wine offered as to a god, not as a gift to a stranger (Eustathius, in Merry on 9.349).
- **9.352** — ἀνθρώπων πολέων, "of the multitudes of men": however many they are, such a welcome will keep them all away (Merry on 9.352).
- **9.353** — ἥσατο, from ἥδομαι; αἰνῶς is not confined to expressions of horror (Merry on 9.353).
- **9.359** — ἀπορρώξ, "properly a part broken off from another", used in Homer of a stream that is a "branch" of the Styx (Merry on 9.359).
- **9.360** — ὣς φάτʼ, ἀτάρ οἱ, with most modern editors, for the common ὣς ἔφατʼ· αὐτάρ οἱ, since οἱ usually has the digamma (Merry's critical note).
- **9.362** — περὶ … ἤλυθεν, with Κύκλωπα its object and φρένας an accusative of nearer definition, as *Iliad* 10.139 (Merry on 9.362).
- **9.366** — ὄνομα with its final syllable lengthened at the caesura (Merry's critical note).
- **9.369** — πύματον … μετὰ οἷς ἑτάροισι, not "last, after his companions", but "last in the list of his companions" (Merry on 9.369).
- **9.372** — ἀποδοχμώσας "drooping", his head bent toward one shoulder (Merry on 9.372).
- **9.377** — ἀναδύη, against ἀναδύῃ and ἀναδυίη (Merry's critical note).
- **9.383** — the common reading ἀερθείς repeats the notion of ἐφύπερθεν; two important manuscripts have ἐρεισθείς, said to be Aristarchus' preference, "throwing my weight upon it", as a man presses upon the stock of a drill, which the text prints (Merry on 9.383).
- **9.384** — τρυπῷ: ὡς ὅτε with the optative is unparalleled, where ὡς εἰ would be expected; Draco read τρυπᾷ, and Ameis proposes the participle τρυπῶν (Merry on 9.384).
- **9.387–388** — ἑλόντες, Aristarchus' reading for ἔχοντες (Schol. H.), with δινέομεν (Merry on 9.387); θερμὸν ἐόντα of the stake, against Voss's conjecture ἰόντα, accepted by Nitzsch and Bekker (Merry on 9.388).
- **9.389** — ἀμφί adverbial with εὗσεν, "round about" (Merry on 9.389).
- **9.392–393** — ἰάχοντα agrees with πέλεκυν; φαρμάσσων "to temper it", the bar answering to the iron and the water to the eye (Merry on 9.392–393).
- **9.398** — ἀλύων: the υ long here, short elsewhere, so that some grammarians proposed ἀλυίων; χερσὶν ἀλύων of the wild movements of his hands (Merry on 9.398 and his critical note).
- **9.402–404** — τίπτε τόσον joined with ἀρημένος, ὧδε with ἐβόησας: "What is this great hurt of thine?" (Merry on 9.402); γρ. ὀρφναίην for ἀμβροσίην (Schol. H., in Merry's critical note on 9.404).
- **9.406** — κτείνει, with Bekker, against κτείνῃ (Merry's critical note).
- **9.408–414** — the name: the ambiguity of Οὖτις carries over into οὐδὲ βίηφιν (408); the Cyclopes answer with μή τις (410), "if no one", which Odysseus hears as μῆτις, "plan" (414), the play that the English keeps with "Nobody" and "nobody" (Merry on 9.408, 9.410).
- **9.410–411** — οἶον ἐόντα "defenceless", with no neighbors to help you; νοῦσον … Διὸς μεγάλου: they think he has gone hopelessly mad (Merry on 9.410–411).
- **9.415–416** — ὠδίνων ὀδύνῃσι, "writhing in agonies of pain", Merry's "in agonies of anguish", the play of sound intentional (Merry on 9.415); ψηλαφόων "groping" (Merry on 9.416).
- **9.419–420** — οὕτω … νήπιον joined, ἤλπετο with ἐνὶ φρεσί (Merry on 9.419); ὅπως … ἄριστα γένοιτο, "how all might be for the very best" (Merry on 9.420).
- **9.423** — ὥς τε περὶ ψυχῆς, "in a matter of life and death", as *Iliad* 22.161 (Merry on 9.423).
- **9.425–427** — ὄιες; Aristarchus ὄες (the scholia, in Merry's critical note); ἰοδνεφές, rightly μέλαν with Eustathius, not "white" as Ernesti would have it (Merry on 9.426); λύγοι, pliant twigs (Merry on 9.427).
- **9.429** — σύντρεις, on the analogy of ξυνεείκοσι (14.98); with some good manuscripts σὺν τρεῖς, a tmesis (Merry on 9.429).
- **9.433** — κατὰ … λαβών joined; ἐλυσθείς "curled up", the scholia's συστραφείς; the variant ἐλιχθείς (Merry on 9.433 and his critical note).
- **9.434–435** — χερσίν emphatic: the men were tied, but no one could tie Odysseus (Merry on 9.434); στρεφθείς commonly means "turning round": Merry takes it of Odysseus hanging downward, as he clings to the fig tree like a bat (12.432–433), rather than the usual "twisted in" of the hands, which the English no longer follows; νωλεμέως with ἐχόμην, "firmly" or "constantly" (Merry on 9.435).
- **9.443** — ὥς οἱ, οἱ an ethic dative (Merry on 9.443).
- **9.445** — λάχνῳ στεινόμενος καὶ ἐμοί, "cumbered by his wool and me", a comic touch; most manuscripts λαχμῷ (Merry on 9.445 and his critical note).
- **9.447** — κριὲ πέπον: the Iliad's word for a ram is κτίλος; Eustathius compares the heroes who speak to their horses (*Iliad* 8.185, 19.400, 23.402), and Merry finds that Cicero (*Tusculan Disputations* 5.115) misses the Cyclops' craving for sympathy (Merry on 9.447).
- **9.448, 9.450** — λελειμμένος … οἰῶν "distanced by the sheep" (Merry on 9.448); μακρὰ βιβάς, the proud bearing of a flock's leader (Merry on 9.450).
- **9.455–457** — ὀλέθρου in two manuscripts (455); ποτὲ φωνήεις in three (456); ἠλυσκάζει (Cod. Vrat.) for ἠλασκάζει (457) (Merry's critical notes); εἰ δὴ ὁμοφρονέοις, "couldest thou feel as I do" (Merry on 9.456).
- **9.465** — περιτροπέοντες: "oft turning round", to see if the Cyclops pursued, or, as Merry prefers, "driving in" into a compact flock to take them down to the ship (Merry on 9.465); the English, "often turning them about", keeps the transitive sense.
- **9.468–470** — ἀνὰ … νεῦον, the gesture of refusal, the words parenthetical and κλαίειν governed by οὐκ εἴων (Merry on 9.468–469); βαλόντας, a hasty action (Merry on 9.470).
- **9.474–475** — κερτομίοισι used as a noun (Merry on 9.474); "He was no weakling whose comrades thou wast minded to eat": the negative goes with ἀνάλκιδος (Merry on 9.475).
- **9.477** — καὶ λίην "to the very uttermost" (Merry on 9.477).
- **9.483** — Merry: the verse "cannot be read here", though appropriate at 540, since a stone that fell in front of the ship would not go near the steering oar, the stern facing the shore (9.489–490); Eustathius reports that it bears the asterisk with the obelus, and editors since Wolf bracket or delete it (Merry on 9.483 and his critical note). The text prints it, and the English keeps it, with τυτθόν taken with προπάροιθε, "by a little".
- **9.485–486** — παλιρρόθιον "the backwashing wave"; πλημυρίς not the flood tide but the swell from the fall of the stone, setting shoreward, as a floating stick is brought to the bank by stones thrown beyond it (Merry on 9.485–486).
- **9.488–491** — ὦσα παρέξ, "punting her along" (Merry on 9.488); ἐμβαλέειν κώπῃς, Latin "incumbere remis", or "dash into the sea with our oars" (Merry on 9.489); κρατί, for no one dared speak within range (Merry on 9.490); δὶς τόσσον: to be out of range should be to be out of hearing, an inconsistency (Merry on 9.491).
- **9.492** — καὶ τότε δή as printed; Merry's text has καὶ τότʼ ἐγώ, as 9.345 and 9.474; the English fits either.
- **9.496–497** — φάμεν … ὀλέσθαι, the aorist after a verb of expectation (Merry on 9.496); φθέγγεσθαι any cry, αὐδᾶν articulate speech (Merry on 9.497).
- **9.499** — ὀκρυόεντι in two manuscripts (Merry's critical note); μάρμαρος, from the root of μαρμαίρω, a white or glittering stone (Merry on 9.499).
- **9.504** — Aristotle quotes φάσθαι Ὀδυσσῆα πτολιπόρθιον as an instance of the anger that is not satisfied unless the wrongdoer knows by whose hand his punishment came (*Rhetoric* 1380b22–24; Merry on 9.504).
- **9.507** — παλαίφατα θέσφατα "the oracles spoken long ago", ἱκάνει "are come home to me"; compare Euripides, *Cyclops* 696–698 (Merry on 9.507).
- **9.509–510** — Telemus, son of Eurymus, appears again in Theocritus 6.23 and in Ovid, *Metamorphoses* 13.771 (Merry on 9.509); the seer was a public servant, δημιοεργός, beside the singer, the healer and the carpenter (17.383) (Merry on 9.510).
- **9.512** — χειρῶν ἐξ, "at the hands of Odysseus" (Merry on 9.512).
- **9.515–516** — ἄκικυς "feeble"; γρ. ἀεικής (Schol. M., Eustathius); Aristotle quotes the verse with ἀεικής (*Poetics* 1458b25–26); the second person ἀλάωσας … ἐδαμάσσαο is a variant of 516 (Merry on 9.515 and his critical notes).
- **9.518** — πομπήν τʼ ὀτρύνω together, "speed your escort", and δόμεναι κλυτὸν ἐννοσίγαιον epexegetic, "that the famous shaker of the earth may grant it" (Merry on 9.518).
- **9.523–525** — "Would that I were able, having robbed thee of life and being, to send thee within the house of Hades, as certainly as Poseidon himself shall never heal thine eye!" (Merry on 9.523, 9.525); εὖνις "bereft", as *Iliad* 22.44.
- **9.527** — χεῖρʼ, the dual χεῖρε (Merry on 9.527).
- **9.531** — since Wolf the verse has been bracketed by nearly all editors, as wanting in twelve manuscripts and in Eustathius' text (Merry's critical note); the text prints it, and the English keeps it.
- **9.535** — νηὸς ἐπʼ ἀλλοτρίης, the Phaeacian ship that brings Odysseus home (Merry on 9.535).
- **9.538** — after *Iliad* 7.269 (Merry on 9.538).
- **9.539–540** — the text prints κὰδʼ δʼ, with a stray elision mark (κὰδ δʼ at 9.482, and Merry's text), as at 10.172: a slip of the kind recorded at 3.400, left as printed. μετόπισθε, γρ. προπάροιθε; with a stop after τυτθόν, τυτθόν goes with μετόπισθε, "a little astern" (Merry on 9.539 and his critical note).
- **9.542** — χέρσος here is the shore of the island (9.116), at 9.486 the mainland of the Cyclopes (Merry on 9.542).
- **9.543–546** — the apodosis begins with νῆα μέν (546), as 12.1–5 (Merry on 9.543).
- **9.550** — ἀρνειόν, the ram by which he escaped (Merry on 9.550).
- **9.553** — ἐμπάζετο: Zeus refused to notice the offering, because the prayer of Polyphemus had been heard and would be answered (Merry on 9.553).
- **10.1–3** — πλωτῇ ἐνὶ νήσῳ "on a floating island": Aristarchus glossed πλωτή as φορητή, "floating", and Merry prefers it, citing Herodotus on Chemmis and Pindar on Delos; Crates took it as "accessible to ships", and Nitzsch leans that way; Merry also floats the notion of an iceberg (Merry on 10.3).
- **10.10** — περιστεναχίζεται αὐλῇ "resounds round about in the court": αὐλῇ a local dative, epexegetical of περί, the house sending out its sounds into the outer court (Merry on 10.10). Variants αὐλή and αὐδῇ (Cod. Vind. 133); Schäfer took αὐλῇ as "with fluting", and Düntzer and Nauck read περιστεναχίζετʼ ἀοιδῇ (Merry's critical note).
- **10.16** — Murray prints καὶ μὲν ἐγώ with the manuscripts; Merry prints αὐτὰρ ἐγώ, Bekker's and Nauck's reading from Cod. Vindob. 56 (his critical note).
- **10.17** — ὁδόν "to be on my way": "leave to depart" (Merry on 10.17).
- **10.19** — δῶκε δέ μʼ: all the manuscripts have δῶκε δέ μοι except Cod. Vrat. (δῶκέ μοι, followed by Wolf); ἐκδείρας tells how Aeolus came by the skin, not when he gave it (Merry on 10.19 and his critical note). ἀσκός is "skin", as 5.265 and 9.212.
- **10.40** — ἐκ Τροίης "from Troy": Aristarchus took Τροίης as an adjective with ληίδος, "Trojan spoil"; Merry takes it "from the land of Troy" (Merry on 10.40).
- **10.43–44** — Αἴολος opens 44 as the enjambed subject; the English turns the verb passive ("these things have been given him … by Aeolus") to keep the name on its line.
- **10.65** — Murray prints ὄφρʼ ἀφίκοιο; Merry prints ἂν ἵκηαι, Bekker ii ἂν ἵκοιο; Merry notes that a subjunctive after a past tense is exceptional in Homer (his critical note).
- **10.70** — Zenodotus read μαλακοῖσιν ἀμειβόμενος, which Schol. H. prefers: Odysseus does not reproach Aeolus but supplicates him (Merry's critical note).
- **10.81–86** — Λάμου αἰπὺ πτολίεθρον: Merry takes Lamus as a name of the king and Τηλέπυλος as "big-gated" (Merry on 10.81). Of the herdsmen whose paths are close together, the one driving in at nightfall is the shepherd with his flock, the one driving out the neatherd with his herd; the scholia explained the double wages by gadflies, Crates by the short nights of the north (citing Aratus), and Merry by an evening twilight that runs into the dawn (his long note, which begins under the heading 81).
- **10.87–88** — τετύχηκε "stands", of the cliff, like τέτυκται; διαμπερές shows that the wall of cliff ran unbroken from one side to the other (Merry on 10.88).
- **10.91** — ἔνθʼ οἵ γʼ … ἔχον "there they all steered", the apodosis to 87 (Merry on 10.91).
- **10.103** — λείη ὁδός, a road cleared for use; ἐκβάντες keeps its sense "disembarking" (Merry on 10.103). A variant ᾗ κεν ἄμαξαι (Merry's critical note).
- **10.106** — ἰφθίμῃ "strong", of a woman, as the Iliad (5.415, 19.116); the name Ἰφθίμη is another word (4.797).
- **10.108** — Ἀρτακίη, the spring of the Laestrygonians, named only here.
- **10.114** — Ἀντιφατῆα, as if from Ἀντιφατεύς: Merry reports that Eustathius pairs Γηρυόνης and Γηρυονεύς; the lexicon keeps it under Ἀντιφάτης, the king of 106.
- **10.124** — ἰχθῦς δʼ ὣς πείροντες "spearing them like fish": Aristophanes read εἴροντες, Apollonius the Sophist ἀσπαίροντας, and a variant has δαῖτα πένοντο (Merry's critical note, whose attributions are partly garbled in the scan).
- **10.130** — ἅλα, with Callistratus and Rhianus; most manuscripts and Apollonius have ἅμα, some ἄρα; Merry prefers ἅλα for the formula ἀναρρίπτειν ἅλα πηδῷ (7.328, 13.78) (Merry on 10.130 and his critical note).
- **10.135** — Αἰαίη: on the position of Circe's island, east or west, see Merry on 10.135 and on 12.3. A reading οὐδήεσσα was attributed to Aristotle at 136; Merry thinks Aristotle wrote αὐλήεσσα (his critical note on 10.136).
- **10.147** — ἐνοπή "voices" here, of the sounds of human life heard from afar (the Iliad has "cries" and once "sound").
- **10.152** — αἴθοπα καπνόν "the fiery smoke": "fire-lit … shining with a ruddy glow" (Merry on 10.152).
- **10.172** — the text prints κὰδʼ δʼ, with a stray elision mark (κὰδ δʼ at 9.482 and 10.163), as at 9.539: left as printed.
- **10.189** — the double opening (κέκλυτε … ὦ φίλοι) is "quite contrary to Homeric custom"; Callistratus thought the line was put in front by someone who did not know Homer's way of opening with γάρ (Merry on 10.189 and his critical note).
- **10.190** — ζόφος … ἠώς: Crates and Strabo took ζόφος as the north; Merry takes the pair together simply as "everywhere" (Merry on 10.190).
- **10.200** — a variant ἀνδροφόνοιο for ἀνδροφάγοιο (Merry's critical note). Κύκλωπός τε βίης "of the mighty … Cyclops": βίη with a person's genitive, the Iliad's periphrasis.
- **10.211** — περισκέπτῳ ἐνὶ χώρῳ "in a place with a view all round": open ground, a clearing in the wood or a place where valleys meet (Merry on 10.211); Döderlein "sheltered", from σκέπας (reported by Merry).
- **10.212–213** — μιν refers to Circe rather than to the house (Merry on 10.212); κατέθελξεν: beasts tamed by her drugs, or men transformed (the scholia, and Scaliger on Aeneid 7.19, as Merry reports them).
- **10.215–219** — the beasts stood on their hind legs to fawn on the men (Merry on 10.215); ἔδεισαν, probably Aristarchus' reading, is in Eustathius (Merry's critical note on 10.219).
- **10.220** — ἐν προθύροισι "in the doorway" is Aristarchus' reading, adopted since Wolf; nearly all the manuscripts have εἰνὶ θύρῃσι (Merry's critical note).
- **10.240** — Zenodotus read καὶ πόδας for καὶ δέμας; Merry would read νόος, since νόος is not found contracted in Homer (νοῦς only here) (Merry on 10.240 and his critical note).
- **10.242** — "Aristarchus did not know the line"; Callistratus wrote another verse in its place (Merry's critical note, partly garbled in the scan).
- **10.244** — Murray prints αἶψʼ with the manuscripts, "at once"; Voss conjectured ἂψ, "back", which Merry prints and most editors accept (his critical note).
- **10.253** — wanting in most manuscripts and in Eustathius; Apollonius Dyscolus inserts 212 in its place (Merry's critical note). Murray prints it (= 211).
- **10.262** — ἀμφὶ δὲ τόξα: βαλόμην is to be supplied again, the bow too slung about the shoulders (Merry on 10.262).
- **10.265** — omitted by most manuscripts and bracketed since Wolf (Merry's critical note); Murray prints it.
- **10.268** — σῶν: Aristarchus took it as σῶον, "safe"; Merry calls that a pedantic distinction and prefers "your" (Merry on 10.268).
- **10.289** — ὀλοφώια δήνεα "the baneful thoughts": δήνεα is "thoughts" at its only other use (Iliad 4.361 ἤπια δήνεα); "wiles" is kept for ὀλοφώια as a noun (4.410).
- **10.295** — the text prints ἐπαῖξαι; the aorist infinitive is ἐπαΐξαι (Iliad 5.263, 7.240, 12.308, 13.513, and Merry's text), as the verse needs: a slip, left as printed.
- **10.301** — θήῃ is Aristarchus' reading (Murray prints it), θείῃ the manuscripts'; ἀπογυμνωθέντα "when you are stripped" (Merry), "without his sword" (the scholion, as Merry reports it) (Merry on 10.301 and his critical note).
- **10.303** — φύσις, only here in Homer, the plant's outward form, like φυή (Merry on 10.303).
- **10.305** — μῶλυ: most likely the poet's own invention (Merry on 10.305), who reports Eustathius' allegory, Theophrastus (*Historia plantarum* 9.15), Sprengel's *Allium nigrum* and the scholia's wild rue.
- **10.306** — δύνανται (Schol. H.); the best manuscripts have ἴσασι, perhaps a reminiscence of 4.379 (Merry's critical note).
- **10.325** — εἰς, here as at 1.170, where the same verse later has εἶς (14.187, 15.264, 19.105, 24.298): left as printed.
- **10.326** — ὡς "how", which Merry keeps against Bekker's πῶς as "suggesting not only the fact but the manner" (Merry on 10.326).
- **10.328** — ὅς κε πίῃ … ἀμείψεται, a general relative clause: "whoever drinks them, once they have passed" (Merry on 10.328).
- **10.329** — the scholia's objection to the line; Merry finds the difficulty more apparent than real (his critical note).
- **10.348–351** — Nitzsch rejects 348 ff., Köchly agreeing; Merry suspects 350–351 (the present γίγνονται is awkward, and the maids read like Naiads, Dryads and Nereids); the scholia allegorize the four maids as the seasons (Merry on 10.350–351 and his critical note).
- **10.353** — λῖτα (λῖθʼ) "linen cloths" here, "a cloth" at 1.130; some take it as a neuter plural (Merry on 10.353).
- **10.365–366** — 365 belongs to the ἐπεί clause; the main clause begins at 366 (Merry on 10.366).
- **10.368–372** — = 4.52–56; Köchly calls them borrowed and inept in Circe's house; La Roche thinks them no older than the thirteenth century, unknown to Eustathius and the scholia (Merry's critical note).
- **10.374–378** — a variant στυγερόν for κρατερόν (376); ἀγχοῦ δʼ ἱσταμένη (377); ἕζεαι against ἕζεο (378) (Merry's critical notes). ἀλλοφρονέων "with my thoughts elsewhere" (Merry on 10.374).
- **10.380** — the Greek ends the clause with a high stop and ἦ … που, "surely, I suppose"; the English makes it a question.
- **10.385** — λύσασθαι, the middle, of Odysseus' own act; λῦσον (387) of Circe's (Merry on 10.385).
- **10.390** — ἐννεώροισιν "nine years old" (as 10.19 and Iliad 18.351), or "of nine seasons" (Merry on 10.390).
- **10.410–414** — the calves of the fields round the cows coming back from pasture; σκαίρουσιν is indicative after ὡς δʼ ὅτʼ ἄν, an anacoluthon, and Bekker conjectured σκαίρωσιν (Merry on 10.410–414).
- **10.416** — αὐτήν (Aristarchus) against αὐτῶν (all the manuscripts but Cod. Aug.) (Merry's critical note).
- **10.425** — the common reading ὀτρύνεσθʼ ἵνα … ἕπησθε (Merry's critical note).
- **10.429** — ἐρύκακε (La Roche, Nauck) (Merry's critical note).
- **10.430** — wanting in most manuscripts (Merry's critical note).
- **10.435** — ἔρξʼ "penned them in", from ἔργω (ἐέργω), as 14.411, so Merry, answering Eustathius' objection that the Cyclops did not set his captives to guard his cave but ate them; others take it from ἕρδω, "as the Cyclops did" (Merry on 10.435).
- **10.437** — καὶ κεῖνοι against κἀκεῖνοι (Merry's critical note).
- **10.440** — ἀποπλήξας against ἀποτμήξας (Merry's critical note).
- **10.453** — φράσσαντό τʼ ἐσάντα "knew each other face to face", against the manuscripts' πάντα (Merry on 10.453 and his critical note).
- **10.456** — omitted by Murray. Wolf bracketed it, because a plural imperative follows an address to Odysseus; Merry would keep it, citing ἡμῖν at 466 (his critical note).
- **10.457** — Aristophanes read στυγερὸν γόον (Merry's critical note).
- **10.463** — ἀσκελέες "withered", the physical sense (Merry on 10.463), against the adverb ἀσκελές "stubbornly" (1.68, 4.543).
- **10.465** — πέποσθε: Merry would adopt Aristarchus' πέπασθε, a perfect of πάσχω, not "acquired" in Eustathius' sense (Merry on 10.465).
- **10.469–470** — περὶ … ἔτραπον "returned in their course", beginning the circuit of a new year, unlike ἐπήλυθον ὧραι (2.107) (Merry on 10.469). 470 has been rejected since Wolf; Merry thinks it probably from Hesiod (*Theogony* 59) and takes the "long days" as the days of spring (Merry's critical note).
- **10.474** — Merry's text has οἶκον ἐυκτίμενον (6.315 "your well-built house"), where Murray prints οἶκον ἐς ὑψόροφον (7.77).
- **10.475–479, 482** — missing in three manuscripts and in Eustathius; Wolf and Bekker reject them, and Merry's text brackets them (his critical note).
- **10.483, 10.501** — the text prints ὢ Κίρκη; the address is ὦ Κίρκη at 337 and 383, and Merry prints Ὦ: a slip, left as printed. The promise of 483 is nowhere stated outright (Merry on 10.483).
- **10.491** — ἐπαινή "dread", as Iliad 9.457; its meaning, "praiseworthy" or "dread", is disputed (Merry on 10.491).
- **10.493** — μάντηος against μάντιος (Merry's critical note).
- **10.495** — Plato reads ταὶ δέ (Merry's critical note).
- **10.502** — εἰς Ἄϊδος "to the house of Hades": Ἀΐδης is always a person and not a place in Homer; Fäsi read a form in -δε (Merry's critical note, garbled in the scan).
- **10.509** — ἀκτή τε λάχεια "a shore of good soil" (λάχεια, 9.116); the scholion says it is not ἐλάχεια (Merry on 10.509 and his critical note).
- **10.516–537** — Circe's instructions for the pit, the libations, the vows and the sacrifice are carried out in the first person at 11.25–50, with the same English.
- **10.532** — κατάκειτʼ, which Merry calls "quite necessary"; κατέκειτʼ is carried over from 11.45 (his critical note).
- **10.545** — Aristarchus read ἐφύπερθε (Merry's critical note).
- **10.548** — ἀωτεῖτε "drowsing": from "breathe", or as "pluck the flower of sleep" (Merry on 10.548).
- **10.549** — two Vienna manuscripts read θέσφατα for πότνια, which Bekker would adopt (Merry's critical note).
- **10.552–560** — Elpenor, the youngest, sleeps on the roof for the cool air and falls when the noise wakes him (Merry on 10.558, on the ladder from the roof); καταντικρύ "straight down" (again only 11.64).
- **10.567** — κατʼ αὖθι (Merry's critical note).
- **11.8** — the text prints Κίρκη εὐπλόκαμος; the same verse reads ἐυπλόκαμος at 10.136 and 12.150, and εὐπλόκαμ- occurs nowhere else in the Odyssey's text: a slip, left as printed.
- **11.14** — Κιμμερίων: Aristarchus and Schol. H. read Κερβερίων, Crates Κερβερίων, others Κεμμερίους; Merry guesses χειμερίων, and holds that these are not the historical Cimmerians (Merry on 11.14 and his critical note).
- **11.16** — καταδέρκεται (Aristophanes, Aristarchus) against ἐπιδέρκεται in the manuscripts, as in Hesiod, *Theogony* 760 (Merry's critical note).
- **11.26** — χεόμην against Zenodotus' χεάμην (Merry's critical note).
- **11.34–36** — the English keeps ἐλλισάμην ("I had begged") on 35 and ἐς βόθρον ("over the pit") on 36, their Greek lines: "the heads bent down in the direction of the pit" (Merry on 11.35).
- **11.38–43** — athetized by Zenodotus and Aristophanes and rejected by Wolf; Bekker, Düntzer and Ameis bracket them, Nauck keeps them, and Bergk would move them after 632; Merry finds the objections "hardly conclusive" (his critical note).
- **11.51–83** — Elpenor: Merry calls the episode "probably a later addition", from the time when the νέκυια was recited as a separate rhapsody, its beginning and end shaped to tally with the scene of Anticleia (51 with 84; 48–50 with 81–82). Callistratus athetized 52–54 (Schol. H. Q.), and La Roche infers that Aristarchus rejected two of the lines (Merry on 11.51 and his critical notes).
- **11.58** — ἰών, against ἐών in all the manuscripts but two (Merry's critical note).
- **11.60, 11.92** — 60 is missing from most manuscripts, and 92 is found only in Eustathius, Ven. 456 and 613 and the Roman edition; both are bracketed since Wolf, and Murray prints both. Merry ties 92 to the question whether Teiresias needs the blood to recognize Odysseus (his critical notes).
- **11.74** — κακκῆαι (Aristarchus) against κακκεῖαι (Merry's critical note).
- **11.81–82** — στυγεροῖσιν "lamentable" for Merry, "hateful" here as everywhere; ἥμεθα may mean "we bided there", not necessarily seated (Merry on 11.81).
- **11.93** — Zenodotus read τίπτʼ αὔτως (Merry's critical note).
- **11.102** — ὅ is the conjunction, "in that he hath stored up wrath" (Merry on 11.102), hence "since he has laid up a grudge"; the repeat at 13.342 has the relative ὅς. Some read λήσειεν.
- **11.112** — τεκμαίρομαι keeps "ordain" (7.317, 10.563), but in the seer's mouth it is "foretell": "then I foretell destruction for you" (11.112 = 12.139). Merry has no note.
- **11.115–116** — Merry raises the chronology: the suitors' outrages had not yet begun when Teiresias spoke; Aristophanes read κατέδοιεν (his notes).
- **11.124** — φοινικοπαρῄους "with crimson prows" (again 23.271), as μιλτοπάρῃοι "with red-painted prows" (9.125; *Iliad* 2.637).
- **11.132** — the text prints ἱερᾶς, a genitive singular; the accusative plural is ἱερὰς, as in the same verse at 23.279 and at 3.144 and 4.478: a slip, left as printed.
- **11.134–137** — ἐξ ἁλός "away from the sea", as the tradition takes it; Merry argues that it means "out of the sea", the death that the Telegonia brought Odysseus from the sea (Ptolemy of Ascalon read ἔξαλος), and that the whole prophecy is a post-Homeric addition. ἀβληχρός "gentle"; Merry argues for "weak". At 136 Eustathius has ἀμφὶ δʼ ἑταῖροι (Merry on 11.134 and his critical notes).
- **11.136** — the text prints γήραι; the dative is γήραϊ everywhere else in the text (2.16, 15.357, 24.233; *Iliad* 3.150, 5.153), and the same verse at 23.283 prints γήραʼ: probably a slip, left as printed.
- **11.144** — πῶς introduces a direct question, "how may she recognise me?", not one dependent on εἰπέ (Merry on 11.144).
- **11.146, 11.149** — ἐπὶ φρεσί (La Roche) against ἐνὶ φρεσί; the text's ἐπιφθονέῃς against the vulgate ἐπιφθονέοις (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.157–159** — rejected by the Alexandrian critics; 161–162 athetized by Aristophanes (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.174, 11.178** — Aristophanes read οὕς at 174; a few manuscripts add a line after φυλάσσει at 178 (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.184–187** — Merry notes that Telemachus could have been at most fourteen when his mother died, and gives the variants τεμένεα and τεμένη, and κοτέουσι, ποθέουσι, κομέουσι for καλέουσι (his notes).
- **11.188–189** — εὐναί is predicative: "nor has he for bedding couches and cloaks and bright-coloured rugs" (Merry on 11.188): hence "Nor has he, to sleep in, / beds and cloaks and glossy blankets", δέμνια "beds" as everywhere.
- **11.196** — σὸν νόστον ποθέων, against most manuscripts' σὸν πότμον γοόων (Merry's critical note).
- **11.202–203** — "longing for thee, and [for] thy counsels and [for] thy tenderness robbed me of sweet life" (Merry on 11.202); Cod. Venet. reads κήδεα.
- **11.218, 11.221** — ὅτε τίς κε θάνῃσιν against the common ὅτε κέν τε θάνωσι; Crates read δάμναται ὡς (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.233, 11.236** — Aristarchus' breathing of ἠδέ at 233; some read ἀτασθάλου at 236 (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.245** — athetized; in all the manuscripts, and bracketed by every editor except La Roche (Merry's critical note).
- **11.248–249** — περιπλομένου ἐνιαυτοῦ (Gellius' Favorinus, as Merry reports); τέξεις (Aristarchus) against τέξεαι (Zenodotus); ἀνεμώλιοι for ἀποφώλιοι, and Autenrieth's "without offspring" (Merry's notes).
- **11.257, 11.264, 11.288** — πολύρηνος; οὔ μιν; οὐδʼ ἄρα against οὐδέ τι (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.269** — Merry holds that Megara's father Creon "is not identical with the brother-in-law of Oedipus" (on 11.269); the poem names neither his city nor his father, and Euripides gives Megara's father and Jocasta's brother the same name and father, Creon son of Menoeceus (*Heracles* 8–9; *Phoenician Women* 10–11).
- **11.272** — μέγα ἔργον "a great deed", as 3.261 and 4.663 even of crime; Merry renders "monstrous".
- **11.273–274** — υἷι against υἱεῖ, υἱῷ; ἄφαρ "at once", the oldest form of the story, in which the truth came out at once (Merry's notes).
- **11.275–276** — θεῶν διὰ βουλάς goes with ἄλγεα πάσχων, "suffering sorrows … through the deadly plans of the gods" (Merry on 11.275).
- **11.284–285** — Μινυείῳ against Μινυηίῳ; ἡ δέ against ἠδέ; Πύλου is a local genitive, "queen in Pylos" (Merry on 11.285).
- **11.289–290** — ὃς μὴ … ἐλάσειε "who did not drive", a condition, not ability (Merry on 11.290).
- **11.295** — περιτελλομένου ἔτεος "as the year circled round", after *Iliad* 8.404, so that ἐπήλυθον ὧραι can keep "came round" (2.107).
- **11.298–304** — Merry doubts the Leda passage; ζωούς sits uneasily with *Iliad* 3.243, where the earth already holds the Dioscuri (Nitzsch proposed ἀμφιζωούς); λελόγχασιν (Merry's notes).
- **11.315–316** — athetized "as impossible" (Aristarchus, according to Schol. V.); Merry thinks them an interpolation from a Gigantomachy.
- **11.321–325** — "probably a later interpolation" (Merry). At 324 Aristophanes read ἔσχε for ἔκτα, which Merry thinks probably original; Dia is the islet off Heracleion, and the μαρτυρίαι of Dionysus are variously explained (Merry on 11.324–325).
- **11.330** — φθεῖτʼ as a variant (Merry's critical note).
- **11.343, 11.407, 11.428** — missing in some or most manuscripts (343, 407), "not in many copies" (428, which Merry brackets) (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.344** — ἀπὸ δόξης: the scholion's "not at variance with our own ideas" against Eustathius' "not at variance with our expectation", which Merry takes (Merry on 11.344).
- **11.381** — ἀγορεῦσαι (most manuscripts, Merry) against Murray's ἀγορεύειν; τούτων goes with the comparative, "things more pitiable even than these" (Merry on 11.381).
- **11.385–386** — ἄλλην (Aristophanes); some copies read αἰνὴ Περσεφόνη (Eustathius), and Merry suspects ἁγνή (his critical notes).
- **11.393** — κῖκυς "vigor", only here in Homer (Merry on 11.393); the English keeps ἔμπεδος "steadfast".
- **11.399–403** — athetized by Aristophanes as carried over from 406–408 (Schol. H.); at 400 he read λευγαλέων; at 403 Merry prefers "fighting to take a city and its women" (*Iliad* 18.265) (Merry's notes).
- **11.416** — ἀντεβόλησα in some copies, which would change the person (Merry's critical note).
- **11.420** — θῦεν "seethed": the floor "swam, bubbled" with blood (Merry on 11.420).
- **11.423–424** — the English follows Merry's reading (after Königinhoff, through Seiler): "raising my hands I let them fall to the ground, dying with the sword through me", not the usual "I threw my hands about the sword"; ἀμφʼ ἐμοί is "close by me" or "as she clung about me" (Merry on 11.423).
- **11.435–440** — athetized by Aristophanes; the obelus runs to 442 in Cod. Marc.; Düntzer adds 441–443, La Roche 435–443 (Merry's critical note).
- **11.454–456** — the scholion "not in most copies, as conflicting" is referred to these lines by Dindorf, La Roche and Nauck, and Merry brackets them.
- **11.461** — athetized "for its silliness"; Aristarchus read οὐ γάρ που (Merry's critical note).
- **11.476, 11.478, 11.483** — ἀδρανέες as a variant at 476; Πηλέος and Πηλῆος at 478; μακάρτερος at 483 (Bekker ii, with Cod. Stutg., Eustathius and the Etymologicum Magnum) (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.489, 11.498, 11.502** — πάρουρος at 489; Zenodotus εἰ γὰρ ἐγών at 498; τέων (Aristarchus) at 502 (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.510–513** — Τροΐην; νεικέσκομεν; at 513 Merry and La Roche read μαρνοίμεθʼ Ἀχαιοί ("nearly all books"), against Murray's μαρναίμεθα χαλκῷ: an iterative optative, "whenever we fought" (Merry on 11.510).
- **11.519** — οἷον is exclamatory: "but what a man he slew with the bronze, the son of Telephus" (as 4.242).
- **11.521** — Κήτειοι: "the great ones", a Mysian people, or κήδειοι, χήτειοι (Merry on 11.521).
- **11.525** — Aristarchus "did not know" the line, which is in all the manuscripts; Merry thinks it borrowed from *Iliad* 5.751 and brackets it.
- **11.531, 11.539, 11.540** — ἐξέμεναι against ἐξίμεναι; σφοδελόν against ἀσφοδελόν; γηθοσύνῃ (Aristophanes, Herodian) for γηθοσύνη (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.547** — athetized by Aristarchus; Merry gives Nitzsch's reasons; on who judged the arms, the *Little Iliad*, the *Aethiopis*, Pindar and Sophocles as Merry reports them (Merry on 11.547).
- **11.548** — ἀέθλῳ is the prize, "for such a prize" (Merry on 11.548).
- **11.565–567** — suspected: ὅμως is found only here in the Odyssey (again *Iliad* 12.393). Schol. H. marks 568–627 as spurious; Merry thinks the insertion begins at 565, and takes the passage as later and Attic, with Plato and Onomacritus (his notes).
- **11.568** — Minos here gives judgment among the dead as he did in life, not the judge of the dead of later legend (Merry on 11.568).
- **11.579–580** — δέρτρον, the caul, or (in Apollonius' lexicon) the vulture's mouth; ἕλκησε and its variants (Merry on 11.579 and his critical note).
- **11.584** — στεῦτο "strained" (Aristarchus), against "stood" (Merry on 11.584).
- **11.586–588** — ἀναβροχέν and its variants; κατʼ ἄκρηθεν (Bekker) for κατὰ κρῆθεν (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.591** — ἐπὶ … μάσασθαι may be tmesis (ἐπιμαίομαι with the genitive τῶν, like 531) or ἐπὶ χερσί, "with his hands".
- **11.596–597** — Sisyphus is the subject of μέλλοι, "but ever as he was just going to topple it over the hill's crest" (Merry on 11.596), hence "whenever he was about / to heave it over the top". κραταιίς "its mighty weight", a force in the stone, or a power outside it (Crataeis, as 12.124); Aristarchus' adverb is rejected (Merry on 11.597).
- **11.598** — Aristotle quotes the line as an instance of lifeless things made alive, with ἐπὶ δάπεδόνδε for ἔπειτα πέδονδε (*Rhetoric* 1411b34–35): the Rhetoric's English renders his wording, "Back down to the ground rolled the shameless stone".
- **11.602–604** — athetized and attributed to Onomacritus; 604 is said to be copied from Hesiod, *Theogony* 952; Aristonicus objected to the division into εἴδωλον, σῶμα and ψυχή; Merry would cut only 602–604 (his notes).
- **11.606–608** — four participles and no finite verb (Fäsi takes ἐοικώς as ἐοικὼς ἦν): the English supplies "was" at 606, and restores αἰεί, "like one ever about to shoot" (Merry on 11.608).
- **11.611, 11.613** — χαλεποί as a variant of χαροποί (Merry's critical note); the wish at 613 may be praise of the maker or dread of the images, and Merry prefers dread.
- **11.614, 11.622, 11.624** — Kayser's variant at 614; Cod. Ven. 4 adds two mutilated lines after 622, restored by Cobet; τοῦδέ τι at 624 (Merry's critical notes).
- **11.618–619** — ἆ δείλʼ, ἦ … is a statement, not a question: "ah, wretch, truly you too drag out some evil fate, / such as I bore under the rays of the sun" (Merry on 11.618; Murray's period).
- **11.631** — Hereas, quoted by Plutarch, says that Peisistratus inserted the line; Merry brackets it.
- **11.632–634** — the Gorgon's head as "any horrible spectre" (Merry on 11.634).
- **11.640** — εἰρεσίῃ, dative in the text; Merry prefers the nominative εἰρεσίη (his critical note).
- **12.1–4** — ἀπὸ δʼ ἵκετο (2) is ἀφίκετο δέ, still part of the protasis; the apodosis begins at 5 (Merry on 12.2). Aeaea is placed at the sunrise, against the course of Book 10: the scholia (B) explain it as the passage out of Hades into the light (Voss, Nitzsch, Klausen), Merry as the meeting of the extreme East and West (Aratus, as on 10.86); the plural ἀντολαί reflects the sun's rising at shifting points through the year (Merry on 12.3–4).
- **12.16** — διείπομεν from the root σεπ, unlike δίειπε (*Iliad* 10.425) (Merry on 12.16); the verse as *Iliad* 11.706, "We were arranging all these things".
- **12.22** — δισθανέες "twice dying"; Schol. Q has the lemma δὶς θανέες (Apollonius; Eustathius gives the reading to Apollodorus); ὅτε passing into "while" (Merry on 12.22).
- **12.33–34** — the scene is probably a grotto, not Circe's house (cf. 143); προσέλεκτο "reclined at my feet", the scholia's προσεῖπε untenable (Merry on 12.34): the English "reclined near me".
- **12.38** — the god is "some kindly power", neither Poseidon nor Helios (Merry on 12.38).
- **12.39, 12.52, 12.167, 12.185** — the ancient etymologies of Σειρῆνες (Merry on 12.39); Homer's Sirens are two, as the dual Σειρήνοιιν (52, 167) and νωιτέρην (185) show: "the two Sirens", "the voice of us two".
- **12.45** — the construction of ὀστεόφιν: genitive with θίς or with πυθομένων, or an instrumental of material (Merry on 12.45).
- **12.47** — the text prints παρεξελάαν as one word, against παρὲξ ἐλάσωσιν (55), παρὲξ ἐλάαν (109) and Merry's text: perhaps a join in the digitization (Murray's page not checked); the lexicon takes ἐπὶ … ἀλεῖψαι as ἀλείφω with ἐπί, as 177 and 200.
- **12.47–50** — the infinitives are imperatival; Ameis takes αἴ κʼ ἐθέλῃσθα as a parenthesis and begins a new sentence at δησάντων, Merry the apodosis there (Merry on 12.47). Alcman's Κίρκη ἐπαλείψασα, quoted by Nitzsch, is the earliest extant allusion to a scene of the Odyssey (Merry on 12.47).
- **12.51** — ἱστοπέδη "the mast-step", virtually the μεσόδμη (Merry's Appendix I §12); πείρατα "rope-ends".
- **12.53–54** — athetized by Aristophanes (Schol. H.: why bind again one already bound?); 54 διδέντων is Aristarchus' reading, the manuscripts δεόντων; 163–164 are obelized too (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.61** — Πλαγκταί "the Wanderers": "wandering" against most moderns' "striking"; the scholia are divided; later the rocks were identified with the Symplegades and the Lipari islands, and Merry suggests icebergs or volcanic islets (Merry on 12.61).
- **12.62–65** — the doves that carry ambrosia, and the Pleiads (Merry on 12.62).
- **12.69–72** — probably a later addition (Merry on 12.70); La Roche reads κείνῃ at 69; Aristarchus is said to have written πασιμέλουσα as one word at 70 (Merry's critical notes); πᾶσι μέλουσα "in the thoughts of all", as 9.20.
- **12.73** — οἱ δέ, "Now, on the other side", the antithesis to ἔνθεν μέν (59) (Merry on 12.73).
- **12.74–75** — νεφέλη … κυανέη runs over the verse, as *Iliad* 20.417–418 "a cloud …, / a dark cloud"; τό (75) a vague neuter (Aristarchus, in Merry).
- **12.77** — ἐπιβαίη is Aristarchus' reading, against the vulgate καταβαίη: "set foot on the top" (Merry on 12.77).
- **12.85–88** — Σκύλλη "the render", the link with σκύλαξ probably later (Merry on 12.85); 86–88 athetized by the scholia (how could she have a puppy's voice?), which Merry defends as "an intentionally grotesque contrast between the vast size of Scylla, and the thin, shrill, cry she utters" (Merry on 12.86).
- **12.89** — ἄωροι: Merry lists seven views, "ugly"; "imperfect" (Ameis); "feeble" (scholia, Eustathius); "pendulous" (Schol. B, Lobeck, adopted by Curtius); "fore-feet" (Nitzsch); Aristarchus' ἀκώλους καὶ πλεκτανώδεις; Crates' δυσφυλάκτους; "(2) and (4) seem to have most to recommend them" (Merry on 12.89). The English "dangling" is the fourth.
- **12.101–102** — the punctuation is Nicanor's; πλησίον against πλησίοι (Merry's critical note).
- **12.104–105** — Charybdis is the whirlpool personified; ἀναρροιβδεῖ (104) and ἀναροιβδεῖ (105) are both required by the metre; τρίς against δίς in Polybius (Merry on 12.104–105).
