- **12.111** — two manuscripts have γρ. ἀτυζόμενος in the margin, which Buttmann thinks very good, since Odysseus interrupts Circe; Murray prints ἀμειβόμενος, as 10.336 (Merry's critical note).
- **12.112–352** — quotation marks: the digitized text gave no opening mark at 112, ” at 114, double marks in the speeches within the tale (116–141, 154–164, 184–191, 208–221, 271–276, 279–293, 297–302, 320–323, 340–351) and a stray “ at 352, a narrative line; corrected to single marks inside Odysseus' tale, as at 4.492–555.
- **12.116–117** — the manuscripts read δʼ αὖ at 116; Bekker θεοῖς ὑποείξεαι at 117 (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.124–126** — athetized; the scholia read κραταιίς as an adverb; the text has the nymph Crataeis, the mother of Scylla (Merry's critical note and note on 12.124).
- **12.127** — Thrinacia: the ancients read Trinacria; Merry derives the name from θρῖναξ "trident"; Düntzer "windswept" (Merry on 12.127).
- **12.129** — the seven herds of fifty, read by an allegory that Merry reports from Aristotle as the days of the year (Merry on 12.129).
- **12.131** — ἐπιποιμένες is Aristarchus' reading, against ἐπὶ ποιμένες (Merry's critical note, its heading misread "121" in the scan).
- **12.140–141** — omitted, or written in the margin, in most manuscripts; La Roche brackets them; Murray prints them (= 11.113–114) (Merry's critical note).
- **12.147** — wanting in Harl., Vind. 133 and other manuscripts and bracketed by almost all editors since Wolf: "The use of the oars was unnecessary when there was a favouring wind" (Merry's critical note). Murray prints it (= 9.104).
- **12.156–157** — ἵνα with κε is found only here; some manuscripts read ἠὲ θάνωμεν (Nauck); φύγοιμεν against φύγωμεν (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.168** — ἠδὲ γαλήνη (nearly all manuscripts, as 5.391) against Aristarchus' ἡ δὲ γαλήνη, with the aspirate (Schol. H.), which Merry prints (his critical note on 12.168). The English follows Murray, as 5.391–392.
- **12.175–176** — κέλετο is used of an external force only here: elsewhere κέλομαι is of persons, and the humour is intended (Merry on 12.175); Nitzsch would delete 176.
- **12.181** — ἀπῆμεν ὅσον (Murray, the manuscripts) against ἀπῆν ὅσσον (Merry, with Schol. M and Schol. B.H.), the form of the formula at 5.400 and 9.473: "ἀπῆμεν ὅσον may have been an early correction to suit διώκοντες" (Merry's critical note and note on 12.181). The English "we were" follows Murray.
- **12.184–191** — the Sirens' song; Cicero translates it, "O decus Argolicum, quin puppim flectis, Ulixes" (*De Finibus* 5.49), and νεῖται (188) is his "ad patrias … oras" (Merry on 12.184, who quotes the version).
- **12.185** — the text prints ὄπ ἀκούσῃς without the elision mark, the only such word in the book (ὄπʼ at 52, 160, 187): a slip, left as printed.
- **12.203** — τῶν is demonstrative; the oars hang in their loops (Merry on 12.203).
- **12.209** — ἕπει (Murray, La Roche, Hentze; the lemma of Schol. H. and of Vind. 133, explained as ἕπω, "for περιέπει") against ἔπι = ἔπεστι (Merry's text; Schol. V ἐπέρχεται) and Ahrens' ἔπει: the corrections "read like attempts to escape from the unprecedented lengthening of the final vowel in ἔπι" (Merry's critical note and note on 12.209). The English "besets" follows Murray.
- **12.212** — τῶνδε means the dangers ahead (Ameis), unlike *Aeneid* 1.203 (Merry on 12.212).
- **12.219–221** — the ship is the subject of ἐξορμήσασα; κεῖσε points toward the Wanderers; σκοπέλου (Murray; Schol. H. ἑνικῶς, Scylla's rock) against σκοπέλων (most editors since Wolf, with strong manuscript authority, and Merry: "the cliffs", both rocks) (Merry's critical note on 12.220 and note on 12.219). The English "the crag" follows Murray.
- **12.223** — Odysseus keeps Scylla secret because he foresees the loss of six men (Merry on 12.223).
- **12.226–227** — ἀλεγεινῆς: Merry "hard", because a fighting man chafes at passive endurance; λανθανόμην "I let myself forget" (Merry on 12.226–227). The English keeps the verb on 227: "I let the grievous command of Circe / slip from my mind".
- **12.234–245** — Merry's text differs from Murray's at 234 (ἡμεῖς δέ), 235 (ἔνθεν γάρ for ἔνθεν μέν), 242 (βεβρύχει) and 245 (κοίλης for γλαφυρῆς); 241 πέτρη (Bekker) or πέτρῃ; 243 κυανέη (Ameis, La Roche) against the vulgate κυανέῃ, which Merry rejects; 244 ἴδομεν, a few manuscripts ἴομεν (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.251–253** — ῥάβδος here a fishing rod, not a spear; κέρας, as Aristarchus explained it (Schol. Q.), a little horn tube on the line above the hook, as at *Iliad* 24.81 (the Iliad's note on 24.80–82 records Leaf's preference for Haskins' horn lure); εἴδατα (Aristarchus) against δείλατα (Callistratus) (Merry on 12.251 and his critical note on 252).
- **12.256–257** — κεκληγῶτας against κεκλήγοντας (La Roche); ἐν αἰνῇ δηιοτῆτι here a "death-struggle" (Merry on 12.257).
- **12.265–275** — Bekker μυκηθμόν (265); the manuscripts μάντιος (267); singular verbs at 268, 273 and 275 in the best manuscripts, of Circe, against the plural; φαεσιμβρότου at 269 (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.284–286** — Zenodotus οὕτως; ἀλάλησθαι (Herodian) against ἀλαλῆσθαι (Ptolemy of Ascalon) at 284; ἐκ νυκτῶν "out of the nights", Merry "by night" (on 12.286).
- **12.290** — the text lacks the question mark, though the question opens at 287 (πῇ) and Merry prints it: a slip, left as printed. The only place where the gods together are ἄνακτες, "sovereign gods", "probably no more than a graphic expression" for the wildness of the winds (Merry on 12.290); Zenodotus wrote φίλων ἀέκητι ἑταίρων (Schol. H., in Merry's critical note).
- **12.293** — ἐνήσομεν is "put out into the wide sea"; Merry rejects "launch" (on 12.293).
- **12.297** — βιάζετε μοῦνον is the reading of all the manuscripts but M; Zenodotus read βιάζεσθʼ οἷον ἐόντα, as 9.410; the active is admissible, and μοῦνον means "in a minority of one" (Merry's critical note and note on 12.297).
- **12.312–313** — τρίχα νυκτὸς ἔην, "the stars had southed" (Merry on 12.312; the verse again 14.483); ὦρσεν ἔπι (Aristarchus) against ὦρσε δʼ ἐπί; ζαῆν an Aeolic accusative, with Aristarchus' accent (Merry on 12.313).
- **12.319, 12.325** — γρ. πᾶσιν for μῦθον (319); ἄη against ἄε (325) (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.328–330** — λιλαιόμενοι βιότοιο, "vitae servandae studiosi" (Merry on 12.328); καὶ δή still within the ὅτε clause, δὴ ἄγρην with synizesis (Merry on 12.330).
- **12.332** — bracketed by Bekker and other modern editors as interpolated from 4.369, though Athenaeus (1.13) quotes it here: "It is not customary (though, as Eustath. says, it is possible) to catch (sea-) birds with a hook and line" (Merry's critical note on 12.332, its heading misread "222" in the scan).
- **12.347** — τεύξομεν a future with κε (Merry) or a short-vowel aorist subjunctive.
- **12.351, 12.356** — στρέγγεσθαι a variant of στρεύγεσθαι (351); περιστήσαντο, the manuscripts' reading as *Iliad* 2.410, against Bekker's conjecture περίστησάν τε (356) (Merry's critical notes).
- **12.369–370** — ἡδὺς ἀυτμή, against Kayser's θερμός (La Roche dissents); μέγʼ is Bekker's conjecture, printed by Murray, against the manuscripts' μετʼ, which Merry keeps: "among the immortals", a standing formula not to be pressed, since Odysseus was not in the gods' presence (Merry on 12.370 and his critical note). The English takes μέγʼ with γεγώνευν, "called out loudly", as μέγα δʼ εὔξατο (17.239).
- **12.374–390** — these lines carry the obelos in M and were probably rejected by Aristarchus and by Aristonicus: they contradict *Iliad* 3.277, where the Sun sees and hears all things and should have known for himself (Schol. B. Q.); Schol. H. on ὠκέα, "in many copies ὠκύς" (Merry's critical note). At 375 ἔκταμεν ἡμεῖς is the reading of Aristarchus' copies, the manuscripts having ἔκταν ἑταῖροι "from a wish to exonerate Odysseus from the blame" (Merry's critical note and note on 12.375).
- **12.383, 12.388** — φαείνω a subjunctive (Merry) or a present indicative; τυτθά, Zenodotus τριχθά (Merry's critical note on 388).
- **12.389–390** — a scholion says that Odysseus lies here, since Calypso had never yet seen Hermes; Merry answers that the falsehood "rather rests with Calypso" and calls Eustathius' defence "very suspicious" (Merry's critical note and note on 12.389).
- **12.394** — the text prints προύφαινον without the coronis (προὔφαινον): a slip, left as printed, as at 3.8 and 9.143–145.
- **12.403** — the half-verse of 201, "But when we were leaving the island" in both, and with Κρήτην at 14.301.
- **12.420–423** — τὴν δὲ ψιλήν, the keel left bare, "the sides of the ship having been torn away from it" (Merry's Appendix I §2); Zenodotus ἔαξε for ἄραξε (422); ἐπίτονος opens 423 with a short syllable, a στίχος ἀκέφαλος; Athenaeus (14.632E) quotes the line with τετάνυστο for βέβλητο; τετευχώς is the only perfect active of τεύχω in a passive sense; the backstay ran from masthead to stern (Hesychius), while Suidas took ἐπίτονος for the rope ring holding the yard to the mast (Merry on 12.423; Appendix I §15).
- **12.428** — θάλασσαν for Χάρυβδιν in Apollonius the Sophist, which Nauck thinks right, though Plato (*Epistle* 7) and Plutarch (*Dion*) have Χάρυβδιν; ὄφρα depends on ἦλθε, and ἀναμετρήσαιμι Χάρυβδιν = ἀναμετρήσας πέλαγος Χάρυβδιν ἱκοίμην (3.179) (Merry's critical note and note on 12.428).
- **12.430–441** — doubted "in many copies" (Schol. H. Q.): Odysseus reaches Charybdis at sunrise but the timbers come up only at evening; ἦμος δέ always opens a verse; ἀνέστη is an aorist of custom; 435 εἶχον, ἦσαν or ἔσσαν; 441 Aristarchus τάδε for τά γε (Merry's critical note, its heading misread "430..441" in the scan, and on 12.439–441).
- **12.445–446** — rejected by the scholia (Schol. H. Q.), since Scylla cannot leave her cave; Merry brackets them and suggests ἐμέ as the subject of εἰσιδέειν, "suffered him not to set eyes on Scylla again"; Ameis thinks them compiled from 223 and other lines (Merry's critical note and note on 12.445–446). The English follows the scholia's construal, with Scylla seeing him.
- **13.5–6** — The words come from *Iliad* 1.59–60, "but with a change of application which has made them somewhat obscure": in the Iliad ἂψ ἀπονοστήσειν is returning disappointed to Greece, which goes with παλιμπλαγχθέντας; a return home is no disappointment (Monro on 13.5–6). The English keeps the Iliad's "be beaten back" for the participle and reads the negative with the whole: "you will not be beaten back / and sail for home again". τῶ is adverbial, "so", distinct from the dative τῷ (Monro on 13.5); παλιμπλαγχθέντα is Aristarchus' reading and the vulgate's, πάλιν πλαγχθέντα in other copies (Monro's critical note).
- **13.9** — ἀκουάζεσθε "please yourselves with listening": verbs of this formation have an ampliative and often unfavorable meaning (μιμνάζω, ἡγηλάζω, ἀεκάζομαι) (Monro on 13.9); the English "listen to the singer", as 9.7.
- **13.45** — ἀρετήν "good", not restricted, as in later Greek, to "merit" or "excellence" (Monro on 13.45, comparing 14.402 and ἀρετάω "prosper", 19.114): "may the gods grant you good / of every kind".
- **13.81–84** — ἡ δʼ … is taken up again at 84 ὣς ἄρα τῆς …: the anacoluthon is softened by the nominative ἵπποι of the simile; τετράορος "four yoked together", contracted from τετρα-ήορος (Monro on 13.81). For πρύμνη (84) some read πρῴρη as a truer picture, but Monro asks how πρύμνη could then have entered the text (Monro on 13.84). The English keeps "her stern lifted".
- **13.86** — ἴρηξ κίρκος: in such pairs the second term is usually the specific one, as βοῦς ταῦρος, σῦς κάπρος (Monro on 13.86): "a hawk, / a falcon".
- **13.89** — θεοῖς ἐναλίγκια μήδεʼ, a brachylogy, "like the (counsels of the) gods", as *Iliad* 17.51 κόμαι Χαρίτεσσιν ὁμοῖαι (Monro on 13.89): "whose counsels were like the gods'".
- **13.91** — πτολέμους and κύματα carry on the construction of πολλὰ πάθʼ ἄλγεα, while πείρων "passing through" belongs only to κύματα, so that it is not properly a zeugma (Monro on 13.91). The verse is 8.183, and its English stands.
- **13.93** — ὑπερέσχε is intransitive, "rises": the morning star, at the dawn of the thirty-fifth day (Monro on 13.93).
- **13.98–99** — ποτιπεπτηυῖαι, literally "crouching towards", sinking down in front of the harbor and so closing it in; ὑποπεπτηυῖαι in some copies (Monro on 13.98 and his critical note); ἀνέμων κῦμα "the waves raised by the winds" (Monro on 13.99, comparing *Iliad* 2.396).
- **13.111** — θεώτεραι, not "more divine" but "divine", in contrast to the human door (Monro on 13.111): "for gods".
- **13.114** — ὅσον τʼ ἐπὶ ἥμισυ, "to half its length", as *Iliad* 10.351 ὅσον τʼ ἐπὶ οὖρα πέλονται (Monro on 13.114).
- **13.144** — the text prints a period after τίει, which cuts the εἰ-clause of 143 from its apodosis (σοὶ δʼ ἐστί …); Monro's text has a comma, and the English reads 143–144 as one sentence. Left as printed (Murray's page not checked).
- **13.158** — the mountain: for μέγα δέ Aristophanes read μηδέ, no doubt because of the Phaeacians' prayers (183), "but these need not affect what Zeus says now" (Monro on 13.158). The English follows the text: "and to cover their city round about with a great mountain".
- **13.162** — διωκομένη "coursing along", as the phrase ἅρμα διώκειν (Monro on 13.162).
- **13.173** — ἀγάσασθαι "had been surprised", i.e. offended, jealous (Monro on 13.173); the English keeps 8.565's "begrudged".
- **13.175** — the text prints a comma after περικαλλέα, which divides the adjective from νῆα (none at 149, nor at 8.567 ἐυεργέα νῆα, nor in Monro's text): a slip, left as printed.
- **13.189–191** — ἤδη δὴν ἀπεών belongs with εὕδων ἐν γαίῃ πατρωΐῃ, while περὶ γάρ … gives the reason of οὐδέ μιν ἔγνω (Monro on 13.189); αὐτόν "himself", his person (Monro on 13.190). The mist was not to make Odysseus invisible: Athena wished to prepare him by consultation and by changing his appearance, as at 429–438 (Monro on 13.191). ἄγνωστον "unknown", as 2.175 and 13.397.
- **13.194** — the manuscripts are divided between φαίνετο and φαινέσκετο; the latter makes ἀλλοειδέα three long syllables, which Buttmann rejected, and Monro prints ἀλλοειδέʼ ἐφαίνετο (Monro on 13.194). After 197 some witnesses add 13.226 (Monro's critical note). The English follows the text.
- **13.198** — the text prints ἄρ before a vowel without its elision mark (the same verse has ἄρʼ ἔπειτα at *Iliad* 15.397): a slip, left as printed, as at 8.450.
- **13.241** — μετόπισθε, westwards, the west being the end, as the east is the beginning, of the day (Monro on 13.241): "those behind, toward the misty darkness".
- **13.252** — a stray comma in κούρη Διός, αἰγιόχοιο splits the genitive formula (no comma at 13.371, 3.42, 4.752): a slip, left as printed.
- **13.265** — θεράπευον "served as θεράπων", and the negative covers χαριζόμενος too (Monro on 13.265): "because I would not court his father's favor by serving as his attendant".
- **13.295** — πεδόθεν "from the ground up", as *funditus*, but probably with the notion of constancy, as in ἔμπεδος; παιδόθεν, the vulgate's reading, is itacism (Monro on 13.295).
- **13.309** — οὕνεκα "that", a meaning confined in Homer to the Odyssey (Monro on 13.309).
- **13.320–323** — "generally condemned as spurious": the first two with little doubt, since the clause πρίν γʼ ὅτε … does not fit (Monro on 13.320–323). The English renders the text as printed.
- **13.324** — the digitized text joined γουνάζομαι — οὐ without spaces around the dash; the spacing is restored (as 14.274, 14.282 and 16.377), a correction of spacing only.
- **13.336** — πειρήσεαι "observe for yourself", not trusting to report (Monro on 13.336).
- **13.347–348** — omitted in some manuscripts; Monro takes them as repeated from 103–104, since the cave is first mentioned at 349, with the deictic τοῦτο (Monro on 13.347–349). The English renders them as printed.
- **13.364** — the text prints θείμεν, no form (the optative is θεῖμεν, which would not scan); Monro and the Iliad have θείομεν, the short-vowel subjunctive: a slip, left as printed. ἵνα "where", "so that there"; σόος is probably post-Homeric, for σάος (Monro on 13.364).
- **13.377** — κοιρανέουσι, ironically, "are lording it" (Monro on 13.377).
- **13.388** — κρήδεμνα "the diadem of towers", from *Iliad* 16.100 ὄφρʼ οἷοι Τροίης ἱερὰ κρήδεμνα λύωμεν, a refinement on calling the battlements of a town its "head" (Monro on 13.388): "the shining veil of Troy", as the Iliad.
- **13.405** — 405 = 15.39; τοι goes with ἤπια οἶδε, "he is of friendly mind to you"; ὁμῶς is disputed: Ameis refers it to ὑῶν ἐπίουρος, Eumaeus as devoted to Odysseus as to his swine (Monro on 13.405). The English: "and is kind to you alike".
- **13.430** — Murray prints κάρψεν μέν; the manuscripts have μέν οἱ, for which Bekker read κάρψεν μέν and Hermann κάρψεν οἱ (Monro's critical note on 13.430).
- **14.10** — ῥυτοῖσιν: Monro (on 14.10) takes it as "dug", "quarried", not "drawn" (after Schulze); the English keeps "hauled", the rendering of 6.267, where Merry explains it (the note on 6.267).
- **14.12** — τὸ μέλαν, "the dark part", the heart of the oak; ἀμφικεάσσας, "splitting off all round", so as to separate the softer wood (Monro on 14.12).
- **14.33** — μετασπών "taking in hand", from a root sep, a different word from ἕπομαι (Monro on 14.33); the English keeps "following".
- **14.58** — Eumaeus means simply ὀλίγη, but uses the set phrase ὀλίγη τε φίλη τε as a euphemism (Monro on 14.58): "a gift is small but dear".
- **14.89–90** — "Join ἴσασι κείνου λυγρὸν ὄλεθρον" (Monro on 14.89): the English sets off the clause about the god, "they have heard some voice of a god", and reads "of that man's grim destruction" with "know something". Monro prints δέ τοι (M) for δέ τι (vulgate) (his critical note on 14.89); Murray prints καί τι.
- **14.145–147** — "The meaning seems to be that the swineherd does not name Odysseus in speaking of him, but uses the word ἠθεῖος, which is properly a form of respectful address. Elsewhere we only find the voc. ἠθεῖε or (once) ἠθείη κεφαλή" (Monro on 14.145–147): "Him, stranger, even though he is not here, I feel shame / to name; … / but I call him dear master, even though he is far away", where the vocative is "dear brother" in the Iliad (*Iliad* 6.518, 10.37).
- **14.158–164** — the oath (158–162 = 19.303–307): Monro brackets 161–162, or, with Kirchhoff, holds that the seven lines of the oath belong originally to Book 19 and have been brought in here wrongly (Monro on 14.158–164); he takes λυκάβας as a day of twenty-four hours, where the English has "year".
- **14.209–211** — κλῆρος in its two senses: ἐπὶ κλήρους ἐβάλοντο "cast lots for it" (209), "'Cast lots for it,' i.e. divided it and assigned the shares by lot. The order ἐδάσαντο καὶ . . ἐβάλοντο marks the division as the main thing, of which casting lots was a detail: cp. 13.274" (Monro on 14.209): "and his livelihood was divided / by his high-hearted sons, who cast lots for it"; and the share itself, "allotment", as κλῆρος at 14.64 "a house and an allotment and a much-wooed wife" (and *Iliad* 15.498 "and his house and his allotment unharmed"), with πολυκλήρων ἀνθρώπων "from people of many allotments" (211), where "The plur. stands for the family or tribe of the wife: cp. Il. 3.49" (Monro on 14.211). οἰκία at 210 is "sc. μάλα παῦρα, repeated from the preceding clause" (Monro on 14.210): "but to me they gave very little, and allotted me a house".
- **14.228** — "This gnomic line is doubtless of later date": the form ἔργοις is doubly post-Homeric (Monro on 14.228), and he brackets it. The English renders it.
- **14.253** — Βορέῃ is an instrumental, with a partly comitative force (Monro on 14.253): "with a fresh and fair North Wind".
- **14.279, 14.422** — the text prints ὁδʼ and ὅγʼ without the space (ὁ δʼ, ὅ γʼ): the only such forms in the digitized Iliad and Odyssey; slips, left as printed.
- **14.311** — ἀμαιμάκετον "of vast length", formed by intensive reduplication from the root μακ- (Monro on 14.311), rather than "irresistible": "the mast of vast length".
- **14.318** — αἶθρος, a word only found here, seems to mean exposure to the air (Monro on 14.318); αἴθρῳ is the reading of Zenodotus, Aristophanes and Aristarchus, λύθρῳ of other copies (his critical note). The English: "overcome by cold and weariness".
- **14.350** — "ἐφόλκαιον is probably a 'lading plank,' to take in (ἐφέλκεσθαι) cargo" (Monro on 14.350, with a report signed "J. L. M." of such planks on Mediterranean boats, "used in shoal water as a gangway"): "I slid down the polished lading plank and brought to the sea / my chest". The next note in Monro, whose lemma the OCR loses, reads "sc. without a splash. His head is disguised (l. 349), so as to look like flotsam as he swims away": "and covering my head with the rag" (349).
- **14.191, 14.213, 14.370, 14.412** — the text lacks a stop at the end of 191 (= 390) and 370, before ἀλλʼ ἔμπης at 213, and at the end of the clause at 412: slips, left as printed.
- **14.274, 14.282** — the digitized text joined the words around the dash; the spacing is restored, a correction of spacing only.
- **14.441** — ὅττι τε occurs nowhere else in Homer; Monro prints ὅττι με (and 15.342 has ὅττι μʼ): possibly a slip, left as printed.
- **14.508** — αἶνος "tale" here, of the story Odysseus has just told, against the Iliad's "praise" (*Iliad* 23.652, 23.795).
- **14.515–517** — omitted in several manuscripts (G F P H M X and others), with a reference to 15.337–339, where the same lines stand (Monro's critical note on 14.515–517). The English renders them.
- **14.425, 14.532** — κείων at 425 is "splitting" (κείω, κεάζω), at 532 "to lie down" (a future participle of κεῖμαι, as Monro takes it): a pair of homographs.
- **15.1–7** — Monro (on 15.1) counts the book from the 36th day and notes that the chronology is not quite clear (see 17.515); the "contradiction" of 4–7, Peisistratus asleep and Telemachus awake in the same bed-chamber, is the manner of epic narration (Monro on 15.4–7).
- **15.19, 15.24–26, 15.91** — Aristophanes obelized 19 ("ἐπὶ σμικρολογίᾳ"), which Monro defends; 24–26 were rejected by some ancient critics as leading to nothing in the story; 91 was "anciently rejected", like 19 (Monro on these lines). The English renders them all. ἐπιτρέψειας (24) is an optative as a polite imperative, "I would have you entrust" (Monro on 15.24): "you should go yourself and entrust".
- **15.23** — the text prints τεθνηκότος, the only such form in the Homeric texts here; Monro prints τεθνηότος, as at 1.289 and 2.220: probably a variant reading, left as printed.
- **15.33–34** — the islands are Dulichium, Same and Zacynthus, to be kept far off by sailing wide of them; ὁμῶς "as well", by night as by day (Monro on 15.33–34).
- **15.45** — Aristarchus and modern critics object to λὰξ ποδὶ κινήσας as absurd when the man to be woken was in bed; but "we do not know the form of the bed", and the point is as undetermined in the parallel, *Iliad* 10.158 (Monro on 15.45).
- **15.63, 15.74, 15.139** — omitted in many manuscripts and bracketed by Monro; of 74, "the gnomic line … is clearly an addition, as such a line is apt to be" (Monro on 15.70). Murray prints all three, and the English renders them.
- **15.70–74** — "Too much kindness, shown in keeping a guest against his will, is as bad as sending him away before he wishes to go" (Monro on 15.70): φιλέω and ἐχθαίρω keep "love" and "hate" for the antithesis.
- **15.78–85** — obelized by Aristarchus. τραφθῆναι "turn aside", a tour through Hellas and the heart of Argos; the apodosis of εἰ δʼ ἐθέλεις (80) is understood, "if you wish to turn … (do so)", and does not begin at ὄφρα (Ameis) or ὑποζεύξω (Bury); Monro rejects Bury's view of Hellas as Achaia and of the journey as a détour (Monro on 15.80–81). The English: "And if you wish to turn aside …, / so that I myself may go with you, and I will yoke …".
- **15.113–119** (= 4.613–619) — omitted in P and H; Monro brackets them; Murray prints them.
- **15.128** — Murray prints κείσθω; Aristarchus read κεῖσθαι, which Monro prints (Monro's critical note on 15.128).
- **15.156–158** — αἲ γὰρ ἐγὼν ὣς … ὡς: "would that I may tell it on returning to Ithaca and finding Ulysses in the house, even as I go on my way after receiving all hospitality from you", the correlatives of a wish and "the only interpretation consistent with the idiom" (Monro on 15.156–158), against those who take ὡς as "how". The English follows Monro: "would that, / having come home …, / I might tell him so, as surely as I come from you …".
- **15.160, 15.164** — δεξιός "to the right", equivalent to ἐπιδέξια, "from left to right": the eagle (and at 164 the bird that "sped to the right in front of the chariot") crosses the path in the lucky direction (Monro on 15.160).
- **15.175** — γενεή τε τόκος τε is a hendiadys (Monro on 15.175): "where he was born and bred".
- **15.192** — the text prints οὐκ ἄκοντε, contracted, where the same verse has ἀέκοντε at 3.484, 3.494 and *Iliad* 5.366, 8.45, and Monro prints ἀέκοντε: probably a slip (the meter allows either), left as printed; rendered as the formula, "not unwilling".
- **15.199** — Pylos lay some way inland (Strabo), so that the town is reached before the shore (Monro on 15.199).
- **15.212** — "such is his overweening spirit" is half-serious (Monro on 15.212).
- **15.213** — εἴσεται, the future of εἶμι, "will make his way" (Monro on 15.213), not of οἶδα: "he will come here himself".
- **15.218** — τὰ τεύχεα are "the arms", as at 16.326 (Monro compares 16.474); the word "does not include the rigging (ὅπλα), which is taken in hand later" (287–291) (Monro on 15.218).
- **15.225–255** — the line of Melampus (Monro on 15.225–247; compare 11.281–297): ἔκγονος (225) "great-grandson"; at 246 the "threshold" of old age is not its beginning: he never fairly set foot in old age (*Iliad* 22.60); some of the ancients (Plutarch, *Consolation to Apollonius* 17) saw in the verse a proof that those whom the gods love die young, "but no such 'pathetic fallacy' was in the poet's mind" (Monro on 15.246); 251 = *Iliad* 20.235, obelized by Aristarchus; τοῦ (256) is Polypheides (Monro on these lines).
- **15.268** — εἴ ποτʼ ἔην, as *Iliad* 3.180 and 24.426, is an assurance, not a doubt (Monro on 15.268): "if that was ever so". Murray prints λυγρῷ ὀλέθρῳ (the manuscripts), Aristarchus λυγρὸν ὄλεθρον.
- **15.275** — τῶν goes with the ὑπ- of ὑπαλευάμενος, "under stress of" (Monro on 15.275): "Avoiding death and black doom at their hands".
- **15.295–300** — 295 (Κρουνούς τε … Χαλκίδα) is in no manuscript; it comes from Strabo and from the *Hymn to Apollo* (425–427). Monro prints it after 297 and takes the whole passage as originally the Odyssey's; Murray prints Strabo's order, which the English follows. Φεάς (Aristarchus, Strabo) against the vulgate Φεράς, which Monro thinks was suggested by the Achaean town Φαραί. θοῇσιν (299) is "best left unexplained": Νῆσοι Θοαί as a name rests only on Strabo's identification with some of the Echinades; "the islands" are Dulichium, Same and Zacynthus (Monro on 15.295–300). Rendered "the pointed islands", lowercase.
- **15.301–495** — the scene in the hut runs through the night while Telemachus sails (Monro on 15.301–495).
- **15.317, 15.324** — 317 is a second apodosis to εἴ μοι δεῖπνον: "(if they would), I would readily serve right well" (Monro on 15.317); in τοῖς ἀγαθοῖσι (324) the article is used as with comparatives (Monro on 15.324): "the lesser … their betters".
- **15.322** — δανά "dry" (F, P, H, Eustathius) against πολλά; "no one would contend with me in respect of service, in piling up a fire" (Monro on 15.322).
- **15.327–328** — the text ends 327 with a period, though 328 (εἰ δὴ μνηστήρων ἐθέλεις …) is its condition, and Monro prints a comma: a slip, left as printed. αὐτόθι "on the spot, without escape" (Monro on 15.327).
- **15.344** — οὐλόμενος always means "miserable, accursed" and is intransitive (after Schulze); here mock-heroic (Monro on 15.344). The English keeps "the ruinous belly", as οὐλομένην "ruinous" (*Iliad* 1.2).
- **15.357** — ὠμῷ γήραϊ "old age before its time" (Monro: "premature").
- **15.367** — Σάμηνδʼ ἔδοσαν is a pregnant construction (Monro on 15.367): given in marriage and sent to Same.
- **15.370** — κηρόθι μᾶλλον "right well", not "more than before" (against Ameis) (Monro on 15.370): rendered "dearly in her heart", an exception to "all the more … in his heart".
- **15.373** — τῶν is a partitive plural; the αἰδοῖοι are those one must respect, family, guests and suppliants (Monro on 15.373).
- **15.403–404** — Syrie and Ortygia are mythical islands, not Syra and Delos; τροπαὶ ἠελίοιο, "the turnings of the sun", is disputed (the solstices, or the west where the sun turns) (Monro on 15.403–404, as far as his note goes; the lexicon's note gives both readings).
- **15.407** — ἄλλη νοῦσος, "disease as well", by the familiar idiom: famine is not counted a disease (Monro on 15.407): "nor does any / hateful sickness besides fall".
- **15.417, 15.424** — πατρός goes with οἴκῳ (417; against Ameis); at 424 πατρός is "my father" (Pierron, Hayman), though the same verse is "her father" at 10.111 (Monro on these lines).
- **15.426** — ῥυδόν "in a stream" (Monro): "whose wealth came in a flood"; Ἀρύβας is perhaps Hasdrubal (Monro on 15.426).
- **15.428** — the text prints πέρασαν δέ τε; Monro prints δέ με and reports no variant, and epic τε does not suit a single past event: probably a slip, left as printed (the English has "me").
- **15.433, 15.445** — traders' idiom: ἀφνειοὶ καλέονται "are reputed rich"; ὦνον ὁδαίων "the buying of your cargo" (Monro on these lines).
- **15.451** — τοῖον after an adjective is adjectival; the meaning is "not, as usually given, 'so very —', but 'quite', 'just'" (Monro on 15.451): "a crafty one indeed".
- **15.453** — Murray and the manuscripts print περάσητε; Monro prints περάσαιτε, calling the subjunctive "post-Homeric both in form and in syntax" (his note on 15.453).
- **15.460** — μετά "at intervals", ἠλέκτροισιν "pieces of amber" (Monro on 15.460): "strung here and there with amber".
- **15.466–468** — the tables and cups had been carried into the πρόδομος; the δαιτυμόνες are the counsellors; θῶκος is the sitting-place or tribunal, and δήμοιο φῆμις the place where the people talk (Monro on these lines).
- **15.470** — ἀεσιφροσύνῃσι "must here mean 'in childish thoughtlessness'"; the derivation is obscure (Monro on 15.470).
- **15.479** — ἐνδούπησε πεσοῦσα adapts, perhaps parodies, the Iliad's δούπησεν δὲ πεσών (Monro on 15.479).
- **15.488** — καί goes with ἐσθλόν, and κακῷ is set between them to bring the contrasted κακῷ ἐσθλόν together (Monro on 15.488): "good too beside the evil".
- **15.495–497** — the dawn of the 38th day (Monro on 15.495); ἐπὶ χέρσου means they had reached land, not that they landed; Murray prints προέρυσσαν (the manuscripts), Monro Aristarchus' προέρεσσαν, "rowed", as *Iliad* 1.435 (Monro on these lines). The English: "brought her forward".
- **15.505** — Telemachus does not come back to the city till the next day (Monro on 15.505).
- **15.524** — Monro would read τελευτήσῃ for τελευτήσει (Monro on 15.524).
- **15.555** — Murray prints Aristarchus' προβιβάντα; the manuscripts have προβιβῶντα. The choice between the participles βιβάς and βιβῶν is left unsettled by La Roche, and Ludwich admits both forms (Monro on 15.555 and his critical note).
- **15.29, 15.52, 15.116, 15.240** — the text ends 29 with a period where the sentence goes on (4.671 and Monro have a comma), prints Ἀτρείδης without the diaeresis at 52 (only here and at 19.183, 24.35 in the digitized Odyssey; 121 has Ἀτρεΐδης), line-initial ἐστὶν at 116 (4.616 and Monro: ἔστιν), and no stop at the end of 240 (Monro has one): slips, left as printed.
- **15.339, 15.509** — ὅππη and πῆ without the iota subscript, only in a few places (ὅππη also 16.81, 21.342; πῆ also 20.43, 21.362), against ὅππῃ, πῇ elsewhere and in Monro: perhaps Murray's own spellings, left as printed.
- **15.545** — the text prints μίμνοι, but σύ needs the second person μίμνοις, which Monro prints: a slip, left as printed (the English has "if you were to stay"). εἰ γάρ κεν "why, if …", γάρ as a kind of interjection (Monro on 15.545).
